No, the laws were passed because white men wanted to have their own women and use coloured women too without any law interfering with their affairs or making them responsible for the consequences.

Kenneth usually ended these arguments with himself with a feeling of complete impotence, of travelling around like a squirrel in a circular cage. No matter where he started or how fast or how far he travelled, he always wound up at the same point and with the same sense of blind defeat. Oh, well, better men than he had tried to answer the same questions and failed. He’d stay to himself and attend to his own business and let such problems go hang. But in spite of himself he often found himself enmeshed in this endless maze of reasoning. Just as frequently he determined to put from himself again the perplexing and seemingly insoluble problems.

===Chinese===
那些成天把「社会平等」挂在嘴边的白人，难道真以为自己不停地咒骂它，就能糊弄住谁吗？美国有二十九个州立法禁止种族通婚。这些法律全都是白人立法者通过的。立这些法，是为了防止某个黑人掳走某个白人女人、逼着她违背意愿嫁给他吗？还是说，这些法律其实是这些白人男人下意识地承认——他们信不过自家的女人、也信不过自家的男人，怕他们忍不住和黑人结婚？任何傻瓜都知道，要是两个人本就不想和对方结婚，无论上帝的律法还是人间的法律，都不能叫他们成婚。不，立这些法，是因为白人男人既要占着自家的女人、又要占有色人女人，而又不让任何法律来插手他们的事、也不让法律要他们为后果负责。

这一类自我辩驳，肯尼思通常总是在一种彻底的无力感里收场——像一只松鼠在一只圆形的笼子里团团转。无论他从哪里起步、转得多快、跑得多远，他总归又转回原地，带着同样一种盲目的挫败感。唉，比他更强的人都试过回答同样的问题，也都失败了。他不如独善其身，管好自己的事，让这些问题见鬼去吧。可他还是身不由己地，常常又缠进这套没完没了的推理迷宫里去。也同样常常地，他下定决心，要再一次把这些棘手的、似乎无解的难题从心头推开。

===Original===
It was after one of these soliloquies on his way from church one bright Sunday in April that Kenneth reached home and found a call for him to come at once to a house down on Butler Street, in the heart of the Negro district in the bottoms. Telling his mother to keep dinner for him as he would be back shortly, he hurried down State Street. Turning suddenly into Harris Street, which crossed State, which in turn would lead him to the house he sought on Butler Street, he caught a fleeting glimpse of a white man who looked like George Parker, cashier of the Bank of Central City Parker, if it was he, turned hastily at Kenneth’s approach and went up a narrow alley which ran off Harris Street. Kenneth thought nothing of the incident other than a vague and quickly passing wonder at Parker’s presence in that part of town.

Kenneth hurried on, instinctively stepping over or around the numerous children whose complexions ranged in colour from a deep black to a yellow that was almost white, and mangy-looking dogs that seemed to infest the street. Approaching the house he sought, he found a group of excitedly talking Negroes gathered around the gate. The group separated to let him pass, and from it came one or two greetings to Kenneth in the form of “Hello, Doc.” He paid little attention to them, but proceeded up the path to the house.

===Chinese===
那是一个明媚的四月礼拜天，肯尼思从教堂回家路上又做完这样一番内心独白，刚到家就接到一个口信，叫立刻到巴特勒街上一户人家去出诊——那地方在洼地黑人区的腹地。他告诉母亲给他留着饭，说自己很快就回来，便顺着州街匆匆赶去。他猛地拐进与州街相交的哈里斯街，由哈里斯街再过去就能到他要去的那所巴特勒街上的房子。就在拐弯的一刹那，他瞥见一个白人男子，看着像是乔治·帕克——中央城银行的出纳员。那人如果是帕克的话，一见肯尼思过来便慌忙转身，钻进了从哈里斯街岔出去的一条窄巷子。肯尼思对这事并未多想，只是一闪而过地纳闷了一下帕克怎么会在城的这一带出现，随即便丢开了。

肯尼思一路疾走，本能地绕开或跨过满街那些孩子——他们的肤色从深深的黑色一直到近乎白色的浅黄，各色各样——还有那些仿佛在街上成窝的癞皮狗。快到他要找的那所房子时，他看见门口聚着一群激动地七嘴八舌的黑人。那群人让开一条道让他过去，人群里抛出一两声招呼：「你好啊，大夫。」他没怎么理会，径直沿着小径朝屋里走去。

===Original===
Entering, he was surprised to find it furnished more ornately and comfortably than usual in that section. He knew the place of old, remembering that his father had always warned him against going into this section. Here it was reported that strange things went on, that a raid by the police was not uncommon. He had upon one occasion seen the patrol wagon, better known as the “Black Maria,” drive away loaded with bottles of whisky and with a nondescript lot of coloured men and women. Most of the property in this section was owned by white people, which they held on to jealously. They charged and received rentals two or three times as high as in other sections of “Darktown.”

Kenneth found in the front room another excited and chattering lot of men and women. The men seemed rather furtive and were dressed in “peg-top” trousers with wide cuffs, and gaudily coloured shirts. The women were clad in red and pink kimonos and boudoir caps. With an inclusive “Hello, folks,” Kenneth followed a woman who seemed to be in charge of the house into the next room. In the centre of the darkened room there stood the bed, dishevelled, the sheets stained with blood. On them lay a man fully clothed, his eyes closed as though in great pain, and breathing heavily, with sharp gasps every few seconds. By the bed, bathing the man’s brow, stood a woman in a rumpled nightdress and kimono. Kenneth recognized the man as Bud Ware, sometimes a Pullman porter, who used his occupation, it was rumoured, to bring liquor from Atlanta, which his wife sold. It was his wife Nancy who bathed his brow and who moved away from the bed when Kenneth approached. She informed him that he had come home unexpectedly from his run, and had been shot. Kenneth said nothing but went immediately to work. He found Bud with two bullet holes in his abdomen and one through his right leg. It was evident that he had but a few hours, at most, to live. Kenneth did what he could to relieve Bud’s suffering. Turning to Nancy, he told her what he had discovered. She stared at Kenneth wide-eyed for a minute and then burst forth in an agony of weeping.

===Chinese===
一进屋，他倒有些意外——这屋子的陈设竟比这一带寻常人家的要华丽、舒适得多。他对这地方早有所闻，记得父亲一向警告他别到这一带来。据说这里头常有些不大对头的事，警察来突袭也并非稀罕。有一次他还亲眼看见那辆巡逻囚车——俗称「黑玛丽」的——装满了一瓶瓶的威士忌和一伙不三不四的有色人男女，开走了。这一带大多数房产都归白人所有，他们死死攥在手里不肯松。他们收的租金，要比「黑镇」（Darktown）[1]其他地段高出两三倍。

肯尼思在前屋又看见一群激动地叽叽喳喳的男女。男人们神色鬼祟，穿着下摆收窄、裤脚宽大的「陀螺裤」，花花绿绿的衬衫。女人们披着红色、粉色的和服式晨衣，头上戴着梳妆帽。肯尼思朝众人笼统地说了声「你们好」，便跟着一个像是这屋里管事的女人进了里屋。这间光线昏暗的屋子正中摆着一张床，床铺凌乱，床单上染着血迹。床上躺着一个男人，衣服穿得齐齐整整，双眼紧闭，像是痛得厉害，喘得沉重，每隔几秒便尖锐地倒抽一口气。床边站着一个女人，穿着皱巴巴的睡裙和晨衣，正在给那男人擦额头。肯尼思认出这人正是巴德·韦尔，他有时在普尔曼卧铺车厢当乘务员，据传他借职务之便从亚特兰大带酒回来，由他妻子兜售。给他擦额头的正是他妻子南希，肯尼思一走近，她便从床边让开了。她告诉肯尼思，巴德这一趟车跑完冷不丁地回了家，便中了枪。肯尼思没说什么，立刻上手救治。他查见巴德腹部有两个弹孔，右腿贯穿了一枪。看样子他至多还有几个钟头的活头。肯尼思尽力减轻巴德的痛苦。他转向南希，把自己发现的情况告诉了她。南希瞪大了眼盯着肯尼思看了一会儿，忽然痛哭失声。

===Original===
“Oh, Lawdy, why didn’t I do what Bud tol’ me to do? Bud tol’ me to let dat man alone! Why didn’t I do it? Why didn’t I do it?”

Her screams mounted higher and higher until they reached ear-piercing shrieks. A head or two were stuck interrogatively through the opened door at the sound of Nancy’s woe, and as quickly withdrawn. Kenneth administered an opiate to Bud to relieve his pain and sat by the bed to do what he could in the short while that life remained. The sordidness of the whole affair sickened him and he longed to get away where he could breathe freely.

Strengthened by the opiate, Bud’s eyes flickered and then opened for a fraction of a minute. He smiled faintly when he recognized Kenneth. He made several ineffectual attempts to speak, but each effort resulted only in a gasp of pain. Kenneth ordered him to lie still. Bud, however, kept trying to speak. Roused by Nancy’s shrieks, he finally managed to gasp out a few words, interrupted by spasms of pain that shook his whole body.

===Chinese===
「哦，老天爷啊，我当初怎么就没听巴德的话呢？巴德叫我把那个男人撇开！我怎么就不听呢？我怎么就不听呢？」

她的号哭一声高过一声，直到变成刺耳的尖叫。听见南希这惨状，有一两个脑袋从敞开的门口探进来探询究竟，又立刻缩了回去。肯尼思给巴德打了一针鸦片剂止痛，在床边坐下，在这所剩无几的残生里尽他所能。整件事那肮脏下作的气息叫他反胃，他巴不得离开这儿，到一个能畅快喘气的地方去。

鸦片剂起了作用，巴德精神振作了一些，眼皮颤了颤，睁开了一会儿。他认出肯尼思，淡淡地笑了一下。他几次想说话都没说成，每一次的努力到头来都只换来一声痛苦的倒抽气。肯尼思叫他躺着别动。可巴德还是一个劲儿地想开口。南希的尖叫激得他拼力挣扎，他总算断断续续地吐出几个字来，每说几句便被一阵阵痛楚打断，痛得他浑身发抖。

===Original===
“I knows I ain’t got long, Doc. Dat’s a’ right, Nancy, I ain’t blamin’ you none. I knows you couldn’t he’p it.”

He fell back on the pillow, coughing and writhing in pain.

“Lif’ me a li’l﻿—hiar﻿—on the pillar, Doc. Dat’s mo’ like﻿—it! Doc﻿—I ain’t been much ’count. I tol’ dat man Parker﻿—to stop foolin’ with my ’oman﻿—but﻿—he keep on﻿—comin’ here﻿—when I’m gone. He knew I wuz sellin’ liquor﻿—an’ he tol Nancy he wuz gwine﻿—hav’ his brudder﻿—She’f Parker put me on﻿—chain gang﻿—if she tell me he come here﻿—w’en I wuz gone.”

He had another paroxysm of coughing and lay for a minute as though already dead. Kenneth administered restoratives, meanwhile telling Nancy to keep quiet, which only made her weep the louder. After a few minutes Bud began speaking again.

===Chinese===
「我知道我没多久了，大夫。是真的，南希，我一点也不怪你。我知道这也是没法子的事。」

他倒回枕头上，一边咳嗽一边痛得扭动身子。

「把我往上扶一点——好——靠着枕头，大夫。这——舒服多了！大夫——我这辈子没多大出息。我跟那个叫帕克的说过——叫他别再缠我女人——可——他还是——一个劲儿地往这儿跑——趁我不在家的时候。他知道我卖私酒——他就跟南希说他要——叫他兄弟——帕克警长把我送到——苦役队里去——要是她告诉我说他趁我不在来过这儿的话——」

他又一阵剧咳，躺了一分钟，像已经死了一样。肯尼思给他用了一些提神的药剂，一边叫南希安静些，可这反倒让她哭得更凶了。过了几分钟，巴德又开了口。

===Original===
“I come home today﻿—an’ kotched him here. W’en I got mad an’ tol him﻿—to get out﻿—and stahted towards him﻿—he grabbed his gun an’﻿—shot me.” After a pause: “Doc, whyn’t dese white fo’ks﻿—leave our women alone?﻿—I ain’t nevah bothered none of their women.﻿—An’ now﻿—I’s done got﻿—killed jus’ ’cause﻿—I﻿—I﻿—”

He half raised himself on the pillow, looking at Nancy.

“Doan cry, Nancy gal﻿—doan cry﻿—”

He fell back dead. Kenneth, of no further assistance, left Nancy to her grief after promising to send the undertaker in to prepare Bud’s body for burial, and made his way out through the crowd, now greatly increased in numbers, gathered around the door. He wondered if anything would be done about the murder, at the same time knowing that nothing would. The South says it believes in purity. What was that phrase the Ku Kluxers used so much﻿—“preservation of the sanctity of the home, protection of the purity of womanhood”? Yes, that was it. Suppose the races of the two principals had been reversed﻿—that Bud Ware had been caught with George Parker’s wife. Why, the whole town would have turned out to burn Bud at the stake. Weren’t coloured women considered human﻿—wasn’t their virtue as dear to them as to white women? Nancy and Bud weren’t of much good to the community but if Bud wanted his wife kept inviolate, hadn’t he as much right to guard her person as George Parker to protect his wife and two daughters? Again he felt himself up against a blind wall in which there was no gate, and which was too high to climb. He had determined to stay out of reach of the long arms of the octopus they called the race problem﻿—but he felt himself slowly being drawn into its insidious embrace.

===Chinese===
「我今天回了家——正好把他堵在这儿。我发了火，叫他——滚出去——朝他走过去——他就抓起枪——把我给打了。」顿了顿，又说：「大夫，这些白人——干嘛就不能放过我们的女人呢？——我可从来没招惹过他们的女人。——可这一来——我就这么白白地——叫人给杀了——就因为我——我——」

他半撑起身子靠在枕头上，望着南希。

「别哭了，南希丫头——别哭——」

他倒了下去，死了。肯尼思再帮不上什么忙，答应去叫殡仪馆的人来给巴德收殓入葬，便把南希留给她的悲伤，自己挤出那群人——此时门口围观的人已经多了许多。他心里嘀咕这桩谋杀会不会有人管，同时又心知肚明：什么也不会管。南方口口声声说自己崇尚纯洁。三K党徒成天挂在嘴上的那句话是怎么说来着——「捍卫家庭的圣洁，保护女性的贞操」？对，就是这句。要是这案子里两个当事人的肤色调个个儿——要是被堵在一起的是巴德·韦尔和乔治·帕克的妻子，那又当如何？哎呀，全城的人准会倾巢出动，把巴德绑在火刑柱上烧死。有色人女人难道就不算人——她们的贞洁难道就不像白人女人的贞洁一样，对她们是同等珍贵的东西？南希和巴德对这社会算不上什么有用的人，可要是巴德想保全自己妻子的贞洁，他守护自己妻子的身子，难道不拥有和乔治·帕克守护妻子和两个女儿一样的权利吗？他又一次觉得自己撞上了一堵盲墙——没有门，又太高翻不过去。他原打定主意，要远远躲开那只名叫「种族问题」的章鱼那长长的触手——可他感到自己正被一点一点地、阴险地，拽进它的怀抱里去。

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **Darktown（「黑镇」）**：白人对黑人聚居区的蔑称。本书作者怀特是 NAACP 领袖、黑人平权活动家，本章叙述中沿用此历史语境用语以揭露南方种族压迫与居住隔离的现实，译文予以保留并加注说明。参见术语表「黑镇」条。

## VII / 七

===Original===
Central City was the county seat of Smith County. The morning after the murder of Bud Ware, Kenneth went down to the County Court House to file his report on the death. It was a two-story building, originally of red brick but now of a faded brownish red through the rains and sun of many years. It sat back from the street about fifty feet and was surrounded by a yard covered here and there with bits of grass but for the most part clear of all vegetation, its red soil trampled by many feet on “co’t day.” The steps were worn thin through much wear of heavy boots. On either side of the small landing at the top, there hung a bulletin board on which were pasted or tacked yellow notices of sheriff’s sales, rewards for the arrest of criminals, and other court documents. The floor of the dark and narrow hallway was stained a reddish colour by the mud and dust from the feet of those who had entered the building. Just inside the doorway, on either side, were rectangular boxes filled with sawdust for the convenience of those of a tobacco-chewing disposition, which included most of the male population. The condition of the floor around the boxes seemed to indicate that only a few of these had realized for what purpose the boxes had been placed there. Over all was a liberal coating of the dust that had blown in the door and windows.

===Chinese===
中央城是史密斯县的县城。巴德·韦尔被杀的第二天早上，肯尼思下楼到县法院去递交那份死亡报告。法院是一栋两层楼房，原本是红砖砌的，经过多年风吹日晒，如今已褪成褐红色。它从街面退后约莫五十英尺，四周是一片院子，这里那里生着零星的草，大半地方寸草不生，红色的泥土被「开庭日」上来来往往的脚踩得结结实实。台阶被沉重的靴子磨得又薄又光。顶端那块小平台两侧，各挂着一块公告板，上面糊着、钉着一张张发黄的告示——警长的拍卖、缉拿罪犯的悬赏，还有别的法庭文书。又暗又窄的门厅里，地板被进进出出的人脚带来的泥灰染成了泛红的颜色。门里两侧各放着一只长方形的木箱，里头装满锯末，是给好嚼烟草的人吐口水用的——而这里的男人几乎个个都好嚼烟草。木箱周围地板的情形看上去，似乎这群人里没几个明白这木箱放在那里究竟是干什么用的。到处都厚厚地落着一层灰，那是从门窗吹进来的。

===Original===
Entering the office of the County Health Commissioner, Kenneth found that dignitary in his shirtsleeves, feet comfortably placed on top of his desk.

“Good morning, Mr. Lane. I’ve come to make a report of a death.”

At the sound of Kenneth’s voice, County Commissioner of Health Henry Lane turned in his chair without moving his feet to see who it was that had entered. Long, lanky, a two days’ growth of red beard on his face, Mr. Lane removed the corncob pipe from his mouth with a rising and falling of a prominent Adam’s apple. Seeing that his visitor was only a Negro, he replaced his pipe in his mouth and, between several jerky puffs to get it going again, querulously replied:

“Can’t you see I’m busy? Why don’t you save up them repo’ts till you git a passel of them, and then bring ’em in? Got no time t’ be writin’ up niggers’ deaths, anyhow. Ev’ry time I turn ’round, some nigger’s gittin’ carved up or shot or somepin’.”

===Chinese===
肯尼思走进县卫生委员的办公室，只见那位大人物只穿着衬衫，两只脚舒舒服服地架在办公桌上。

「早上好，莱恩先生。我来报一桩死亡。」

听见肯尼思的声音，县卫生委员亨利·莱恩在椅子里转过身来——脚却没挪动——看看进来的是谁。他身形又长又瘦，脸上是两天没刮的红胡子，把玉米芯烟斗从嘴里取下时，突出的喉结上下滚动了一下。一看进来的不过是个黑人，他又把烟斗塞回嘴里，抽上几口断断续续的烟气好重新把它抽着，一边没好气地回话：

「你没瞅见我正忙着吗？你干嘛不把那些个报告攒着，攒上一大捆再一块儿送来？反正我哪有闲工夫去给黑鬼（nigger）[1]的死写报告。我每回一转身，准有哪个黑鬼叫人给砍了、给崩了，要不就是出了别的什么岔子。」

===Original===
“I understand it’s the law, Mr. Lane, that deaths of anybody, white or coloured, must be reported by the physician at once.”

“Drat the law. That’s fo’ white folks.”

He drew himself out of his chair with great reluctance and ambled over to the counter, drawing to him a pad and pencil as he turned towards Kenneth.

“What nigger’s dead now?” he inquired.

===Chinese===
「据我所知，莱恩先生，按规矩，无论白人还是有色人，谁死了，大夫都得立刻上报。」

「规矩见鬼去吧。规矩是给白人定的。」

他老大不情愿地从椅子里把自己拖起来，慢吞吞地踱到柜台前，一面拉过一本便笺簿和一支铅笔，转身朝向肯尼思。

「这回又是哪个黑鬼死了？」他问。

===Original===
“Bud Ware, who lived at 79 Butler Street,” replied Kenneth.

“How’d he die?” was the next question.

“Shot through the abdomen.”

“Know who shot him?”

“Yes. George Parker.”

“Th’ hell you say! And you come in here to repo’t it?”

Kenneth was somewhat startled at the ferocity of the Commissioner’s expression, which had replaced that of laziness and resentment at being disturbed. “I thought it my duty …” he began.

Lane spat disgustedly.

“Duty, Hell! You’re a Goddamned fool and one of these damned niggers that’s always causin’ trouble ’round here. I always said eddication spoiled a nigger and, by God, you prove it. Lemme tell you somepin’—you’d better remember s’long’s you stay ’round these parts. When you hear anything ’bout a white man havin’ trouble with a nigger, you’d better keep your mouth shet. They’s lots of niggers been lynched for less’n you said this mornin’. Ain’t you got sense enough t’ know you hadn’t any business comin’ in here t’ tell me ’bout Mr. Parker? Don’t you know his brother’s sheriff? If y’ aint, goin’ up No’th tuk away what li’l’ sense you might’ve had befo’ you went.”

===Chinese===
「巴德·韦尔，住巴特勒街 79 号，」肯尼思答道。

「他怎么死的？」紧跟着是下一个问题。

「腹部中枪。」

「知道是谁开的枪？」

「知道。乔治·帕克。」

「你他妈胡扯！你还跑来报告这事？」

那位委员方才还是一副懒散、嫌被打扰的神情，此刻已换成一脸凶相，肯尼思不禁有些吃惊。「我以为这是我的本分……」他开口道。

莱恩厌恶地啐了一口。

「本分，见鬼！你是个该死的大傻瓜，是这一带那种成天给这儿惹麻烦的混账黑鬼之一。我早说过，读书会把黑鬼宠坏，老天作证，你这就是明证。我告诉你件事——只要你还在这地界待着，最好给我记牢了：凡是听见白人跟黑鬼之间闹了什么事，你最好把嘴闭紧。有多少黑鬼，就因为比你今早说的还轻的事儿，就遭了私刑（lynching）。难道你连这点见识都没有——你压根儿就不该上这儿来跟我说帕克先生的事？你不知道他兄弟是警长？要是上北方那一趟把你走以前那丁点见识都给走没了的话。」

===Original===
Kenneth stood silent, a deep red flush suffusing his face, while the official continued his vituperative tirade. His fists, thrust deep into his pockets, were clenched until they hurt, but he did not feel the pain. He longed to take that long, yellow, unshaven neck in his hands and twist it until Lane’s eyes popped out and his face turned black. He knew it would be suicide if he did it. He realized now that he had done an unwise thing in telling Lane who had killed Bud Ware—he should have remembered and said that he did not know. If he was going to stay in the South, he would have to remember these things.

When Lane had paused for breath, Kenneth bade him good morning and left the room. As he went down the steps, he heard Lane shouting after him:

“You’d better not lemme hear o’you doin’ any talkin’ ’bout this. If y’ do, you’ll fin’ yo’self bein’ paid a visit one o’ these nights by the Kluxers!”

===Chinese===
肯尼思站着不作声，一脸涨得通红，任那官员一连串地骂下去。他两拳深深插在口袋里，攥得发疼，可他觉不出疼来。他恨不得双手掐住那根又长又黄、没刮过的脖子，一直拧到莱恩眼珠凸出、脸憋成黑色才罢。他心里清楚，真这么做无异于自寻死路。他这会儿才明白，自己把杀巴德·韦尔的凶手告诉莱恩，是做了一件蠢事——他本该记着，说自己不知道。既然他要在南方待下去，这些事他就得记牢。

等莱恩停下来喘气，肯尼思道了声早安，走出了屋。他下台阶时，听见莱恩在身后嚷：

「你最好别让我听见你到处瞎说这件事。你要是说了，迟早哪天夜里，会有人上门来拜访你——三K党徒（Kluxers）！」

===Original===
Hardly had Kenneth left the court house before Lane rushed as fast as his natural indolence would permit him into the office of Sheriff Robert Parker—known throughout the county as “She’f Bob.” Lane was so indignant he spluttered in trying to speak. The sheriff looked at him amusedly and counselled:

“Ca’m yo’self, Henry. What’s eatin’ you?”

“Bob, d’you know George shot and killed a nigger buck over in ‘Darktown’ yestiddy mornin’ named Ware?” Lane finally managed to get out.

“Yeh. What about it? George tol me about it las’ night,” was the sheriff’s easy reply.

“Well, that nigger doctor Harper who’s been up No’th studyin’ and come back here las’ fall, come into my office this mornin’ to repo’t it, and he had the gall t’ tell me George done it.”

“Th’ black bastard! What th’ hell’s he got to do with it?”

“Said it was his duty. You bet I tol him good an’ plenty where he got off at. Guess he won’t come in here repo’tin’ no more ‘accidents’ like George run into.”

===Chinese===
肯尼思前脚刚离开法院，莱恩后脚便以他那天生的懒散所能容许的最快速度，冲进了罗伯特·帕克警长的办公室——警长在全县都被人叫作「鲍勃警长（She’f Bob）」。莱恩气得说话都直喷唾沫。警长饶有兴味地瞧着他，劝道：

「消消气，亨利。谁招你惹你了？」

「鲍勃，你知不知道乔治昨天早上在『黑镇』（Darktown）[2]开枪打死了一个叫韦尔的年轻黑鬼（nigger buck）？」莱恩总算把话憋了出来。

「知道。怎么啦？乔治昨晚跟我说了，」警长轻描淡写地答道。

「是这样，那个上北方去读过书、去年秋天回来的黑鬼医生哈珀，今早进我办公室来报告这事，他居然胆大包天，跟我说是乔治干的。」

「这黑皮的狗杂种！这事他妈的关他屁事？」

「他说这是他的本分。你放心，我把他好生训了一顿，叫他认清自己的斤两。我估摸他下回再不敢来报告乔治碰上的这种『意外』了。」

===Original===
Sheriff Parker’s face had assumed the colour of an overripe tomato as he jumped to his feet and banged his right fist on the table with a resounding thwack.

“I’ll keep my eye on that nigger,” he promised. “His daddy was as good a nigger as ever I did see, but they ain’t no way o’tellin’ what these young bucks’ll do. Roy Ewing was saying only this mornin’ that Bob, that nigger doctor’s kid brother, was tellin’ him the other day that he’d have to stop them boys ’roun’ the sto’ from botherin’ with th’ nigger gals when they pass by. Humph! They ain’t no nigger gal that’s pure after she’s reached fo’teen years ol’. Yep, I’ll jus’ kep my eye on those boys, and the first chance I git, I’ll—!”

His eyes narrowed in malevolent fashion as he left his threat unuttered.

===Chinese===
帕克警长的脸涨得像一只熟透的番茄，他一跃而起，右拳砰地一声砸在桌上。

「我会盯着那黑鬼的，」他应承道。「他爹是我这辈子见过的最听话的黑鬼，可这些年轻气盛的家伙会干出什么，谁也说不准。就在今天早上，罗伊·尤因还说，那个黑鬼医生的小弟鲍勃，前两天居然跟他说，得管管店门口那帮小子，叫他们别在那些黑鬼丫头路过时纠缠人家。哼！黑鬼丫头过了十四岁，哪还有一个身子干净的。行，我就盯着这哥俩，一有机会，我就——！」

他眼睛恶毒地眯起来，把后半句威胁咽了下去。

===Original===
In the meantime, Kenneth had gone home. He hesitated to talk the matter over with Bob or tell him what had happened to Bud Ware or what had taken place at the court house that morning. Bob was so hotheaded and insults made him angry so easily, he was afraid of what might be the outcome if Bob knew what had occurred. He would breathe a deep sigh of relief when Bob left in the fall to go back to school. Up in Atlanta there wouldn’t be so many chances for Bob to run up against these white people and, besides, Bob’s studies would keep him busy, leaving little time to brood over the indignities he had suffered. Kenneth determined that when Bob had finished his course at Atlanta University, he would urge him to go to Columbia University or Harvard and study law, and then settle down in some Northern city. It wouldn’t do for Bob to come back as he had done to Central City. Sooner or later Bob’s fiery temper would give way.

===Chinese===
这时肯尼思已经回了家。他犹豫着要不要把这事跟鲍勃谈一谈，要不要告诉鲍勃巴德·韦尔出了什么事，以及今早法院里又发生了什么。鲍勃脾气太爆，受辱又太容易动怒，他不敢想鲍勃一旦知道了这一切，会闹出什么结果。等到秋天鲍勃回亚特兰大去上学，他才能长长地松一口气。在亚特兰大，鲍勃撞上这些白人的机会不会这么多，再说，鲍勃的功课会让他忙得没多少时间去反复咀嚼自己所受的那些屈辱。肯尼思打定主意，等鲍勃在亚特兰大大学读完书，他要劝他去哥伦比亚大学（Columbia University）或哈佛（Harvard）读法律，然后在某个北方城市安顿下来。鲍勃可不能再像他这样回中央城来了。迟早有一天，鲍勃那火爆性子会撑不住的。

===Original===
He wondered to whom he could turn to talk this thing out. He felt that if he didn’t have a chance soon to unburden his soul to somebody, he would go insane. He thought of his mother. No, that wouldn’t do. His mother had enough to worry about without taking his burdens on her shoulders.

Mamie? No, she wouldn’t do either. She had no business knowing about the sordidness of the affair of Bud Ware and Nancy and George Parker. All her life she had been sheltered and kept away, as much as is possible in a Southern town, from the viciousness and filth and brutality of the race relations of the town.

===Chinese===
他想不出能找谁把这桩事掰开来谈。他觉得，要是不尽快找个人把心里的话倾吐一番，他非疯了不可。他想到了母亲。不行，这不成的。母亲自己烦心的事已经够多，不能再让她把自己的担子也压到肩上。

玛米？不行，她也不成。巴德·韦尔、南希、乔治·帕克之间那桩肮脏事，她不该知道。她这辈子一直被呵护着，尽可能远远地隔开——在南方小城里，能隔开多少就隔开多少——不让她沾上这座城里那种族关系里的恶毒、污秽和暴戾。

===Original===
Mr. Wilson, the clergyman? He was ignorant and coarse, but he had lived in South Georgia all his life and he would know better what to do than anybody else. He determined to go and talk with Mr. Wilson that evening as soon as he was free. He had hardly made the decision when Mr. Wilson himself entered the reception room and called out to Kenneth as he sat in his office:

“Good mawnin’, Brudder Harper. It certainly has done my heart good to see you attendin’ chu’ch ev’ry Sunday with your folks. Mos’ of these young men and women, as soon’s they get some learning, thinks they’s too good to ’tend chu’ch. But, as I says to them all th’ time, th’ Lawd ain’t goin’ t’ bless none of them, even if they is educated, if they don’t keep close to Him.”

Kenneth rose and showed his visitor to a seat. He did so with an inward repugnance as the coarseness of the man repelled him. Mr. Wilson seemed always overheated even in the coldest weather, and his face shone with a greasiness that seemed to indicate that his body excreted oil instead of perspiration. Yet, perhaps this man could give him some ray of light, if there was any to be had.

===Chinese===
威尔逊牧师呢？他无知、粗鄙，可他在南佐治亚住了一辈子，该怎么做，他会比谁都清楚。他决定晚上一得空就去和威尔逊先生谈一谈。这主意刚定下，威尔逊先生本人倒走进了候诊室，朝坐在诊室里的肯尼思招呼起来：

「早安哪，哈珀兄弟。瞧你每个礼拜天都跟家里人一块儿上教堂，我这心里可真舒坦。这些年轻后生和姑娘家，一大半是一读了点书，就觉得自己了不得，不肯上教堂了。可我老跟他们说，主是不会赐福给这些人的，哪怕他们念了书，只要不挨近主，就得不到福气。」

肯尼思起身请来客坐下。他这么做时心里一阵反感，因为这人的粗鄙叫他抵触。威尔逊先生似乎永远在冒热气，哪怕最冷的天也是如此，他脸上泛着一层油光，仿佛他身上分泌出来的不是汗，是油。然而，也许这个人能给他一线光亮，要是这世上还有一线光亮可寻的话。

===Original===
He told Mr. Wilson of his experiences of the past two days. The preacher’s eyes widened with a mild surprise and the unctuous, benevolent mask which he wore most of his waking hours seemed to drop rapidly as he heard Kenneth through to the end without comment. At the same time he dropped his illiterate speech much to Kenneth’s surprise, when he finally spoke.

“Dr. Harper, I’ve been watching you since you came back here. I knew that you were trying to keep away from this trouble that’s always going on around here. That’s just why I came here today. Your case is a hard one, but it’s small to what a lot of these others are feeling. I have asked a number of the more sensible coloured men to meet at my house tonight. I think it would be a good thing to talk over these things and try to find a way to avoid any trouble.”

Kenneth looked at him in surprise, not at the idea of holding a meeting, but at the language the man was using.

===Chinese===
他把这两天的事原原本本告诉了威尔逊先生。牧师的眼睛微微睁大，带着些许惊讶，他醒着的大半时间里都戴着的那副油滑、慈祥的面具，似乎迅速褪了下去——他从头听到尾，没插一句嘴。与此同时，等他终于开口时，他竟把那口粗陋的乡音也一并丢下了，肯尼思大吃一惊。

「哈珀医生，你回这儿来之后，我一直留心观察你。我知道你一直想躲开这一带没完没了的乱子。我今儿个就是为这才来的。你的处境是难，可比起这一带许多人心里受着的，你这点儿还算小的。我邀了几位比较有见识的有色人，今晚到我家聚一聚。我看大家把这些事摆到桌面上议一议，想法子别再闹出乱子，这是件正经该做的事。」

肯尼思惊讶地望着他——惊讶的不是开会这个提议，而是这人此刻说话用的语言。

===Original===
“I hope you’ll pardon me for asking so personal a question, Reverend Wilson, but you don’t talk now as I’ve always heard you before. Why, your language now is that of an educated man, and before you—you—talked like a—like a—”

Mr. Wilson laughed easily.

“There’s a reason—in fact, there are two reasons why I talk like that. The first is because of my own folks. Outside of you and your folks, the Phillips family, and one or two more, all of my congregation is made up of folks with little or no education. They’ve all got good hard common sense, it’s true. They’d have to have that in order just to live in the South with things as they are. But they don’t want a preacher that’s too far above them they’ll feel that they can’t come to him and tell him their troubles if he’s too highfalutin. I try to get right down to my folks, feel as they feel, suffer when they suffer, laugh with them when they laugh, and talk with them in language they can understand.”

Mr. Wilson smiled, almost to himself, as memories of contacts with his lowly flock came to him.

===Chinese===
「希望您别见怪我问这么个私人的问题，威尔逊牧师，可您如今说话，跟以前我听您说话全然不一样了。这——您现在的谈吐，是一个有学问的人的谈吐，而从前您——您——说起话来像——像一个——」

威尔逊先生轻松地笑了笑。

「这是有缘故的——其实是有两个缘故，我才那么说话。头一个，是为了我自己的会众。除了你和你们一家、菲利普斯一家，还有一两户人家之外，我的会众全是些几乎没怎么读过书的乡亲。他们都有很扎实、很硬朗的常识，这是实情。要在南方如今的世道里活下去，他们非有这种常识不可。可他们不希望自己的牧师高出他们太多——牧师太高高在上，他们就会觉得自己够不着他，没法把自己心头的难处说给他听。我尽量把自己放到我那些乡亲中间去，他们怎么感受我就怎么感受，他们受苦时我跟着受苦，他们欢笑时我跟着欢笑，用他们听得懂的话跟他们说话。」

威尔逊先生几乎像是独自一人似的微笑起来，脑海里浮现出与他那卑微的会众相处的种种。

===Original===
“I remember when I first started preaching over at Valdosta. I was just out of school and was filled up with the ambition to raise my people out of their ignorance. I was determined I would free them from a religion that didn’t do anything for them but make them shout and holler on Sunday. I was going to give them some modern religion based on intelligence instead of just on feeling and emotion.”

He chuckled throatily in recollecting the spiritual and religious crusade on which he had based such exalted hopes.

“I preached to them and told them of Aristotle and Shakespeare and Socrates. One Sunday, after I’d preached what I thought was a mighty fine sermon, one old woman came up after the services and said to me: ‘Brer Wilson, dat’s a’ right tellin’ us ’bout Shakespeare and Homer and all dem other boys. But what we want is for you t’ tell us somethin’ ’bout Jesus!’ ”

===Chinese===
「我还记得我刚到瓦尔多斯塔（Valdosta）开始讲道那会儿。我刚从学校出来，满心是要把我的乡亲从愚昧里拔出来的抱负。我打定主意，要让他们摆脱掉那种除了教他们在礼拜天上大声喊叫、呼天抢地之外别无用处的宗教。我要给他们一种建立在理智之上、而不只是建立在感情和情绪之上的、新式的信仰。」

他低沉地笑了笑，回想起当年自己曾寄予厚望的那场精神与宗教的十字军。

「我跟他们讲道，给他们讲亚里士多德、莎士比亚、苏格拉底。有个礼拜天，我自以为讲了一篇极精彩的道，礼拜做完，一个老太太走到我跟前，对我说：『威尔逊兄弟啊，你给我们讲莎士比亚、讲荷马、讲那一帮子人，那是挺好的。可我们想要的是你给我们讲讲耶稣！』」

===Original===
Kenneth laughed with the preacher at the old woman’s insistence on his not straying from the religion to which they were used.

“I had to discard my high-flown theories and come down to my folks if I wanted to do any good at all.”

He continued:

“These same folks, however, don’t want you to come down too close. Like all people with little education, whether they’re black, white, or any other colour, they like to look up to their leaders. So I use a few big words now and then which have a grand and rolling sound, and they feel that I am even more wonderful because I do know how to use big words but don’t use them often.”

===Chinese===
肯尼思和牧师一起笑起来，笑那老太太执拗地不让他偏离他们所习惯的那种信仰。

「我要是真想做成点什么事，就只能丢开我那些高调的理论，回到我那些乡亲中间去。」

他接着说：

「可这些乡亲，又不希望你和他们贴得太近。凡是读书不多的人，不论黑人、白人、还是别的什么肤色，都一样——他们喜欢仰望自己的领头人。所以我有意时不时甩出几个大字眼，听着响亮气派，他们就愈发觉得我了不起——因为我知道怎么用这些大字眼，却又不常用。」

===Original===
He paused while Kenneth looked at this man and saw him in a new light. He had known that Mr. Wilson, many years before coming to Central City, had attended a theological seminary in Atlanta, and he had wondered how a man could attend a school of theology of any standing and yet use such poor English. It had never occurred to him that it might be deliberate.

“And then there’s another reason,” continued Reverend Wilson. “The white folks here are mighty suspicious of any Negro who has too much learning, according to their standards. They figure he’ll be stirring up the Negroes to fighting back when any trouble arises. I had to make a decision many years ago. I decided that somebody had to help these poor coloured folks bear their burdens, and to comfort and cheer them. I knew that if I came out and said the things I thought and felt, I would either be taken out of my house some night and lynched, or else I’d be run out of town. So I decided that I’d smile and bear it and be what the white folks think they want—what the coloured folks call a ‘white man’s nigger.’ It’s been mighty hard, but the Lord has given me the strength somehow or other to stand it this far.”

===Chinese===
他停了停，肯尼思望着这个人，对他有了新的认识。他知道威尔逊先生许多年前——在来中央城之前——曾在亚特兰大的一家神学院读过书，他一直纳闷，一个人怎么能在一家像样的神学院读过书，却说起那么蹩脚的英文来。他从未想过，这也许是故意的。

「还有另一个缘故，」威尔逊牧师接着说。「这儿的白人，对任何一个按他们的标准读书太多的黑人，都满腹狐疑。他们觉得，一有风吹草动，这种人就会去煽动黑人群起反抗。许多年前我不得不做一个抉择。我认定，总得有人帮这些可怜的有色人扛一扛他们的担子，安慰他们、给他们鼓劲。我知道，要是我把心里想的、嘴里该说的都亮出来，要么哪天夜里我会被人从家里拖出去遭了私刑，要么就是被赶出城去。所以我就决定，笑着忍下这一切，去做白人以为他们想要的那种人——也就是有色人所说的『白人的黑鬼（white man’s nigger）』[3]。这一路走来不容易，可主到底给了我力量，让我撑到了今天。」

===Original===
With his deliberately imperfect English, there had gone from the preacher’s face the subservient smile. Kenneth felt his heart warming to this man. He found his feeling of distaste and repulsion dissipating, now that the shell had been removed and he saw beneath the surface. The simile of the protective device of the chameleon came to his mind. Yes, the Negro in the South had many things in common with the chameleon—he had to be able to change his colour figuratively to suit the environment of the South in order to be allowed to stay alive. His own trouble with the Parkers and Lane seemed much more trivial now than before. He looked at Mr. Wilson and asked:

“What’s the purpose of this meeting tonight? How can I help, Reverend Wilson?”

“It’s like this. A good part of my congregation is made up of folks who live out in the country. They’ve had a lot of trouble for years getting honest settlements from the landlords on whose land they work. Within the last five years, two of my members have been lynched when they wouldn’t stand for being cheated any longer. The folks out there are in a pretty bad way, and they want us to advise with them as to the best way to act. I haven’t time to go into the details now, but it’ll all be taken up tonight. Can I count on your being there? We need a man like you, with your education.”

Kenneth deliberated several minutes before giving his answer. What Mr. Wilson wanted him to do was just exactly what he had determined not to do. But what harm could come from attending the meeting? If he didn’t want to take any part in the plans, he didn’t have to. Anyhow, it seemed that the more a man tried to keep away from the race question, the more deeply involved he became in it. Might as well do what little he could to help, if he didn’t have to take too prominent a part. He’d go anyway. He told Reverend Wilson they could look for him that night.

===Chinese===
随着他这番不再遮掩的、故意蹩脚的英语，牧师脸上那副唯唯诺诺的笑也一同消失了。肯尼思觉得自己的心向这个人暖了过去。如今这层外壳卸下了，他看穿了底下的真相，方才那一阵反感和抵触随之散去。变色龙那种自我保护的本事，作为一个比喻，浮上了他的心头。是啊，南方的黑人，与变色龙有许多相通之处——他必须能——比喻意义上地——变换自己的肤色，去迁就南方的环境，才能被允许活下去。他自己与帕克家、与莱恩那一摊子事，此刻看来比先前小得多了。他望着威尔逊先生，问道：

「今晚这个聚会有什么打算？我能帮上什么忙，威尔逊牧师？」

「是这样。我那些会众里头，一大半是住在乡下的乡亲。多年来，他们想从自己为其种地的东家那儿得到一个公道的结算，一直碰钉子。这五年里头，我手底下有两个会众，就因为不肯再忍气吞声地任人坑骗，遭了私刑。乡下的乡亲如今日子很不好过，他们想请咱们给他们出出主意，看怎样行事最妥当。这会儿我没工夫细说，今晚都会一并议一议。我能指望你到场吗？我们需要你这样的人，有你这样的学问。」

肯尼思斟酌了好几分钟才作答。威尔逊先生要他做的，恰恰是他原本打定主意不去做的事。可去参加这个聚会，又能出什么岔子呢？要是不想掺和到那些计划里，他不掺和就是了。再说，一个人越是想躲开种族问题，似乎就越深地陷进去。既然不必抛头露面，那就不妨尽一点绵薄之力。他横竖是要去的。他告诉威尔逊牧师，当晚他们会看到他。

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **nigger（黑鬼）**：白人种族主义贬语。本书作者怀特是 NAACP 领袖、黑人平权活动家，有意在叙事中保留此类词语以揭露南方种族压迫的现实，译文不软化其贬义冲击力。本章卫生委员亨利·莱恩、帕克警长等白人角色口中反复出现，保留其轻蔑与威胁的语气；参见术语表「种族用语」条及第1章译注。
>
> [2] **Darktown（「黑镇」）**：白人对黑人聚居区的蔑称。本章莱恩口中再次出现，承接第6章首现之译注（揭露居住隔离与租金盘剥）；参见术语表同名条。
>
> [3] **white man's nigger（「白人的黑鬼」）**：有色人社区内部的讥讽用语，指那些为求自保而顺从白人、对白人唯唯诺诺的黑人；威尔逊牧师以此自况其多年来的生存策略——以佯装粗鄙、唯唯诺诺为伪装，掩护其在南方种族压迫下暗中帮扶族人、替人扛担的真实用心。译注呼应术语表「nigger」条，保留「黑鬼」之贬义冲击力以存原文批判锋芒。

## VIII / 八

===Original===
Kenneth was late in reaching the meeting-place that night. When he arrived he found all there waiting for him. Besides himself and Mr. Wilson were the Reverend Richard Young, pastor of Bethel African Methodist Episcopal Church, and Herbert Phillips, Jane’s father. There were also three men from the farming district whom Kenneth did not know, but who were introduced as Tom Tracy, Hiram Tucker, and James Swann.

Mr. Wilson opened the meeting after the introductions had been completed.

“Brothers, we’ve met here this evenin’ to talk over some way we can he’p these brothers who live out in the country and who ain’t been able to get an honest settlement from the folks they’s been farmin’ for. I’m going to ask Brother Tucker to tell us just how things are with the folks out his way. Brother Tucker.”

===Chinese===
那天晚上肯尼思迟到了，等他赶到开会的地方，大家都已在等他了。除了他和威尔逊先生之外，还有伯特利非洲卫理公会（AME）教堂的牧师理查德·杨牧师，以及简的父亲赫伯特·菲利普斯。另外还有三个从乡下来的汉子，肯尼思并不认识，经介绍才知道是汤姆·特雷西、海勒姆·塔克和詹姆斯·斯旺。

介绍完毕，威尔逊先生宣布开会。

「弟兄们，咱们今儿晚上聚到一块儿，是想商量个法子，帮帮这些住在乡下的弟兄们——他们给东家种地，到头来却从东家那儿拿不到一个公道的结算。我想请塔克弟兄给我们讲讲，他那一片的乡亲们到底过的是什么日子。塔克弟兄。」

===Original===
“Brother” Tucker rose and stood by the table around which they were seated and on which flickered an oil lamp. He was a man between fifty and sixty years of age, of medium height and thickset. His black skin was wrinkled with age and toil. His hands, as they rested on the table in front of him, were gnarled and hardened through a lifetime of ploughing and hoeing and the other hard work of farm life. It was Mr. Tucker’s face, however, which attracted interest. Out of the rolls of skin there shone two kindly, docile eyes. One gained the impression that these eyes had seen tragedies on top of tragedies, as indeed they had, and their owner had been taught by dire necessity to look upon them in a philosophic and pacifist manner. One remembered a biblical description: “He was a man of sorrows and acquainted with grief.” Kenneth, as he looked at him, felt that Socrates and Aristotle and Jesus Christ must have had eyes like Brother Tucker’s. His impression was heightened by Mr. Tucker’s hair. Of a snowy whiteness, his head bald on top, his hair formed a circle around his head that reminded Kenneth of the picture-cards used at Sunday school when he was a boy, where the saints had crowns of light hovering over their heads. The only difference was that Mr. Tucker’s halo seemed to be a bit more firmly and closely attached than those of the saints, which he remembered always seemed to be poised perilously in midair. He had often wondered, as he gazed intently at the pictures, what would have happened had a strong gust of wind come suddenly upon the saints, and blown their haloes away.

===Chinese===
「塔克弟兄」站起身来，在他们围坐的桌旁站着，桌上那盏油灯忽明忽暗。他是个五六十岁年纪的人，中等身材，敦敦实实。他那一身黑皮肤，已被岁月和劳作刻满了皱纹。他那双手搭在面前桌上，节节疙瘩、又粗又硬，是犁了一辈子地、锄了一辈子草、吃了一辈子庄稼人的苦活才磨成那样的。然而真正叫人注目的，是塔克先生的那张脸。从层层叠叠的皱纹里，透出两只和善、温顺的眼睛。人会不由得觉得，这双眼睛见过一桩又一桩的惨事——事实上也确是如此——而它的主人，被严酷的世道逼着，学会了用一种近乎哲人的、平和忍让的态度去看待它们。人会想起《圣经》上那句话：「他是个多受痛苦、常经忧患的人。」肯尼思望着他，觉得苏格拉底、亚里士多德，还有耶稣基督，他们的眼睛一定也像塔克弟兄的眼睛一样。塔克先生的头发更加强了这种印象。他的头发雪白雪白的，头顶已经秃了，剩下的一圈头发围在脑袋四周，叫肯尼思想起他小时候在主日学里看过的那些画片——画上的圣徒们，头顶上都有一圈光晕悬浮着。唯一不同的是，塔克先生这一圈光晕，似乎比那些圣徒的还要牢靠、还紧贴着一些；他记得那些圣徒的光晕，总是悬在半空中，摇摇欲坠似的。他常常盯着那些画片出神地想：要是一阵大风忽然刮过来，把那些圣徒的光晕吹跑了，那可怎么办呢。

===Original===
Mr. Tucker began speaking slowly, in the manner of one of few words and as one unused to talking in public.

“Brudders, me ’n’ Brudder Tracy, and Brudder Swann ast Reverend Wilson here to let us come t’ town some time and talk over with you gent’men a li’l’ trouble we’s been havin’. Y’ see, all of us folks out dat way wuks on shares like dis. We makes a ’greement wif de landlord to wuk one year or mo’. He fu’nishes de lan’ and we puts de crap in de soil, wuks it, and den gathers it. We’s sposed to ’vide it share and share alike wif de landlord but it doan wuk out dat way. If us cullud folks ain’t got money enough to buy our seed and fert’lizer and food and the clo’es we needs du’in’ de year, we is allowed t’ take up dese things at de sto’. Den when we goes to settle up after de cott’n and cawn’s done laid by, de sto’ man who wuks in wif de landlord won’t giv’ us no bill for whut we done bought but jes’ gives us a li’l piece of paper wif de words on it: ‘Balance Due.’ ”

===Chinese===
塔克先生开口说话，说得很慢，像个平日少言寡语、又不习惯当众讲话的人。

「弟兄们，我跟特雷西弟兄，还有斯旺弟兄，请了这儿的威尔逊牧师，让我们挑个时候进城来，跟几位先生商量一桩我们一直遇上的麻烦事。你们看，我们那一带的人，都是这样租地分成的。我们跟东家订下契约，种上一年或者更久。地是他出的，庄稼是我们下的种、锄的草，到了时候再收回来。本该是我们和东家平分，一人一半，可事情从来不按这个规矩办。我们有色人要是手上钱不够，买不起这一年的种子、肥料、口粮和穿的用的，就准许我们到铺子里去赊账。等到棉花和玉米都收完了，我们去结算的时候，那个跟东家穿一条裤子的铺子掌柜，不给我们所赊东西的明细账，只给我们一张小纸条，上面写着几个字：『结欠若干。』」

===Original===
He paused to wipe the perspiration from his face caused by the unusual experience of speaking at such length. He continued:

“An’ dat ain’t all. W’en we starts to pickin’ our cotton, dey doan let us ca’y it to de gin and weigh it ourself. De lan’lord send his wagons down in de fiel’ and as fas’ as we picks it, dey loads it on de wagons and takes it away. Dey doan let us know how much it weighs or how much dey sells it for. Dey jus’ tells us it weighs any ’mount de lan’lord wants to tell us, and dey says dey sol’ it at any price dey set. W’en we comes to settle up for de year, dey ’ducts de balance due’ from what we’s got comin’ t’us from our share of de craps. I’s been wukin’ for nigh on to six years for Mr. Taylor out near Ashland and ev’y year I goes deeper in debt dan de year befo’. Las’ year I raised mo’ dan twenty-fo’ bales of cott’n dat weighed mo’ dan five hundred poun’s each. My boy Tom whut’s been t’ school figgered out dat at eighten cents a poun’—and dat’s de price de paper said cott’n sol’ at las’ year—I oughter got mo’ dan a thousan’ dollars for my share. An’ dat ain’t all neither. Dey was nearly twelve tons of cott’n seed dat was wuth ’bout two hundred and fo’ty dollars. An’ den dey was mo’ dan three hundred bush’ls of cawn at a dollar’n a ha’f a bush’l dat makes fo’ hundred and fifty dollars mo’. All dat t’gether makes nearly three thousan’ dollars an’ I oughter got ’bout fifteen hundred dollars fo’ my share.”

===Chinese===
他停下来擦了擦脸上的汗——一口气说这么长一番话，对他来说是少有的事，竟把他累出一头汗来。他接着说：

「这还不算完。我们一开始摘棉花，他们就不让我们自己把棉花送到轧花厂去过秤。东家把他的大车赶到地里，我们前脚刚摘下来，他们后脚就装上车拉走了。他们不让我们知道这棉花到底有多重，也不告诉我们卖了多少钱。他们只管随东家高兴，说有多重就有多重；说卖了多少价，就是多少价。等到年底结算，他们就把那张『结欠』从我们庄稼该得的那一份里扣出去。我在阿什兰附近给泰勒先生做工，眼看就要满六年了，年年都比上一年欠得更多。去年我收了二十四包还多的棉花，每包都重过五百磅。我那上过学的儿子汤姆算了一笔账，按一磅十八分钱算——报上说的去年棉花就是卖的这个价——我那一份本该到手一千多块。这还不算。还有将近十二吨棉花籽，值个两百四十来块。再有三百多蒲式耳玉米，一蒲式耳一块半，又是四百五十块。这些全加起来差不多三千块，我那一份本该到手一千五上下。」

===Original===
Tucker stopped again and shifted his feet while Tracy and Swann nodded agreement with his statements.

“Las’ year me ’n’ my wife said we wuz gwine t’ get along without spendin’ no mo’ money at de sto’ dan we had to, so’s we could get out of debt. We wukked ha’d and all our chillen we made wuk in de fiel’s too. My boy Tom kept account of ev’ything we bought at de sto’, and when de year ended he figgered it up an’ he foun’ we’d done spent jus’ even fo’ hundred dollars. But when we goes to make a settlement at de end of de year, Mr. Taylor said he sol our cott’n at eight cent a poun’ and didn’have but sev’n hundred and thutty-five dollars comin’ to us. An’ den he claim we tuk up ’leven hundred dollars wuth of stuff at de sto’ which he done paid for, so that leave me owin’ him three hundred ’n’ sixty-five dollars dat I got to wuk out next year.”

===Chinese===
塔克又停下话头，挪了挪脚，特雷西和斯旺都点头，赞同他说的这些。

「去年我和我媳妇商量定了，能不在铺子里花钱就尽量不花，好把债还清。我们拼命地干，把几个孩子也都赶到地里去干活。我那儿子汤姆把我们赊的每一样东西都记了账，到了年底一算，我们整整花了四百块。可到了年底我们去结账，泰勒先生说我们的棉花是一磅八分钱卖掉的，我们名下只剩七百三十五块。接着他又说我们在铺子里赊了一千一百块钱的东西，都由他替我们垫付了，这么一来，我倒欠他三百六十五块，得在来年做工还清。」

===Original===
His face took on a dejected look as though the load had become almost too heavy to bear. His voice took on at the same time a plaintive and discouraged tone.

“An’ when you adds on dat three hundred dollars dat Mr. Taylor says I owed him from las’ year, dat makes neah’ly sev’n hundred dollars I owes, and it doan look like I’s evah goin’t git out of debt. An’ I thought we wuz goin’ to be able t’ sen’ Tom and Sally and Mirandy t’ Tuskegee dis year off de ’leven hundred dollars I thought I wuz gwine t’ make.”

===Chinese===
他脸上现出灰颓的神气，仿佛那副担子已压得他几乎扛不动了。他的声音同时变得又哀怨又泄气。

「再加上泰勒先生说我去年还欠他那三百块，这么一来我差不多欠了七百块，看来我是再也还不清这笔债了。我原想着，今年能靠我本以为可以挣到手的那一千一百块，把汤姆、萨莉和米兰达送到塔斯基吉去念书的。」

===Original===
The discouraged air changed to one of greater courage and determination. His voice rose in his resentment and excitement.

“Now I’s tiahed of all dis cheatin’ an’ lyin’! Mr. Taylor mus’ take me for a fool if he thinks I’m gwine stan’ for dis way of doin’ things all de time. I stahted to tell him dat I knew he wuz cheatin’ me in Janua’y w’en he give me dat statemen’, but den I ’membered whut happen t’ Joe Todd two years ago w’en he tol’ dat ol’ man Stanton dat he wukked for, de same thing. W’en ol’ man stahted thit Joe, Joe hit him fust and run. Dey came one night and call Joe to his do’ and tuk him down in de swamp an’ de nex’ mawnin’ dey foun’ Joe full of bullets, hangin’ to a tree. De paper say Joe done spoke insultin’ to a white ’oman, but all de cullud folks, an’ de white too, know dat Joe ain’t nevah even seen no white ’oman dat day. Dey knew dat if dey say he ’sulted a white ’oman, de folks up Nawth won’t crit’cize dem for lynchin’ a nigger down here in Georgy. So I jus’ kep’ my mouth close’. Now we wants t’ know if dey ain’t somethin’ we c’n do t’ make dese white folks we wuks for stop cheatin’ an’ robbin’ us po’ cullud folks.”

===Chinese===
那股泄气的劲头又变了，变成了一种更有勇气、更有决心的神气。他的声音随着愤懑和激动高了起来。

「我受够了这些坑蒙拐骗了！泰勒先生要是以为我会一辈子忍着这种事，那他就是把我当傻子看。今年正月他给我那张结算单的时候，我本想当面告诉他我知道他在骗我，可我一下想起两年前乔·托德的事——乔跟他的东家那个老斯坦顿说了同样的话。那老头子要动手打乔，乔先下手打了他一拳就跑了。后来有一天夜里，他们来叫开乔家的门，把他押到沼泽地里去了，第二天一早，人们发现乔浑身是子弹，吊在一棵树上。报上说乔对个白人女人说了无礼的话，可有色的也好，白人也好，大家都知道，乔那天根本连个白人女人的面都没见着。他们知道，只要说是他侮辱了白人女人，北方那边的人就不会怪他们在佐治亚这儿把一个黑鬼（nigger）[1]给私刑处死了。所以我把嘴闭得紧紧的。现在我们想知道，到底有没有什么法子，能叫我们给做工的这些白人，别再坑蒙拐骗、劫掠我们这些穷苦的有色人。」

===Original===
He sat down, evidently greatly relieved at finishing a task so arduous. Kenneth had listened in amazement to the story of exploitation, crudely told, yet with a simplicity that was convincing and eloquent. Having lived in the South all his life, he naturally was not unaware of the abuses under the “share-cropping” or “tenant-farming” system in the South, but it had never been brought home to him so forcefully how close at hand and how oppressive and dishonest the system really was. No wonder the South lynched, disfranchised, Jim-Crowed the Negro, he reflected. If the Negro had a yote and a voice in the local government of affairs, most of these bankers and merchants and landowners would have to go to work for the first time in their lives instead of waxing fat on the toil of humble Negroes like Hiram Tucker. He turned to Tucker to get further information on the system.

“Mr. Tucker, have you and the other folks like you ever thought of trying to get loans from the Federal Government through the banks they have established to aid farmers in buying land and raising their crops?”

===Chinese===
他坐下了，显然是卸下了一桩这么吃力的差事，大大地松了一口气。肯尼思听得目瞪口呆地听完了这一番被压榨的故事——讲得粗糙，却因其质朴而格外有说服力、格外动人。他一辈子住在南方，自然不是不知道南方这种「分成制」或者「佃农制」下的种种弊端，但这制度离他竟如此之近、如此苛刻、如此奸诈，却从未这样有力地直击过他的心。难怪南方要对黑人施以私刑、剥夺黑人的选举权、把黑人关进吉姆·克劳（Jim Crow）的隔离车厢——他心里想。要是黑人在地方的政务上有一张选票、有一副嗓子，这些银行家、商人、地主中的大多数，就得有生以来头一回自己下地干活了，再也靠不着像海勒姆·塔克这样卑微的黑人的血汗来养肥自己。他转向塔克，想再打听些这制度的情况。

「塔克先生，您和您周围这些乡亲们，有没有想过，通过政府设立的那些帮助农户买地、种庄稼的银行，去向联邦政府借钱呢？」

===Original===
“Oh, yes, Doc. Soon’s they started lendin’ money to farmers, I ’plied for a loan to buy me a li’l’ place dat I wuz gwine t’ wuk an’ pay for off whut I raised. But dey tol’ me dey didn’ have no funds t’ lento niggers an’ dat dey already done loaned all dey had to de white farmers. W’en I ast dem to put my name down on de lis’ to get a loan when some mo’ money came in, dey tol me dat it wa’n’t no use ’cause dey already had so many white folks’ names down on de lis’ dat dey nevah would come to de cullud folks.”

“Did you think about writing to Washington and telling them that they were discriminating against Negro farmers?” questioned Kenneth.

“Yas, suh, we done dat too. But dey wrote us back dat de onliest way any loan could be made was th’u’ de local agents, so dat didn’t come to nuthin’.”

“But, good Lord, they can’t discriminate in that way against you without something being done about it!” was Kenneth’s indignant comment.

===Chinese===
「哦，想过的，大夫。他们一开始放款给农户，我就递了申请，想买一小块地，自己种庄稼，慢慢还钱。可他们跟我说，他们没有款子借给黑鬼（niggers），又说他们手头的款子早就全借给白人农户了。我叫他们把我的名字记在单子上，等再来钱的时候借给我，他们说那也没用，因为单子上白人的名字已经排了那么多，永远也轮不到有色人头上。」

「那您有没有想过去信给华盛顿，告诉他们这是对黑人农户的歧视？」肯尼思问。

「是，先生，这个我们也做了。可他们回信说，贷款只能通过本地的代理人来办，所以这事也就不了了之了。」

「可是，老天爷，他们总不能这样歧视你们，却没人管吧！」肯尼思愤愤地说。

===Original===
Tucker looked at him with a wan smile that was almost pitying at the ignorance of the younger man. His voice became paternal.

“Son, dat’s jes’ zactly like de man whut wuz in jail and his frien’ come by and ast him whut dey put him in jail for. When de man in jail tol’ him whut he wuz ’cused of, de man on de outside said: ’Dey can’t put you in jail for dat! De man dat was lookin’ out at him th’u’ de bars laughed and said: ‘But I’se in jail!’ An’ dat’s de way ’tis wif de cullud folks in de Souf. Dey’s lots of things dey can’t do to ’em but dese white folks does it jes’ de same. I reckon you got a lot of things t’ learn yet, Doc, spite of goin’ up Nawth t’ study.”

Kenneth felt properly rebuked by this humble man who, though illiterate, was far from being ignorant. He joined in, but not very heartily, at the general laughter at Tucker’s homely sally.

===Chinese===
塔克望着他，淡淡地笑了一下，那笑里几乎带着怜悯，怜悯这年轻人竟这般无知。他的声音变得像长辈一般。

「孩子，这事就像那个坐牢的人——他朋友路过，问他到底犯了什么事给关进去的。牢里那人告诉他自己被控的罪名，外头那朋友说：『他们总不能为这个把你关起来吧！』隔着铁栏杆朝外看的那个笑了笑，说：『可我确确实实是在牢里啊！』南方这些有色人的处境，就是这样。他们不能对我们做的事多得很，可这些白人照做不误。我看你还有好些事情要学呢，大夫，哪怕你上北方读过书。」

肯尼思觉得自己被这位谦卑的人好好地教训了一顿——此人虽不识字，却绝非无知之辈。塔克这一句质朴的俏皮话引得众人发笑，肯尼思也跟着笑了，只是笑得并不很由衷。

===Original===
Mr. Wilson, as acting chairman, ended the discussion by calling on Tom Tracy. Tracy was a much younger man than Tucker and was about Kenneth’s age. Tall, well built, intelligent looking, his dark brown face had worn a scowl of discontent and resentment while Tucker had been talking. He began talking in a clear voice that but poorly masked the bitterness he felt but which he tried to keep out of his voice. Older men like Mr. Tucker were always quick to rebuke any sign of “uppishness” in the younger generation.

“I graduated from Tuskegee three years ago. My old mother worked herself almost to death to keep me in school, and I came back here determined to earn enough money to let her rest the balance of her life. But she and my father had been living all their lives just like Mr. Tucker here, and they didn’t have anything to give me a start. So I went to work on shares, taking that thirty acres that joins on to Mr. Tucker’s farm on the South. I took this land that wasn’t thought to be any good, because it had been exhausted through overworking it year after year. I bought some new ploughs and fixed it up fine. I thought I could put the things I learned at Tuskegee into practice and in a couple of years pay off all I owed. But instead of doing that, I’m getting deeper in debt every year. I rent my place from Ed Stewart and he knows that I know he’s cheating and robbing and lying to me, but when I try to show him where he is wrong in his figures, all he does is to get mad and start to cussing me and telling me that if I don’t keep a civil tongue in my head, the Ku Klux Klan will be hearing about this ‘sassy young nigger Tracy’ and I’ll wish I had kept my mouth shut. I’m getting sick of the whole thing, too. If it wasn’t for the old folks, I expect I’d ’a’ started something long ago. They are all talking about me being a dangerous character out my way already. Say I’m too ‘uppity’ and I need to be taught a lesson to show me that ‘niggers must stay in their places.’ ”

===Chinese===
威尔逊先生身为临时主席，请汤姆·特雷西发言，结束了这段讨论。特雷西比塔克年轻得多，和肯尼思差不多年纪。他个子高，身板壮实，看上去挺精明，塔克说话的时候，他那张深棕色的脸一直绷着，满是愤懑和不满。他开口说话，声音清亮，却掩饰不住满心的苦涩——他尽量不让那股苦味透到嗓音里，却收效甚微。像塔克先生这样年纪大的人，一向最不能容忍小辈有半点「不知天高地厚」的样子。

「我三年前从塔斯基吉毕业。我那老母亲为了供我念书，差点把命搭进去。我回到这儿，下定决心要挣够钱，让她下半辈子能歇一歇。可她和父亲一辈子都像这儿的塔克先生一样过活，一点能给我起手的本钱都没有。于是我就去租地分成，租下了塔克先生农场南边挨着的那三十英亩。我挑了这块地，谁都觉得它没什么指望了，因为年年狠种，地力早耗尽了。我添了几副新犁，把地好好拾掇了一番。我满以为能把在塔斯基吉学到的东西用上，过两年就能把欠的账都还清。可非但没还清，我反倒一年比一年欠得多。我这块地是向埃德·斯图尔特租的，他心里清楚，我知道他一直在骗我、抢我、坑我，可等我指着账上不对的地方跟他理论，他只会发火，破口大骂我一顿，还说我要是嘴上不留点德，三K党（Ku Klux Klan）就会听说『特雷西这个小黑鬼（nigger）有多放肆』，到时候我巴不得自己没开过口。这一摊子事我也真是受够了。要不是惦记着家里老人，我恐怕早闹出点事来了。我这会儿在他们嘴里已经是个危险人物了，说我太『不知天高地厚』，得好好教训我一顿，让我知道『黑鬼就该待在黑鬼该待的地方』。」

===Original===
Tracy finished speaking in a tone that was almost a shout. It could be seen that he was very near the breaking-point from brooding over the wrongs he had suffered.

Mr. Phillips, who had said nothing, broke in with a question.

“Tom, why don’t you move away from Ed Stewart’s place if he doesn’t treat you right?”

Tracy replied bitterly:

“Yes, suppose I tried to leave, what would happen? The same day I left, Sheriff Parker would come and get me. They’d put me on trial for jumping my contract and fine me. Old Stewart would be in court to testify against me. He’d pay my fine and then I’d have to go back to Stewart’s place and work a year or two for nothing, paying off the fine. A fat chance I’ve got with the cards all stacked against me!”

===Chinese===
特雷西几乎是吼着说完这番话的。看得出来，他日日想着所受的种种冤屈，已到了快要爆发的边缘。

一直没开口的菲利普斯先生插进来问了一句。

「汤姆，既然埃德·斯图尔特待你不好，你干嘛不搬离他那儿呢？」

特雷西苦涩地答道：

「是啊，我要是走的话，会怎么样？我前脚走，帕克警长后脚就来抓我。他们会告我违约逃租，给我判一笔罚金。老斯图尔特会到庭上作证告我。他替我把罚款交了，然后我就得回斯图尔特的地里，白干一两年，把那笔罚款做回来。整副牌都让人家做足了手脚，我还能有个什么指望！」

===Original===
Mr. Young, of the Bethel African Methodist Episcopal Church, nodded assent to Tracy’s statement.

“Brother Tracy’s right. Look at what happened to Jeff Anderson down near Valdosta last spring. He ran away and got to Detroit where he had a good job working in an automobile plant. They swore out a warrant against him for stealing, brought him back, and the last I heard of him he was back down there working out a three-hundred-dollar fine. No, Brother Phillips, you’ve been reading the law that applies to white folks—not to us coloured people.”

James Swann’s story was along the same lines as the others. The seven men entered into a discussion of ways and means of taking some action which would alleviate conditions before the harvesting of the crop which was now in the ground. One suggestion after another was offered, only to be as quickly discarded because of local difficulties. Midnight came, with no decision reached. When it became apparent that nothing would be settled, Kenneth was chosen with Mr. Wilson and Mr. Phillips to work out some plan to be reported at the meeting to be held one week later.

===Chinese===
伯特利非洲卫理公会的杨先生点头赞同特雷西的话。

「特雷西弟兄说得对。你们看看去年春天瓦尔多斯塔附近杰夫·安德森的事。他逃了出去，跑到底特律，在一家汽车厂里找了一份好活。他们给他安了个偷东西的罪名开了拘票，把他抓回来，我最后听到的消息，是他在那儿做工抵那三百块罚款呢。不，菲利普斯弟兄，您读的那套法律，是给白人使的——不是给咱们有色人使的。」

詹姆斯·斯旺讲的也和别的人大同小异。七个人便讨论起种种办法，想赶在这季还长在地里的庄稼收割之前，做点什么，好让大家的日子好过些。一个主意接着一个主意提出来，可一提出就因为本地这层那层的难处马上被否掉了。到了半夜，什么也没议定。眼看着再议也议不出结果，大家就推举肯尼思和威尔逊先生、菲利普斯先生三人，商量出一个办法来，向一周后召开的下次会议汇报。

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **nigger（黑鬼）**：白人种族主义贬语。本章为黑人农户塔克转述白人加害者的话时所引——南方白人惯于以「侮辱白人女人」为名对黑人施以私刑，并以「私刑处死一个 nigger」自辩，料定北方不会追究。译文依项目种族用语策略保留其贬义冲击力，不软化，以存原文对种族压迫的批判锋芒。参见术语表「nigger / niggers」条。

## IX / 九

===Original===
There was being held another meeting the same night. Two miles from Central City, to the North, was a natural auditorium, an amphitheatre formed by three hills. In this place a meeting alfresco was in progress. Though the place was far enough from the road to be reasonably free from prying intruders, sentinels paced the narrow roads that led to the place of assemblage. Skeleton-like pine-trees formed an additional barrier to the lonely spot, making as they did a natural fringe atop the three hills.

There was no moon. Light was furnished by pine torches fastened in some instances to trees, in others borne aloft by members of the gathering. About three hundred men were ranged in a circle around a rudely carved cross stuck in the ground. Each man was garbed in a long white robe reaching to his feet. On the left breast of each hood was a cross with other strange figures. Over the head of each man was a cowl with holes for eyelets. It was a meeting of Central City Klan, Knights of the Ku Klux Klan, Realm of Georgia. The Exalted Cyclops, whose voice bore a remarkable likeness to that of Sheriff Parker, was initiating new members into the mysteries of the order. He held in his hand a sheet from which he was reading the oath which the “aliens” repeated after him with their right hands upraised. Whether through fright or excitement or because the night air was chilly, the voices of the embryo “knights” had a strange quaver in them. Around them, rank on rank, stood the Klansmen, who followed the ceremony closely.

“… will willingly conform—to all regulations, usages, and requirements—of the Knights of the Ku Klux Klan—which do now exist—or which may hereafter—be enacted—and will render—at all times—loyal respect, and steadfast support—to the Imperial Authority of same.…”

===Chinese===
同一夜，还有另一场集会正在举行。从中央城往北两英里，有一座天然的剧场，三面环山，形同一座露天圆形剧场。就在这个地方，一场露天集会正在进行。这地方离大路虽远，足以大致免遭爱打探者的闯入，可通往集会处的几条窄路上，仍有哨兵在来回踱步。骨架似的松树构成了这片僻静之地的又一道屏障，在三座山顶上连成一道天然的边饰。

这夜没有月亮。照明全靠松脂火把——有的绑在树上，有的由集会者高举着。约莫三百人围成一圈，中间地上插着一座粗雕的十字架。每个人都罩着一件垂到脚面的白色长袍。每顶兜帽的左胸上都绣着一个十字，配着些别的怪异图样。每个人头上都扣着一顶兜帽，只在眼睛处开了两个洞。这是中央城三K党（Central City Klan）[1]——三K党佐治亚州分部骑士团——的一次集会。那位崇高独眼巨人（Exalted Cyclops）——他的嗓音和帕克警长惊人地相像——正为新成员主持入会秘仪。他手里拿着一张纸，照着念那段誓词，那些「外人」举起右手，跟着他一句一句复诵。也不知是惊惧、是兴奋，还是夜风太凉，这些准「骑士」们的嗓音里都带着一种古怪的颤抖。在他们四周，一排又一排地站着三K党徒，紧跟着仪式的每一步。

「……甘愿遵从——三K党骑士团——之一切规章、惯例与要求——无论现存的——抑或日后——所订立的——并将随时随地——对三K党之帝国当局——效以忠敬——予以坚定的支持——……」

===Original===
The droning voices ended the monotonous recital. The flickering torches gave forth a weird light that was lost in the darkness cast by the trees. The pungent odour of burning resin and the thick stifling smoke were blown by vagrant breezes into the faces of the hooded figures, causing a constant accompaniment of coughs, sneezes, and curses to the mumbled words. A recent rainstorm had left the low-lying ground soggy and damp and mightily uncomfortable underfoot. The crowd shifted uneasily as their feet grew cold with the dampness. Moths, mosquitoes, and other flying insects, attracted by the flaring lights, swarmed, getting beneath the cowls and robes and adding to the discomfort of the wearers. Even the imperfect illumination showed the cheap material of which the disguises were made, exhibited the wrinkles and dirt around the hems, revealed every aspect of the ill-fitting garments. Once from a spluttering torch there fell a bit of blazing resin on the hand of the man holding the light. With a yell he dropped the torch, danced and howled with pain, a ludicrous figure, until the agony had subsided. The torch, flung hastily away, set fire to the underbrush into which it had been cast. An unlooked-for intermission in the ceremonies followed as a score of the figures, holding the skirts of their robes aloft like old maids frightened at the appearance of a mouse, stamped out the fire, circling and yelling like a band of whirling deryishes.

Stodgy, phlegmatic, stupid citizens by day, these by night went through the discomforts of so unprepared a meeting-place, and through the absurdities of the rites imposed upon them by clever rogues who extracted from them fees and donations for the privilege of being made to appear more silly than is usually apparent. Add to that gullibility a natural love of the mysterious and adventurous and an instinct towards brute action restrained only by fear of punishment, by a conjuring of bogies and other malevolent dangers, and one understands, at least in part, the presence of these three hundred “white, Gentile, Protestant” citizens of Central City at this meeting.

===Chinese===
嗡嗡的嗓音念完了那段单调的诵文。明灭的火把发出一种诡异的光，被树影投下的黑暗吞没了大半。燃烧松脂的刺鼻气味和那浓重呛人的烟，被乱窜的风吹进那些蒙面人脸上，于是那些含混的诵词便配上了此起彼伏的咳嗽、喷嚏和咒骂。不久前的一场暴雨把这片低地泡得又湿又软，踩在脚下极不舒服。人群随着脚底受潮发凉，不安地挪动着身子。飞蛾、蚊子和别的飞虫被火光吸引，一团团地扑来，钻进兜帽和袍子底下，更增穿戴者的难受。即便是这并不明亮的火光，也照得出这些化装用料之廉价——袍边的褶皱、污垢一览无遗，把这些不合身的衣物暴露得纤毫毕现。有一回，一把噼啪作响的火把溅出一小块燃烧的松脂，正落在举火把那人的手上。他大叫一声扔下火把，又跳又叫，痛得直蹦，那模样滑稽可笑，直到痛劲儿过去了才消停。那把被匆匆甩出去的火把，点燃了它落进去的灌木丛。仪式因此有了一段意想不到的中断——只见二十来个蒙面人，像老姑娘见了耗子一样惊惶地撩起袍子的下摆，绕着圈跺脚叫喊，活像一群旋转舞动的托钵僧，这才把火踩灭。

白天那些沉闷、麻木、愚钝的市民，到了夜里却来忍受这么一个毫无准备的集会场所的种种不适，忍受那些强加在他们头上的荒唐仪式——这些仪式是一些狡猾的无赖编出来，好从他们身上榨取会费和捐献，作为一种特权，让他们得以显得比平日还要愚蠢。除了这份轻信之外，再加上他们对神秘和冒险天生的爱好，再加上一种只有靠对惩罚的恐惧、靠招魂唤鬼之类的恶毒恐吓才能约束的蛮干本能——这么一想，你便多少能明白，这三百名中央城的「白人、新教徒、纯种（Gentile, Protestant）」[2]市民，为何会出现在这场集会上。

===Original===
The initiation ended, the Exalted Cyclops ordered the Kligrapp or secretary to read several communications from the Imperial Klan Palace at Atlanta. This he did, struggling manfully through the weird and absurd verbiage that would have made any of the men present howl with laughter had he heard his children using it in their play. Instead it was listened to attentively, seriously, and solemnly.

Then followed a recital of the work to be done by the local Klan. The Kligrapp consulted a sheet of paper in his hand.

“The eye that never sleeps has been seeking out those in our city who have acted in a manner displeasing to the Invisible Empire. There is in Central City a nigger wench named Nancy Ware who has been saying evil things against our brother, George Parker. In the name of our sacred order, and in the furtherance of our supreme duty of preservation of white supremacy, she is being watched and will be treated so as to end her dangerous utterances.”

At this statement a robed figure that, even under the disguise, seemed to resemble him who had been “defamed” by Nancy Ware’s tongue nodded approvingly. The Kligrapp continued after a pause:

===Chinese===
入会仪式结束，崇高独眼巨人命纪录官（Kligrapp，即书记）[3]宣读几封来自亚特兰大帝国党宫（Imperial Klan Palace）的通函。纪录官照办，费力地一行行念着那些古怪荒唐的字眼——这些字眼，在场的任何一个人，要是听见自己的孩子在玩耍时说出来，准会笑得直不起腰。可眼下这些话，却被大家一字不漏地、严肃而庄重地听着。

接下来是本地三K党待办事务的逐项宣读。纪录官看了看手里的一张纸。

「那只永不阖眼的眼睛，一直在我们城中搜寻那些行事有忤『无形帝国（Invisible Empire）』[4]之人。中央城有一名黑鬼娘们（nigger wench）[5]，名唤南希·韦尔，一直在散布恶毒的言辞，诋毁我们的兄弟乔治·帕克。奉我们神圣结社之名，并为我等捍卫白人至上（white supremacy）[6]之至高职责计，此人正受监视，并将予以处置，以绝其危言。」

听到这话，一个蒙面人点了点头，表示赞许——那人即便隔着这身化装，看上去也很像那个被南希·韦尔的舌头「诋毁」了的人。纪录官顿了顿，又往下念：

===Original===
“Word has also come to us from Brothers Ed Stewart and Taylor that there’s a young nigger named Tom Tracy out this way who’s going around among the niggers saying that they have got to stop white people from robbing them on their crops. Tracy hasn’t done anything but talk thus far, but we will keep our eye on him and stop him if he talks too much.” Cowled heads nodded approvingly.

“And then there’s a nigger doctor who came in my office I mean, he went into the office of Health Commissioner Lane—and had the gall to repo’t the death of a nigger bootlegger and say that a white man had killed him for fooling around with the nigger’s wife. This nigger’s daddy was one of the best niggers that ever lived here in this town, and this boy’s keeping away from the other trouble-making niggers, but we’ve got to watch all these niggers that’s been spoiled by goin’ to school.” He added, as an afterthought: “… up Nawth.”

===Chinese===
「又，埃德·斯图尔特兄弟和泰勒兄弟传来消息，说我们这一带有个名叫汤姆·特雷西的年轻黑鬼，专在黑鬼们中间串来串去，鼓动他们阻止白人在庄稼账上劫掠他们。特雷西迄今还只是说说而已，没干什么别的，但我们会盯紧他，他要是再多嘴，就制止他。」一顶顶兜帽点着头，表示赞许。

「还有那么一个黑鬼医生，跑进我办公室里——我是说，他跑进了卫生专员莱恩的办公室——竟然厚着脸皮报告一个黑鬼私酒贩子的死，还说是一个白人因为跟那黑鬼的老婆鬼混而把他杀了。这黑鬼的爹是这城里住过的最好的黑鬼之一，这小子也还算没跟其余那些爱闹事的黑鬼搅在一起，但这些读了书学坏的黑鬼，我们全都得盯着。」他像想起什么似的，又补了一句：「……是在北边（Nawth）[7]读的书。」

===Original===
And so he droned on. Negroes, two Jews, three men suspected of Catholic leanings—all were condemned by the self-appointed arbiters of morals and manners. One or two men were singled out as violating the code of morals by consorting with Negro women. There was not much to report on this score, as those who were violating this rule in Central City had rushed, on formation of a Klan there, to join the order, that they might gain immunity from attack and yet continue their extralegal activities without check or interference. With the conclusion of the Kligrapp’s report, the meeting dispersed, the members silently entered the woods and there disrobing, and scattering to their various homes. Some went towards “Factoryville,” some towards the country districts, others climbed into automobiles parked near the road and drove towards the residential section of Central City where lived the more affluent merchants and other upper-class whites of the town.

The place was soon deserted. The ceremony had been a strange mixture of the impressive and the absurd. There was underneath the ridiculously worded language, the amusing childlike observance of the empty ceremonies, the queer appearance of the robes all designed alike with little regard for fatness or thinness of the prospective wearers, a seriousness which betokened a belief in the urgent need of their organizing in such a manner. They had been duped so long by demagogues, deluded generation after generation into believing their sole hope of existence depended on oppression and suppression of the Negro, that the chains of the ignorance and suppression they sought to fasten on their Negro neighbours had subtly bound them in unbreakable fashion. They opposed every move for better educational facilities for their children, for improvement of their health or economic status or welfare in general, if such improvement meant better advantages for Negroes.

===Chinese===
他便这样嗡嗡地念下去。黑人、两个犹太人、三个被怀疑信奉天主教的人——全都被这些自封的道德礼俗仲裁者定了罪。还有一两个人被点了名，说他们违犯了道德准则，与黑人女子厮混。这一条上倒是没什么可报的，因为中央城违犯这一条的人，一听说本地成立了三K党，便赶紧入伙，好让自己免遭攻击，又能继续他们那些法外的勾当，不受掣肘、不受干扰。纪录官的报告念完，集会便散了。成员们默默地走进树林，在那里脱下袍子，四散回各自的家去。有的往「工厂镇」走，有的往乡间去，有的爬上停在大路边的汽车，朝中央城那片住着殷实商贾和城中其他上等白人的住宅区驶去。

那地方很快便空无一人。这场仪式是庄严与荒诞的一种奇异混合。在那措辞可笑的语言、那近乎儿戏的空洞礼仪的遵行、那全然不顾穿戴者高矮胖瘦一刀切的古怪袍服底下，藏着一种认真——这种认真表明他们当真相信，他们非得这样结社不可。他们被那些煽动家政客愚弄得太久了，一代又一代地被骗，相信他们唯一的活路就在于压迫和钳制黑人；以至于他们想要套在黑人邻居脖子上的那条无知与钳制的锁链，已经反过来以一种挣不开的方式，套到了他们自己身上。他们反对任何旨在为他们子女改善教育条件、改善他们的健康或经济状况或一般福利的举措——只要这种改善意味着黑人也能得到更好的好处。

===Original===
Creatures of the fear they sought to inspire in others, their lives are lived in constant dread of the things of evil and terror they preached. It is a system based on stark, abject fear—fear that he whom they termed inferior might, with opportunity, prove himself not inferior. This unenlightened viewpoint rules men throughout the South like those who formed the Central City Klan—dominates their every action or thought—keeps the whites back while the Negro—in spite of what he suffers—always keeps his face towards the sun of achievement.…

In spite of the secrecy surrounding the meeting, next morning all Central City talked of what had taken place on the previous evening. In such a town, where little diversion exists, the inhabitants seize with avidity upon every morsel of news that promises entertainment. Though they had taken fearful oaths of secrecy, it was asking too much of human frailty to expect three hundred men to refrain even from mysterious hints of their doings. With the love that simple minds have of the clandestine, the midnight secrecy, the elaborately arranged peregrinations to the place of meeting, the safeguards adopted by the leaders not so much to prevent interference as to impress their followers, the “inviolable oath,” the grips and passwords—all these added to the human desire to be considered important in the eyes of family and friends and neighbours. Thus many of the three hundred dropped hints to their wives of what had been said and done. Over back fences, at the stores on Lee Street, in the numerous places where women contrived to meet and gossip, the one topic discussed was the meeting of the night before. One told her bit of information to another, who in turn contributed her mite. Each in turn told a third and a fourth. With each telling, the ball of gossip grew, and each repetition bore artistic additions of fact or fancy designed to add to the drama of the story. By noon the compounded result assumed the proportions of a feat bordering on the heroic.

===Chinese===
他们是他们自己想在别人身上制造的那种恐惧的造物，他们的日子便是在不断的战栗中度过的——战栗于他们自己所宣扬的那些邪恶与恐怖。这是一种赤裸裸、卑怯的恐惧之上建立起来的体制——恐惧那个被他们称作低人一等的人，一旦有了机会，便会证明自己并非低人一等。正是这种蒙昧的观念，支配着南方各处像组成中央城三K党的那类人——主宰着他们的每一个行动、每一个念头——它把白人拽在原地动弹不得，而黑人——尽管吃尽苦头——却始终把脸朝着成就的那轮太阳……

尽管集会严守秘密，第二天早上整个中央城却都在谈论昨夜发生的事。在这样一个没什么消遣的小城里，但凡有一星半点可以博人一乐的消息，居民们便如饥似渴地抓住不放。这三百人虽都发过骇人的保密毒誓，可要指望他们连一个神秘兮兮的暗示都不露，未免太把人的弱点看得太高了。头脑简单的人天生偏爱这等隐秘勾当——子夜的秘密，煞费苦心安排的迂回赴会，头目们所设的那些与其说是防人干扰、不如说是要震慑部下的层层关卡，那「不可违背」的誓言，那些握手礼和口令——这一切，又都助长了人想在家人、朋友、邻居眼里显得要紧的那份人之常情。于是这三百人里便有许多人，向自己妻子漏了些口风，透露说了些什么、做了些什么。在后院的栅栏边，在李街上的一家家铺子里，在女人们变着法儿凑到一起嚼舌头的无数个场合，议论的话题只有一个，那就是昨夜的集会。这一个把她那点消息告诉另一个，另一个又添上自己那点；每一个人又转告了第三个、第四个。每传一遍，这桩闲话的雪球便滚大一分，每一次复述都添上一些出于事实或出于想象的「点睛之笔」，好叫故事更富戏剧性。到了晌午，层层叠叠累积起来的版本，已夸张到近乎一出英雄壮举了。

===Original===
At the noonday meal, known as dinner, the men found themselves viewed in a new and admiring light by their spouses and offspring. They basked in the temporary glamour and sought to add to the fame of their midnight prowling by elaborate hints of deeds of dark and magnificent proportions.

In turn, to the Negro section of Central City were borne the tales by cooks and laundresses and maids, servants, with acutely developed ears, in the houses of the whites. Everywhere in the Negro section, in homes, on street corners, over back fences, the news was discussed by the dusky inhabitants of the town. In the eyes of a few, fear could be discerned. Most of the Negroes, however, discussed the news as they would have talked about the coming of the circus to town. Some talked loudly and in braggart fashion of what they would do if the “Kluxers” bothered them. Others examined for the hundredth time well-oiled revolvers. Most generally the feeling was a hope the Klan would not bother any coloured person—but if it did—!…

===Chinese===
那顿被称作「正餐」的午饭时分，男人们发现自己被妻子儿女以一种崭新的、钦佩的目光打量着。他们沐浴在这转瞬即逝的光彩里，还想借着煞有介事的暗示，给他们那趟午夜的潜行更添几分「黑暗而辉煌」的壮举意味。

消息又辗转传到了中央城的黑人区——由那些在白人家里当厨子、洗衣妇、女佣的仆人们，带着练得灵敏无比的耳朵，带了出来。黑人区的每个角落，在家里，在街角，在后院的栅栏边，城里那些深肤色的居民都在议论这桩新闻。少数人的眼里看得出惧色。然而大多数黑人谈论这新闻，就像在谈论马戏团要进城来了一样。有的大声地、吹牛似的嚷嚷，说「三K党徒（Kluxers）」要是来找他们的麻烦，他们要怎样怎样。还有的把那支擦得油亮的左轮手枪拿起来，第一百次地端详检视。最普遍的心绪，是盼着三K党别去招惹任何一个有色人——可万一它当真招惹了——！……

===Original===
It was natural that the news should eventually reach Nancy Ware and Tom Tracy and, last of all, Kenneth. Mrs. Amos, bustling with importance, hastened as fast as her rheumatism would allow to tell Mrs. Harper what the Klansmen had said or, to be more accurate, what Dame Rumour said the Klansmen had said, about Kenneth and Bob. It was obvious the two men had taken on a new importance in her eyes in being singled out for the attention of the clandestine organization.

That night in Kenneth’s office the brothers talked over the news. Kenneth scoffed at what seemed to him a fantastic and improbable tale. He looked searchingly at his brother.

“Well Bob, what do you make of it?”

“Trouble for somebody,” said Bob positively. “And I have a sort of feeling that that somebody is us,” he added after a pause.

“I’m not so sure,” was Kenneth’s doubtful rejoinder. “Some of these Crackers are just mean enough to start something, but I’m pretty sure there are enough decent white people in Central City to check any trouble that might start.”

===Chinese===
这消息最终传到南希·韦尔、汤姆·特雷西耳中，最后也传到了肯尼思那里，这原是顺理成章的事。阿莫斯太太卖弄重要消息似的，拖着风湿病的腿脚能走多快就走多快，赶着去把三K党徒说的话——说得准一些，是传闻里三K党徒说的话——告诉哈珀太太，内容正是关于肯尼思和鲍勃的。很明显，这两兄弟既然被这个秘密组织点名留意，在阿莫斯太太眼里便格外有了新的分量。

那天夜里，肯尼思在诊所里和弟弟谈起了这桩新闻。在肯尼思看来，这故事离奇古怪、不大可信，他一笑置之。他审视地望着弟弟。

「怎么样，鲍勃，你怎么看？」

「有人要有麻烦了，」鲍勃肯定地说。「而且我有一种感觉，那有人就是我们，」他顿了顿，又加上一句。

「我倒说不准，」肯尼思半信半疑地应道。「这些穷白佬里头，有些是坏到了会生事的地步，可我敢说中央城有的是正派的白人，足以压下任何可能闹起来的乱子。」

===Original===
Bob said nothing, though his face showed plainly he did not share his brother’s confidence. Kenneth went on:

“Besides, they must have sense enough to know that a sheet and pillowcase won’t scare coloured folks today as they did fifty years ago. It wasn’t hard to scare Negroes then—they’d just come out of slavery, and believed in ghosts and spooks and all those other silly things. But today—”

“I think white people are right sometimes,” broke in Bob with conviction, “when they say education ruins a Negro. One of those times is when you talk like that.”

The irony in his voice was but thinly veiled. He continued:

“The Southern white man boasts he knows the Negro better than anybody else, but he knows less what the coloured man is really thinking than the man in the moon. I’ll bet anything you say, that seven out of every ten men in town believe that you and I and all the rest of us coloured folks are scared to death every time we hear the word ‘Ku Klux.’ They believe the sight of one of those fool robes’ll make us run and hide under a bed—”

===Chinese===
鲍勃没作声，但他脸上分明写着，他并不和哥哥一般有把握。肯尼思接着说：

「再说，他们也该有点脑子，知道如今一块床单、一个枕套，再不能像五十年前那样吓唬得住有色人了。那时候吓唬黑人倒是不难——他们刚从奴隶身份里出来，还信鬼信魂儿，信那些乱七八糟的玩意儿。可如今——」

「我看白人有时候说得对，」鲍勃带着确信打断他，「他们说读书毁了黑人。这话对你这种人开口的时候，倒是对得很。」

他话里的那点讥讽，几乎没怎么遮掩。他接着说：

「南方白人吹牛说自己最懂黑人，其实他连有色人心里到底在想些什么，都不比月亮上那个人懂得多。我跟你打什么赌都行，城里十个人里头有七个都相信：你、我，还有我们所有这些有色人，每回一听见『三K党』这三个字，就吓得要死。他们信只要瞧见那种蠢袍子一眼，我们就会撒腿就跑，钻到床底下去——」

===Original===
“Oh, I don’t go quite that far,” interrupted Kenneth. “I only said I thought some of the good white people.”

“You can name all your ‘good white folks’ on one hand,” replied Bob irritably. “A lot they could do if these poor white trash decide to raise hell. Why, they’d lynch Judge Stevenson or Roy Ewing or anybody else if they tried to stop ’em. Look what they did to Governor Slaton at Atlanta just because he commuted the sentence of that Jew, Leo Frank!” he added triumphantly. “A mob even went out to his house to lynch *him—the governor*!”

“But that was an extraordinary case,” replied Kenneth.

“Call it what you will, it just shows you how far they will go when they are all stirred up. And with this Ku Klux outfit to stir them up, there’s no telling what’ll happen.”

===Chinese===
「哦，那倒也不至于，」肯尼思打断他。「我只是说，我想总有些正派的白人……」

「你那些『好白人』，一只手都数得过来，」鲍勃没好气地答道。「要是这些穷白人垃圾铁了心要闹翻天，他们顶个什么用。呸，他们要是闹起来，就是把史蒂文森法官、罗伊·尤因或别的什么人判了私刑也办得到，只要谁想拦他们。你瞧瞧他们在亚特兰大对斯莱顿州长（Governor Slaton）[8]干的好事——就因为他给那个犹太人利奥·弗兰克（Leo Frank）[9]减了刑！」他得意洋洋地补上一句。「甚至有一伙暴民跑到他家，要把*他——堂堂州长*——都处私刑！」

「可那是个非同寻常的案子，」肯尼思答道。

「随你怎么叫它，这只不过说明他们一旦被煽起来，能闹到什么地步。如今再加上三K党这一套在煽风点火，谁知道会出什么事。」

===Original===
“Bob, do you really believe what you said just now about most of them really believing Negroes will be scared by the Klan? That seems so far-fetched.”

“Believe it? Of course I do. Just use your eyes and see how Negroes fool white folks all the time. Take, for instance, old Will Hutchinson who works for Mr. Baird. Will cuts all sorts of monkey-shines around Baird, laughs like an idiot, and wheedles old Baird out of anything he’s got. Baird gives it to him and then tells his friends about ‘his good nigger Will’ and boasts that Will is one ‘darky’ he really knows. Then Will goes home and laughs at the fool he’s made of Baird by acting like a fool.” Bob laughed at the memory of many occasions on which Will had bamboozled his employer. “And there are Negroes all over the South doing the same thing every day!” he ended.

“That’s true,” admitted Kenneth, “but what ought we to do about this meeting last night?”

“Do?” echoed Bob. A determined look came to his face, his teeth clenched, his eyes narrowed until they became thin slits. “Do?” he repeated. “If they ever bother me, I’m going to fight—and fight like hell!”

Long into the night Kenneth sat alone in his office, wondering how it was all going to turn out.

===Chinese===
「鲍勃，你方才说的话——说他们多半当真以为黑人都被三K党吓住了——你真信吗？那未免太离谱了。」

「信？我当然信。你只要睁开眼瞧瞧，看黑人是怎样天天把白人哄得团团转的。拿老威尔·哈钦森（Will Hutchinson）来说吧，他在贝尔德（Baird）先生手下做事。威尔在贝尔德跟前装出种种滑稽相，笑得像个傻子，把老贝尔德哄得要什么给什么。贝尔德把东西给了他，回头就跟朋友们夸耀『我那个好黑鬼（nigger）威尔』，吹嘘威尔是他真正『摸透了』的一个黑佬（darky）。然后威尔回到家，暗自嘲笑贝尔德那个被他装疯卖傻骗惨了的大傻瓜。」鲍勃想起威尔多少回把东家哄得晕头转向，不禁笑出声来。「南方各地到处都有黑人天天干着这样的事！」他总结道。

「这话不假，」肯尼思承认，「可昨晚那个会，我们该怎么办呢？」

「怎么办？」鲍勃应道。他脸上掠过一道决绝之色，牙关紧咬，两眼眯成了一条细缝。「怎么办？」他重复了一遍，「他们要是敢来招惹我，我就跟他们干——往死里干！」

直到深夜，肯尼思仍独自坐在诊所里，琢磨着这一切究竟会怎样收场。

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **Central City Klan / Knights of the Ku Klux Klan（中央城三K党 / 三K党佐治亚州分部骑士团）**：三K党（Ku Klux Klan）是美国白人至上主义的秘密恐怖组织，1860 年代由南方邦联旧人创立，20 世纪 20 年代（本书成书之际）借「百分之百美国主义」之名再度猖獗，以暴力恐吓、私刑、纵火等手段迫害黑人及其他少数族裔。本书作者怀特是 NAACP 领袖、黑人平权活动家，本章以讽刺笔触揭露其集会之荒唐与本质之恐怖。下文「崇高独眼巨人（Exalted Cyclops）」「纪录官（Kligrapp）」「帝国党宫（Imperial Klan Palace）」等均为三K党内部职衔与机构，译文照录原称以存其历史语境。
>
> [2] **Gentile, Protestant（非犹太裔、新教徒）**：三K党入会资格限定为「白人、信基督新教、非犹太裔（white, Gentile, Protestant）」，即所谓「百分之百美国人」，明确排斥黑人、犹太人、天主教徒及外来移民。译文保留原文以见其排他本质。
>
> [3] **Kligrapp（纪录官）**：三K党地方支部（Klavern）之书记，源自该组织自造的术语体系，与「崇高独眼巨人（Exalted Cyclops，即支部首领）」「大巨人（Great Titan，即州级首领）」「帝国巫师（Imperial Wizard，即全国首领）」等同属其刻意营造神秘感的内部称谓。
>
> [4] **Invisible Empire（无形帝国）**：三K党自称其组织为「无形帝国」，奉「帝国巫师」为首领，对应其种种帝国式虚衔。下文「永不阖眼的眼睛（the eye that never sleeps）」亦为其内部用语。
>
> [5] **nigger wench（黑鬼娘们）**：白人种族主义对黑人女子的极度贬称，nigger（黑鬼）之外再加以 wench（旧时指低贱女仆、淫妇）双重贬抑。本章此处出自纪录官宣读三K党文书之口，作者怀特意在揭露其语言与意图之恶毒，译文保留其贬义冲击力，不软化。
>
> [6] **white supremacy（白人至上）**：三K党的核心信条，主张白种人对其他种族的绝对统治与优越地位，本书时代为南方各州种族隔离与暴行的意识形态根基。
>
> [7] **Nawth（「北边」）**：南方白人对「North（北方）」的方言读法，纪录官以此补缀「在北边读的书」一语，带轻蔑口吻，指肯尼思等人在北方受教育。译文以「北边」对应并附原文。
>
> [8] **Governor Slaton（斯莱顿州长）**：约翰·M. 斯莱顿（John M. Slaton），时任佐治亚州州长。1915 年他将被判死刑的犹太工厂主利奥·弗兰克减刑为终身监禁，引发一场暴民骚动——暴民闯入州立监狱将弗兰克劫出私刑绞死，并扬言要惩办斯莱顿本人。本书以这段历史为「白人暴民不可制」之显例。
>
> [9] **Leo Frank（利奥·弗兰克）**：纽约出生的犹太裔工厂主管，1913 年在亚特兰大被诬告谋杀一名白人女工，在排犹与种族狂热的氛围下被判死刑；1915 年斯莱顿州长复审证据后予以减刑，旋即遭暴民劫狱私刑绞死，为二十世纪美国最著名的犹太人遭私刑冤案之一，亦直接催生了反犹组织「三K党」的死灰复燃与「反诽谤联盟（ADL）」的成立。

## X / 十

===Original===
The next day Kenneth received a letter from Jane Phillips. In it she announced that she would arrive in Central City on Monday morning.

Kenneth’s face took on a satisfied smile and deep down in his heart there was happiness and contentment. Jane had occupied an increasingly large portion of his thoughts ever since those wonderful ten days they had spent together last December. Kenneth’s life had been singularly free from feminine influence, other than that of his mother. It was not that he was averse to such influence, but his life had been so busy that he had had no time to spend in wandering through the Elysian fields of lovemaking. There had been one girl in New York. He had met her at a dance in Harlem. Together they had spent their Sundays and the evenings when he was free from his duties at the hospital in wandering through Central and Bronx Parks. Occasionally they had attended the theatre. One night their hands had touched as they sat in the semidarkness and watched the tender love scene on the stage. She had not withdrawn her hand. He sat there thrilled at the touch and had lived the character of the make-believe hero as he made ardent love on the stage. Naturally, the heroine was none other than the girl who sat beside him. Afterwards, they had ridden home atop a Fifth Avenue bus, and the whole city seemed filled with romance. He had imagined himself at the time deeply in love. But that tender episode had soon ended when he told her he was planning to return to Georgia. “Kenneth!” she had exclaimed. “How can you think of living down South again? It’s silly of you even to think of it! I could never think of living down there where they are likely to lynch you at a moment’s notice! It’s too barbaric, too horrible an existence to consider even for a minute!” Kenneth had tried to show her that it wasn’t as bad as it had been painted, that coloured people who minded their own business never had any trouble. But she had been obdurate. Kenneth left the house in a huff, and had never gone back again. What silly notions women have, he had thought to himself. The reason they talked about the South that way was because of sheer ignorance. As if he couldn’t manage his own affairs and keep away from trouble! Humph! Well rid of the silly creature, and he felt glad he had found out before going in too deep.

But now this was different. Jane had no such absurd notions as those girls up North had. She wasn’t the sort that couldn’t leave promenading down Seventh Avenue in New York or State Street in Chicago or U Street in Washington. It wasn’t that she didn’t know what it meant to live in the North. Hadn’t she been to Atlantic City and New York and Washington with her mother? No, Jane was just the sort of girl who would make the right sort of companion for him in a place like Central City. Intelligent, with a good education, talented musically—she would make an ideal wife. Kenneth found himself musing along in this fashion until aroused by his mother as she called him to supper.

===Chinese===
第二天，肯尼思收到简·菲利普斯的一封信。信里她说她将于周一早晨抵达中央城。

肯尼思脸上浮起一抹满意的笑，心底里满是欢喜和踏实。自去年十二月他们一起度过那美妙的十天以来，简在他心思里所占的位置便越来越大。肯尼思这一生，除了母亲之外，几乎没有受过什么女性的影响。这倒不是因为他厌恶这种影响，只是他一直太忙，根本没工夫去谈情说爱的极乐世界里徜徉。在纽约时有过一个姑娘。他是在哈莱姆的一次舞会上认识她的。每逢星期天，以及晚上从医院下班的时候，他们便一起逛中央公园和布朗克斯公园。偶尔也去看看戏。一天夜里，他们在半明半暗的座位上看台上那一幕温柔的爱情戏，两人的手无意间碰到了一起。她没有把手抽回去。他坐在那里，被那一点接触激得心头怦怦直跳，仿佛自己化作了台上那个假想的男主角，正在台上热烈地求爱。自然，那女主角不是别人，正是坐在他身边的那个姑娘。过后，他们坐第五大道的公共汽车顶层回家，整座城市似乎都洋溢着浪漫。他当时以为自己深爱着那个姑娘。可那段柔情的小插曲，在他告诉对方自己打算回佐治亚之后，很快便收场了。「肯尼思！」她叫道，「你怎么能想再回南方去住呢？你连想想都觉得荒唐！我绝想不到要去他们那种动不动就要把你私刑（lynching）弄死的地方住！那种生活太野蛮、太可怕了，想上一分钟都受不了！」肯尼思想让她明白，事情并不像别人说的那么糟，凡是守本分的有色人（coloured）都不会惹上麻烦。可她固执得很。肯尼思一气之下离开了她家，再也没有回去过。女人家的念头多可笑啊，他心里想。她们那样谈论南方，纯粹是因为无知。就好像他连自己的事都打理不好、躲不开麻烦似的！哼！甩掉这个傻丫头倒好，他庆幸自己在陷得太深之前看清了她。

可这一回不同了。简不像北方那些姑娘有这种荒唐念头。她不是那种离了纽约第七大道、芝加哥州街、华盛顿U街就活不下去的姑娘。这又不是她不知道在北方生活是什么滋味。她不是跟母亲一起去过大西洋城、纽约和华盛顿吗？不，简正是那种能在中央城这样的地方做他合适伴侣的姑娘。聪慧，受过良好教育，又有音乐天赋——她会是个理想的妻子。肯尼思发现自己就这样一路想入非非，直到母亲喊他吃晚饭，才把他唤回来。

===Original===
It was darned silly of him, he thought as he arose to comply, to go along thinking like this. He and Jane had spoken no word of love when she had been at home at Christmas. Nor had their letters been other than those of good friends. But hadn’t she written him almost every week since she left? She must think something of him to have done that. He determined that as soon as he could he would skilfully direct the conversation to the point where he could find out just where he stood. It was time that he was thinking about settling down, anyhow. He would be twenty-nine his next birthday—he was making money—if he acted wisely his future was assured. Yes, he would find out how Jane felt. Both his mother and Mamie liked Jane—and Mr. Phillips had called him “my boy” several times lately and had repeated to him snatches of the letters that Jane had written home. The only doubtful quantity was the attitude of Jane herself.

On Monday morning Kenneth reached the railroad station long before the train arrived. He tried to sit in the filthy little waiting-room with the sign over the door, “**For Coloured**,” but the air was so oppressive that he chose rather to walk up and down the road outside the station. At last the train came. He walked down towards the engine where the Jim Crow car was. It was half baggage car and half coach.
