A motley crowd of laughing, shouting Negroes descended, calling out to friends and relatives in the group of Negroes on the ground. Standing on tiptoe, Kenneth strained his eyes to get glimpse of Jane. The windows of the coach were too dirty to see inside. At last she appeared on the platform, dainty, neat, and looking as though she had just emerged from her own room, in spite of the filth and cindery foulness of the coach. Kenneth thought of the simile of a rose springing up from a bed of noisome and unlovely weeds as he hurried forward to help Jane with her bags through the crowd of coloured people that flocked around the steps.

Jane greeted him cordially enough, her eyes shining with pleasure at seeing him again. Kenneth, however, felt a vague disappointment. He had let his thoughts run riot while she had been away. So far as he was concerned, the only things necessary were the actual asking of the all-important question and the choosing of a wedding-day. As he followed her to his car, he turned over in his mind just what it was that disappointed him so in her greeting. He couldn’t put his finger on it exactly, but she would have greeted Bob or any other man just as warmly and he would not have felt jealous at all. Maybe she’s tired from the ride in that dirty and noisy car? She’ll be quite different when I go over to see her tonight, he thought.

===Chinese===
他站起来准备去吃饭，一边想，自己这样一路想下去，真是太傻了。圣诞节她回家那阵子，他和简之间连一句情话都没说过。他们通的信，也不过是好朋友之间那种。可她走后不是几乎每周都给他写信吗？她要不是心里有他，怎会这样做。他拿定主意，一有机会，就要巧妙地把话引到那个节骨眼上，好摸清自己的处境到底如何。不管怎么说，他也该想想成家的事了。下一个生日他就二十九了——他正在赚钱——只要他行事得当，前途是有保障的。对，他要弄清楚简的心意。母亲和玛米都喜欢简——菲利普斯先生最近也好几次叫他「我的孩子」，还把简写给家里的信一段段念给他听。唯一拿不准的，只是简本人的态度。

周一早晨，肯尼思老早就到了火车站，离火车进站还早着呢。他想在门上挂着「**有色人候车室**（For Coloured）」牌子那个肮脏的小候车室里坐坐，可屋里空气实在太闷，他索性在站外的路上来回踱步。火车总算来了。他朝机车那边走去，吉姆·克劳（Jim Crow）车厢就挂在那儿。那车厢半截是行李车，半截是客车。一群肤色不一的黑人有说有笑地涌下来，朝站台上那群黑人里的亲友大声招呼。肯尼思踮起脚，伸长脖子想瞧见简。车厢的窗户脏得根本看不见里面。她终于出现在站台上，娇小、整洁，看上去就像刚从自己房里走出来一样，全然不顾这车厢的肮脏和煤渣的污浊。肯尼思赶上前去帮简拎行李，穿过挤在车门口的一群有色人——他想起一个比方：一朵玫瑰从一床恶臭难看的杂草丛中亭亭升起。

简招呼他倒算热络，眼睛里闪着重逢的欢喜。然而肯尼思心里却有一丝说不清的失望。她不在的这些日子里，他由着自己的心思胡乱驰骋。在他看来，如今只消当面问出那句最要紧的话、再定下婚期，便万事齐备了。他跟在她身后走向自己的车，一路上心里反复琢磨，她的招呼到底哪一点让他如此失望。他也说不上来，可她这般热情地招呼鲍勃、或任何一个男人也全是一样，而他丝毫不会嫉妒。也许是坐了那样又脏又吵的车，累着了？今晚我去看她，她就会大不一样了，他想。

===Original===
He inquired regarding her trip—was it pleasant? “Ugh, it was horrible!” she replied, shuddering at the memory of it. “I had a Pullman as far as Atlanta, but there I had to change to that dirty old Jim Crow car. There was a crowd of Negroes who had three or four quarts of cheap liquor. They were horrible. Why, they even had the nerve to offer me a drink! And the conductor must have told everybody on the train that I was up front, because all night long there was a constant procession of white men passing up and down the coach looking at me in a way that made my blood boil. I didn’t dare go to sleep, because I didn’t know what might happen. It was awful!”

She sat silent as she lived over again the horror of the ride. Then, shaking off her mood, she turned to him with a cheerful smile. “Thank Goodness, it’s over now, and I don’t want to think of it any more than I can help. Tell me all about yourself and what you’ve been doing and everything,” she finished all in one breath.

He told her briefly what had been going on, of his plans for the hospital, of the meeting at Reverend Wilson’s, and other items of interest about life in Central City, until they had arrived at her home. He waited for an invitation to come in, but in the excitement of seeing her mother and father again, she forgot all about Kenneth. Placing her bags on the porch, he turned and left after promising to run over for a while that evening.

===Chinese===
他问起她这一趟——还顺利吗？「呃，糟透了！」她答道，一回想起来就打了个寒噤。「到亚特兰大为止我坐的是普尔曼卧铺车（Pullman），可一到那里就得换上那又脏又旧的吉姆·克劳车厢。车上一群黑人（Negro），带着三四夸脱廉价烈酒。他们讨厌透了。嗐，他们竟然还有脸请我喝上一口！那列车员准是把我在前面车厢的事告诉了车上每一个人，因为整整一夜，白人男人一队接一队地从车厢里走来走去打量我，那眼光看得我火冒三丈。我都不敢合眼，因为不知道会出什么事。真是受罪！」

她默默坐着，似乎又把那一夜的折磨重新经历了一遍。随即她甩开这种情绪，转过脸来对他粲然一笑。「谢天谢地，总算过去了，能不去想它我就不去想。跟我说说你自己的事吧，你都在忙些什么，样样都讲给我听，」她一口气说完了。

他简要地把最近发生的事告诉了她——他办医院的打算、在威尔逊牧师家里的那次会议，以及中央城生活里其他有趣的见闻，一直说到车停在她家门口。他等她请他进屋，可她沉浸在重见父母亲的兴奋里，把肯尼思忘得一干二净。他把行李放到门廊上，答应晚上再过来坐坐，便转身走了。

===Original===
The time seemed to go by on dragging feet that day. It seemed as though evening never would come. It did at last, however, and as soon as he finished with the last patient, he went over to Jane’s home. Refreshed by a long rest, she greeted him clad in a dress of some filmy blue material. They seated themselves on the porch, shaded by vines from the eyes of passersby. Over Kenneth there came a feeling of contentment—life had not been easy for him and he had been denied a confidante with whom he could discuss the perplexities he had experienced in Central City. The talk for a time drifted from one topic to another. Before he knew it, Kenneth was telling Jane of his ambitions, of the plans he had made before coming back to Central City, of the successes and failures he had met with, of his hopes for the future. Jane listened without speaking for some time. Life among coloured people is so intense, so earnest, so serious a problem in the South, that never do two intelligent Negroes talk very long before the race problem in some form is under discussion. Jane interrupted Kenneth in the midst of his recital.

“Kenneth, did you really believe that you could come back here to Central City and keep entirely away from the race problem?”

“I don’t know that I thought it out as carefully as that, but I hoped to do something like that,” was his uneasy reply. He had the feeling that she didn’t altogether approve of him. Her next words proved that she didn’t.

“Well, you can’t do it. Just because your father got along all right is no reason why you should do the same things he did. You are living in a time that is as different from his as his was from his great-grandfather’s.”

“But—” he attempted to defend himself.

===Chinese===
那一天，时间像是拖着脚步走过似的。怎么也盼不到天黑。天总算还是黑了，他一打发走最后一个病人，便赶到简家里。她长长歇了一觉，养足了精神，穿着一身轻纱似的蓝色衣裙出来迎他。他们在门廊上坐下，藤蔓遮住了路人的视线。肯尼思心里涌起一股踏实——他这一生并不顺遂，在中央城经历的种种困惑，也一直找不到一个可以推心置腹的人商量。两人东一句西一句地聊了一阵。不知不觉间，肯尼思已对简讲起了自己的抱负，讲起他回中央城之前所做的种种打算，讲起他所经历的成败，以及对将来的期望。简一言不发地听了一会儿。在南方，有色人的生活是如此紧张、如此严峻、如此沉重的一个问题，以至于两个有头脑的黑人，谈不了一会儿，总要以某种形式谈起种族问题。简在肯尼思正讲着的时候打断了他。

「肯尼思，你当真以为，你能回到中央城来，完全置身于种族问题之外吗？」

「我也不知道自己有没有那么仔细地想过，可我确实盼望能办到差不多那样，」他局促地答道。他有一种感觉，觉得她并不完全赞同他。她接下来的话证实了这一点。

「哼，你办不到。你不能因为你父亲那一套行得通，就以为自己照着他那样做也行。你所处的时代，和他那个时代截然不同，就像他的时代和他曾祖父的时代截然不同一样。」

「可是——」他想为自己辩解。

===Original===
“Wait a minute until I’ve had my say,” she checked him. “Only a few years ago they said that as soon as Negroes got property and made themselves good citizens the race problem would be solved. They said that only bad Negroes were ever lynched and they alone caused all the trouble. But you just think back over the list of coloured people right here in Central City who’ve had the most trouble during the past two years. What do you find? That it is the Negro who has acquired more property than the average white man, they are always picking on. Poor whites resent seeing a Negro more prosperous than they, and they satisfy their resentment by making it hard on that Negro. Am I right—or am I wrong?”

“I suppose there is something in what you say—but what’s the answer? You’re damned if you do—and you’re damned if you don’t!”

“I don’t know what the answer is—if I did, I’d certainly try to put it into use, instead of sitting around and trying to dodge trouble. If one of your patients had a cancer, you wouldn’t advise him to use Christian Science in treating it, would you?”

Without pausing for a reply, she went on, her words pouring out in a flood that made Kenneth feel as he did as a boy when spanked by his mother. “No, you wouldn’t! You’d operate! And that’s just what the coloured people and the white people of the South have got to do. That is, those who’ve got any sense and backbone. If they don’t, then this thing they call the race problem is going to grow so big it’s going to consume the South and America. It’s almost that big now.”

===Chinese===
「等一下，让我把话说完，」她止住他。「就在几年前他们还说，只要黑人有了产业、做了好公民，种族问题就自然解决了。他们还说，只有坏黑人才会遭私刑，麻烦全是他们一个人惹出来的。可你回想一下，光是中央城这里，过去两年间闹出最大乱子的那批有色人，你都数得上谁？你发现了什么？他们挑中下手的，恰恰是那些产业置得比普通白人还多的黑人。穷白佬（Cracker）[1]见黑人比他们还阔气，心里就嫉恨，便拿那个黑人出气，故意让他日子难过。我说得对——还是说错了？」

「我想你说的有几分道理——可办法是什么？做也不是，不做也不是！」

「我也不知道办法是什么——我要是知道，肯定早就拿来一试了，犯不着坐在这儿东躲西藏地躲麻烦。要是你的一个病人长了癌，你总不会劝他去信基督教科学派（Christian Science）来治吧？」

她不等他回答，又接着说下去，那话像洪水一般倾泻出来，让肯尼思觉得自己就像小时候挨母亲打屁股时的滋味。「对，你绝不会！你会动手术！而这正是南方的有色人和白人所必须做的事。我是说，那些还有点头脑、有点骨气的人。他们要是不这么干，那么他们口中的所谓种族问题，就会越长越大，大到把整个南方、整个美国都吞下去。如今差不多已经长到那么大了。」

===Original===
She paused for breath. Kenneth started to speak but she checked him with her hand.

“I’m not through yet! I’ve been thinking over this thing for a long time, just as every other Negro has done who’s got brains enough to do any thinking at all. I am sick and tired of hearing all this prating about the ‘superior race.’ Superior—humph! Kenneth, what you and all the rest of Negroes need is to learn that you belong to a race that was centuries old when the first white man came into the world. You’ve got to learn that a large part of this thing they call ‘white civilization’ was made by black hands, as well as by yellow and brown and red hands, too, besides what white hands have created. You’ve got to learn that the Negro today is contributing as much of the work that makes this civilization possible as the white race, if not more. Be proud of your race and quit whining and cringing! You’ll never get anywhere until you do! There, I’ve wanted to get that out of my system for a long time ever since we talked together last Christmas. Now it’s out and I’m through!”

Kenneth sat quiet. While she had been pouring forth her tirade, he had thought of several logical arguments he could have advanced. But she had given him no chance to utter them. Now they seemed weak and useless. He was resentful—what did women know about the practical problems and difficulties of life, anyway? His anger was not abated by the realization that Jane felt that he had been trying to avoid his responsibility to himself and to his people—that he had been a coward. And yet she was right in a general way in what she had said. Masking as well as he could the chagrin he felt at her words, he told her of the trouble Tucker and Tracy and Swann and the other sharecroppers were having, and gave her further details of the meeting at Reverend Wilson’s.

===Chinese===
她停下来喘口气。肯尼思刚要开口，她抬手止住了他。

「我还没说完呢！这事我想了很久了，就像每一个脑瓜子还管点用、还肯动动脑子的黑人一样。那一套鼓吹什么「优越种族」的陈词滥调，我都听腻了、听烦了。优越——哼！肯尼思，你和所有别的黑人最需要明白的一件事是：你们所属的这个种族，在第一个白人来到这世上之前，就已经有了几百年的历史。你得明白，他们口中所谓「白人的文明」，有很大一部分是黑人的手创造的，还有黄种人的、棕种人的、红种人的手，除了白人的手所创造的之外。你得明白，今日的黑人对维持这一文明所做的工作，和白人种族一样多，甚至更多。要为自己的种族骄傲，别再唉声叹气、低三下四！你要不这样，永远也别想有出息！好了，自从去年圣诞节我们谈过之后，我就一直想把这番话痛痛快快地说出来。如今说出来，我也说完了！」

肯尼思默默坐着。她那一番话倾泻而出的时候，他脑子里其实想到好几个可以反驳的逻辑论点。可她根本不给他开口的机会。如今这些论点显得苍白无力。他心里恼火——女人家懂得什么生活中的实际问题与艰难？而他越想越气的是，简显然觉得他一直在逃避对自己、对本族人的责任——觉得他是个懦夫。然而她说的话，大体上是对的。他尽力掩藏住她那番话给自己带来的难堪，把塔克、特雷西、斯旺以及其他佃农（sharecropper）所遭遇的麻烦讲给她听，又把威尔逊牧师家里那次会议的详情细细说了一遍。

===Original===
She sensed the wound to his pride that she had inflicted. She did not regret doing what she had done—on the long ride home she had determined that she would tell him those very things as soon as she could find opportunity—but, with a woman’s natural tenderness, she regretted the necessity of hurting him. She put her hand over his for an instant, touched at his dejected manner.

“I’m sorry, Ken, if I hurt you, but I did it because you are too fine a man, and you’ve got too good an education, to try to dodge an issue as plain as yours. Why, Kenneth, you’ve had it mighty soft—just think of the thousands of coloured boys all over the South who are too poor to get even a high-school training. You’ve never had to get down and dig for what you’ve got—perhaps it would have been better if you had. It’s men with your brains and education that have got to take the leadership. You’ve got to make good! That’s just the reason they try to make it hard for men like you—they know that if you ever get going, their treating the Negro as they have has got to stop! They’re darned scared of educated Negroes with brains—that’s why they make it hard for you!”

Kenneth threw out his hands, palms upward, and shrugged his shoulders.

“I suppose I agree with you in theory, Jane, but what are the practical ways of doing the things you say I ought to do? How, for example, can I help Tracy and Tucker and all the rest of the farmers who’re being robbed of all they earn every year?”

“Don’t get angry now just because I touched your masculine vanity. I know about the share-cropping system in a general way. Tell me the facts that were brought out at the meeting.”

===Chinese===
她察觉到自己刺伤了他的自尊。她并不后悔刚才所做的——在回家的漫长旅途上，她已下定决心，一有机会便要把这些话原原本本告诉他——但出于一个女人天生的温柔，她又为不得不刺痛他而心生不忍。她把自己的手覆在他的手上片刻，为他那垂头丧气的样子而心生怜惜。

「对不住，肯，要是我伤了你，可我这么做，是因为你是个太好的男人，受过的教育也太好了，不该去回避像你眼前这样明摆着的问题。嗐，肯尼思，你这一辈子过得太舒坦了——你想想南方有成千上万的有色人孩子，穷得连中学都念不起。你从来没有过蹲下身子、土里刨食地去挣你所有的一切——也许你要是那样过过，反倒更好。正是有你这样脑子和教育的人，必须出来带头。你非得干出个名堂来不可！正因如此，他们才要故意让你这样的人日子难过——他们知道，你这样的人一旦上了路，他们那样对待黑人的做法就得收场！他们最怕的就是有脑子的、受过教育的黑人——这就是他们为难你的原因！」

肯尼思双手一摊，掌心朝上，耸了耸肩。

「我想我在道理上是同意你的，简，可你说的那些我应该做的事，具体怎么做呢？比方说，我怎么能帮得上特雷西、塔克，还有所有那些一年到头辛辛苦苦、却被洗劫一空的农户？」

「别因为我戳了你那男子汉的虚荣心就恼火。分成制（share-cropping）那一套我大体上也知道一些。把会上摆出来的那些事实讲给我听。」

===Original===
Kenneth told her in detail the things Hiram Tucker and the others had said. She sat in thought for a minute, her chin cupped in the palm of her hand, her elbow resting on the arm of the chair, as she rocked back and forth. Kenneth sat watching her in what was almost sardonic amusement. He had been wrestling with this same problem ever since Thursday night and was no nearer a solution than he had been then. It would be amusing in a few minutes, after all her high-flown thoughts and elaborate generalities about bucking the race question, when she would be forced to admit that when it came to solving one of the practical problems of the whole question her generalizing would be of no avail. He was aroused by a question thrown at him suddenly by Jane.

“Do these folks have to buy their supplies from the landlord?”

“Not that I know of,” he replied. “They buy from the landlord, or the merchant designated by the landlord, because they haven’t the money or the credit to trade anywhere else.”

There followed another pause while the rocking began again.

“Do you remember any of the economics you learned at school?” was the next query. He replied that he supposed he did.

“Have you got any books on cooperative societies?” He doubted whether he had.

“Well, never mind.” She swung her chair around, facing Kenneth, and leaned forward intently, the light from the arc-lamp in the corner illumining her face and revealing the eager, enthusiastic look upon it.

“Kenneth, why can’t those coloured people pool their money and buy their goods wholesale and then distribute them at cost?”

Kenneth laughed, it must be confessed a little cheerfully, that she had gone from one problem into the mazes of another that was just as difficult.

“For the very same reason that they are in the predicament they are in today. They haven’t got the money. Perhaps you can tell me where the money to start this cooperative scheme is coming from?”

===Chinese===
肯尼思把海勒姆·塔克和旁人所说的那些事，原原本本讲给简听。她托着下巴想了一会儿，下巴捧在掌心里，胳膊支在椅子扶手上，身子前后摇着。肯尼思坐在一旁看她，几乎带着一种讥诮的玩味。自从周四晚上以来，他一直和这个问题较劲，可离解决还和那时一样遥远。再过几分钟就要好笑了——她方才发表了那一大通高远的思想、关于硬碰种族问题的种种宏论，到头来却不得不承认，一旦要解决整个问题里某个实际难题，她那些泛泛而谈便全然派不上用场。简突然抛过来一个问题，把他惊醒了。

「这些人非得到东家那儿去买日用品不可吗？」

「据我所知不是，」他答道，「他们到东家、或东家指定的铺子去买，是因为他们既没有钱、也没有信用到别处去买。」

接着又是一阵沉默，椅子又摇了起来。

「你在学校里学的那些经济学（economics），还记得多少？」这是下一个问题。他答道，大概还记得一些吧。

「你家里有没有关于合作社（cooperative societies）的书？」他怀疑自己有没有。

「嗯，那也没关系。」她把椅子一转，正对着肯尼思，身子急切地前倾，角落里弧光灯的光照在她脸上，映出她脸上那一副热切、振奋的神情。

「肯尼思，那些有色人为什么不能把钱凑在一起，批发进货，再按成本价分发给大家呢？」

肯尼思笑了——不得不说，笑得有几分畅快——笑她从一个难题钻进了另一个同样棘手的迷阵里。

「就因为他们今日陷在这一困境里的同一个原因。他们没钱。开办这个合作社（cooperative）的钱从哪儿来，你大概能告诉我吧？」

===Original===
“That’s an easy one to answer. It’s going to come from you and papa and three or four more of these folks here in town who can afford it! Oh, Ken, can’t you see what a big thing you can do? There are lots of people, white people I mean, right here in Central City, who’d be glad to help these poor Negroes get out of debt. Papa was telling us today about a talk he had with Judge Stevenson the other day. The Judge said he wished there was some way to help without it making him unpopular with the other folks here in town. Of course, the folks who are making money off this system, the landlords and the storekeepers, won’t like it, but you can go and talk with folks like Judge Stevenson and Mr. Baird down at the Bank of Central City. If this first trial succeeds—and I know it will be a success—it’ll spread all over Smith County, and then all over Georgia, and then all over the South, and the coloured folks will have millions of dollars that they’ve been cheated out of before. That, Kenneth Harper, is one way you can lead, and it won’t get you in bad with the white people at least the decent ones—either.”

Kenneth began to be infected by her enthusiasm. He saw that her idea had possibilities. But, manlike, he didn’t want to give in too soon or too readily.

“There is something in what you say, Jane, but the details will have to be worked out first before we can tell if it is a practicable idea. I’ll think it—”

Jane interrupted him, showing that she hadn’t even been listening to him.

“When are you to meet again at Reverend Wilson’s?” she asked.

He told her.

“Well, I tell you what we’ll do. You go home and think over all the ways we can put this idea into practice. I’ll do the same thing. And then we’ll talk it over again tomorrow night. On Wednesday you go down to see Judge Stevenson and see if he will draw up the papers so it’ll be legal and binding and everything else. Then on Thursday night you can present this as your own idea, and I’ll bet you anything you say, they’ll take it up and you’ll be the one chosen to lead the whole movement.”

After some discussion of details, Kenneth left. The more he thought of Jane’s idea, the more it appealed to him. At any rate, she had suggested more in half an hour than he had been able to think of in four days. Hadn’t the cooperative societies been the backbone of the movement to get rid of the Czar in Russia? If the Russian peasants, who certainly weren’t as educated as the Negro in America, had made a success of the idea, the Negro in the South ought to do it. By Jove, they could do it! Idea after idea sprang to his mind, after the seed had been sown by Jane, until he had visions of a vast cooperative society not only buying but selling the millions of dollars’ worth of products raised by the nine million Negroes of the South. And that wasn’t all! These societies would be formed with each member paying monthly dues, like the fraternal organizations. When enough money was in the treasury, they would employ the very best lawyers money could get to take one of those cases where a Negro had not been able to get a fair settlement with his landlord, and make a test case of it. What if they did lose in the local court? They’d take it to the State Supreme Court! What if they did lose even there? They’d take it clear up to the United States Supreme Court! They were sure to win there. Kenneth walked home with his head whirling with the project’s possibilities. He saw a new day coming when a man in the South would no longer be exploited and robbed just because he was black. And when that came, lynching and everything else like it would go too. He felt already like Matthew and Andrew and Peter and John and the other disciples when they started out to bring the good news to the whole world. For wasn’t he a latter-day disciple bringing a new solution and a new hope to his people?

It was not until Kenneth had gone to bed that he realized that though he had been with Jane all the evening, he had had not one minute when he could have spoken of love to her. Musing thus, he fell asleep.

===Chinese===
「这个好回答。钱从你、爸爸，还有城里另外三四个出得起这钱的人这儿来！噢，肯，你难道看不出你能做成一件多大的事吗？就在中央城这里，有很多人——我是说白人——会很乐意帮这些可怜的黑人摆脱一身债。爸爸今天还跟我们讲起，前几天他和史蒂文森法官的一席谈。法官说，他巴不得有个什么法子能帮上一把，又不至于让他在城里其他人面前落个不是。当然了，那些靠这套制度发财的人，东家和铺子老板，是不会乐意的，可你可以去找史蒂文森法官、还有中央城银行（Bank of Central City）的贝尔德先生那样的人谈。这头一炮要是打响了——我知道一定能成——它就会传遍整个史密斯县，然后传遍整个佐治亚，然后传遍整个南方，有色人就能把过去被人骗走的成百上千万的钱揣进自己兜里。肯尼思·哈珀，这就是你能带头的一条路，而且至少不会让你得罪白人——至少不会得罪那些正派的白人。」

肯尼思渐渐被她的热忱感染了。他看出她这个主意确有可为之处。可是，像所有男人一样，他不愿这么快、这么轻易地就服输。

「你说的有几分道理，简，可总得先把细节捋清楚了，我们才看得出这主意到底行不行得通。我得想一想——」

简打断了他，可见她根本连听都没在听他说话。

「你们什么时候再到威尔逊牧师那里碰头？」她问。

他告诉了她。

「嗯，我跟你说我们这么办。你回家去，把能把这个主意落到实处的种种办法都想一遍。我也一样。然后明晚我们再把它过一遍。周三你去找史蒂文森法官，看他肯不肯把文书拟好，好叫这事合法、有约束力，样样齐全。然后周四晚上，你就把这个当作你自己的主意提出来，我跟你随便打什么赌，他们一定会上赶着采纳，而且挑你出来领头做这整个运动。」

又商量了一阵细节之后，肯尼思告辞了。他越想简这个主意，越觉得它对胃口。不管怎么说，她半小时里想出来的，比他四天里琢磨出来的都多。俄国推翻沙皇的那场运动，合作社（cooperative societies）不正是它的脊梁骨吗？俄国农民——他们的教育程度肯定不及美国的黑人——既能把这套主意办成，南方的黑人自然也该办得到。老天，他们办得到！简一旦播下了这颗种子，一个接一个的念头便涌上他心头，直到他眼前浮现出一幅图景：一个庞大的合作社，不仅采买，还推销南方九百万黑人种出来的那价值几百万美元的产品。这还不算！这些合作社将像兄弟会（fraternal organizations）那样组织起来，每个成员按月缴纳会费。等库里攒够了钱，他们就要花钱请得起的最好的律师，挑一件黑人没法和东家公平了结的案子来打，把它做成一个判例案子（test case）。在地方法院输了又怎样？他们就告到州最高法院（State Supreme Court）！就是在那里输了又怎样？他们就一直告到美国最高法院（United States Supreme Court）！在那里他们一定赢。肯尼思一路走回家，脑子里翻腾着这计划种种可能。他看见一个新时代正在到来：在南方，一个人再不会仅仅因为皮肤是黑的，就被人盘剥、被人劫掠。到那时候，私刑以及诸如此类的一切，也都要跟着绝迹。他觉得自己已经像马太、安得烈、彼得、约翰以及其他门徒当年出发去向天下传福音时那样。因为他，岂不也是一个新时代的使徒，要把一种新的解决办法、一份新的希望带给他本族的人吗？

直到肯尼思上了床，他才意识到：尽管他和简待了整整一个晚上，他却连一分钟能向她倾诉爱情的机会都没有。他这样想着，便睡着了。

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **Cracker（穷白佬）**：美国南方对贫穷白人的贬称，源出他们放牧时甩鞭子驱赶牛群的声响（crack），后泛指南方教育程度不高、生活拮据的白人阶层。此处出自简之口，带阶级与种族的双重意味——她指出正是这些穷白人最嫉恨黑人比他们过得好。译文保留其贬义，以「穷白佬」对应，附原文以存其历史语境。

## XI / 十一

===Original===
Early the next morning Kenneth rose and rummaged through his books until he found his old and battered textbooks on economics.

Into these he dipped during the intervals between patients, making notes of ideas which seemed useful in the organization of the cooperative society. The more he read, the more feasible the plan seemed. Properly guided and carefully managed, there was no reason, so far as he could see, why the society should not be a success. Eighty percent of the farmers of the South, white and coloured, he estimated, suffered directly or indirectly from the present economic system. Though his interest was in the Negro tillers of the soil, success in their case would inevitably react favourably on the white just as oppression and exploitation of the Negro had done more harm to white people in the South than to Negroes. Kenneth felt the warm glow of the crusader in a righteous cause. Already he saw a new day in the South with white and coloured people free from oppression and hatred and prejudice—prosperous and contented because of that prosperity. He could see a lifting of the clouds of ignorance which hung over all the South, an awakening of the best in all the people of the South. Thus has youth dreamed since the beginning of time. Thus will youth ever dream. And in those dreams rests the hope of the world, for without them this world with all its defects would sink into the black abyss of despair, never to rise again.

===Chinese===
第二天一清早，肯尼思起了床，在书堆里翻找，直到找出了他那几本破旧的经济学（economics）老课本。

在病人看病的间隙，他便翻阅这些书，把那些似乎对组织合作社（cooperative society）有用的想法一一记下。他越读，这计划越显得行得通。只要引导得当、经营细致，就他所见，这合作社没有不成功的道理。据他估计，南方的农户——不论白人还是有色人——十之有八都直接或间接地吃这现行经济制度的苦头。他虽把心放在那些黑人（Negro）庄稼人身上，但他们的成功必然惠及白人，正像过去对黑人的压迫和盘剥，对南方白人所造成的祸害，反倒比对黑人本身更深。肯尼思感到一股十字军为正义事业而战的暖意涌上心头。他已经依稀看见南方一个新时代的到来：白人和有色人都不再受压迫、受仇恨、受偏见——因这富足而殷实、而知足。他看见笼罩整个南方的那片愚昧之云正在散去，看见南方所有人身上最美好的东西正在苏醒。自古以来，青年人就是这样做梦的。今后，青年人也永远会这样做梦。而世界的希望，就寄托在这些梦上；若没有这些梦，这个满是缺陷的世界便会沉入绝望的黑渊，再也浮不起来。

===Original===
His work finished for the day, he went as soon as he decently could to talk with Jane. She, too, had been at work. Eagerly they planned between them the infinite details of so ambitious a scheme. Confidently they discounted possible difficulties they might expect to encounter—the opposition of the whites who were profiting from the present system, the petty jealousies and suspicions of those who would gain most from the success of their scheme. They realized that the Negro had been robbed so much, both by his own people and by the whites, that he was chary of new plans and projects. They knew he was contentious and quarrelsome. These things seemed trivial, however, for with the natural expansiveness of the young they felt that difficulties like these were but trifles to be airily brushed aside.

===Chinese===
当天的事一办完，他一得着合宜的空便赶去和简商谈。她那一头也没闲着。两人热切地一起筹划着这么一个雄心勃勃的计划中那千头万绪的细节。他们满怀信心地不把可能遭遇的种种难处放在眼里——那些正从现行制度中获利的白人的反对，那些本将从计划成功中得益最多的人那点小小的嫉妒和猜疑。他们心里明白，黑人被自己的本族人、也被白人坑骗得太多次了，对新计划、新花样总是心存戒备。他们也知道他爱争爱闹。但这些都显得无足轻重，因为凭着年轻人天然的开朗，他们觉得这种种难处不过是些可轻轻拂去的小麻烦。

===Original===
Jane was not too much engrossed in their plans to notice the change in Kenneth’s manner. She had watched him closely during the times she had seen him since his return. He had been almost morose, his mind divided between his work and the effort to keep to a “middle-of-the-road” course in his relations with the whites. The inevitable conflict within himself, the lack of decisiveness in his daily life that he consciously developed and which was so diametrically opposite to that he used in his profession, had begun to create a complex personality that was far from pleasing. In a freer atmosphere Kenneth would have been a direct, straightforward character, swift to decision and quick of action. One cannot, however, compromise principle constantly and consciously without bearing the marks of such conflicts.

===Chinese===
简并未因沉浸在计划中而忽略了肯尼思神态上的变化。自从他回来之后，她每次见他，都把他看得很仔细。他几乎有些阴郁，心思一头挂在工作上，一头又用在竭力维持与白人交往时的那条「中间路线」。他内心那不可避免的冲突，他在日常生活中有意养成的优柔寡断——这与他行医时的作风恰恰相反——已开始造就出一个并不讨人喜欢的复杂人格。换一个更自由的空气，肯尼思本会是个直来直去、干脆利落的性子，决断迅速，行动敏捷。然而，一个人不能时刻、清醒地妥协原则，而不在身上留下这种种冲突的痕迹。

===Original===
His compromises were not all conscious ones, though. He believed honestly it was wisest that he observe some sort of halfway ground between rank cowardice and uncompromising opposition to the conditions which existed. In doing so, he had no sense of physical or moral timidity. He knew no Negro could yet safely advocate complete freedom for the Negro in the South. He felt there had been improvement during the past half-century in those conditions. He believed that in time all of the Negro’s present problems would be solved satisfactorily. If, by not trying to rush things, he could help in that solution, he was content. In believing thus, Kenneth was different in no way from the majority of intelligent Negroes in the South: temporizing with the truth, it may be, yet of such temporizations and compromises is the life of the Negro all over the South.

===Chinese===
不过，他的妥协并不全是清醒为之。他真心相信，在赤裸裸的懦弱和对现行状况毫不妥协的对抗之间，守某种折中之道，是最为明智的。他这样做，丝毫没有肉体上或道德上的怯懦之感。他知道，在南方，还没有哪个黑人能安然地为黑人争取完全的自由。他觉得这半个世纪以来，境况是有所改善的。他相信，黑人眼下所有的问题，终有一日会得到圆满的解决。只要他不去急于求成，就能为这解决出一分力，他便知足了。肯尼思这样想，与南方大多数有头脑的黑人并无二致：也许是对真理的一种权宜，然而南方黑人的全部生活，正是由这种种权宜与妥协构成的。

===Original===
With the evolving of a plan which enabled him to be of help and, at the same time, involved him in no danger of trouble with his white neighbours, Kenneth took on an eagerness which was at marked variance with his former manner. His eyes shone with the desire to make their plan a success. Of a tender and sympathetic nature, almost with the gentleness of a woman, he realized now that the burdens of his race had lain heavy upon him. He had suffered in their suffering, had felt almost as though he had been the victim when he read or heard of a lynching, had chafed under the bonds which bound the hands and feet and heart and soul of his people. But launched as he now was on a plan to furnish relief from one of the worst of those bonds, he had changed overnight into a determined and purposeful and ardent worker towards the goal he and Jane had set for themselves. Jane rejoiced at the changed air of Kenneth—he seemed to have emerged from the shell in which he had encased himself and, womanlike, she rejoiced that he had done so through her own work.

So absorbed had they been in discussion of their plans that the time had flown by as though on wings. Ten o’clock was announced by Mr. Phillips in the room above by the dropping of his shoes, one after the other, on the floor. Kenneth needed no second signal, he rose to go. Jane went to the door with him.

“Kenneth, you’re entirely different from the way you were yesterday. I’m so glad.…”

===Chinese===
如今有了一个既能让他出力、又不致把他卷入与白人邻居纠纷的计划，肯尼思生出一种热切，与往日那副神情截然不同。他眼里闪着要使计划成功的光。他天性温柔、富于同情，几乎带着几分女子的细腻，此刻才意识到自己本族的重担一直沉沉地压在他身上。他曾为本族的苦难而一同受苦，读到或听到一桩私刑（lynching）时，几乎像是自己也成了受害者一样感同身受，曾为本族那捆住了手脚、也捆住了心魂的枷锁而愤懑不已。可如今他既已投身到一个能松开其中最沉重一道枷锁的计划之中，他一夜之间便变成了一个坚决、有目标、满腔热忱的人，朝着他和简为自己定下的那个目标奋力向前。简为肯尼思这番变化而欣喜——他似乎已从自己为自己裹上的那层壳里钻了出来，而她，像一个女子那样，为他是经由她的一番功夫才钻出来的而更是欣喜。

他们谈计划谈得太投入，时光竟像插上了翅膀一般飞逝。十点钟是楼上房里的菲利普斯先生宣布的——他把鞋子一只接一只地丢在地板上，便是报时。肯尼思无需第二个信号，起身便要走。简陪他走到门口。

「肯尼思，你跟昨天简直判若两人。我真高兴。……」

===Original===
The next morning he called on Judge Stevenson. The Judge’s office was above the Bon Ton Store in a two-story brick building on Lee Street. Kenneth climbed the flight of dingy, dusty stairs which bore alternately on the vertical portions tin signs inscribed:

Richard P. Stevenson, Attorney-at-Law

				and

				Dr. J. C. Carpenter, Dentist.

The judge’s office was at the head of the stairs and in it Kenneth found the old lawyer seated near the window, his coat off, and in his mouth the long, thin, villainous-looking cigar without which few persons in Central City ever remembered seeing him, though none had ever seen one of them lighted. He chewed on it ruminatively when in repose. When engaged in an argument, either in or out of a courtroom, and especially when opposition caused his choleric temper to be aroused, he chewed furiously as though he would have enjoyed treating his enemy of the moment in similar fashion. He was tall and thickset, his snow-white hair brushed straight back from his forehead like the mane of a lion. Skin reddened by exposure to sun and wind, bushy eyebrows from under which gleamed fiery eyes that could shift in an instant from twinkling good humour to flashing indignation or anger, thin nose and ample mouth, his face was one that would command respect or at least attention in almost any gathering. He wore loosely fitting, baggy clothes that draped his ample figure with a gracefulness that added to his distinguished appearance. Many thought he resembled at first glance that famous Kentuckian, Henry Watterson, and indeed he did bear an unmistakable likeness to “Marse Henry.”

===Chinese===
第二天上午，他去拜访史蒂文森法官（Judge Stevenson）。法官的事务所在「好时光」百货店（Bon Ton Store）楼上，是李街（Lee Street）上一栋两层的砖楼。肯尼思爬上那道昏暗、积灰的楼梯，楼梯的竖板上交替钉着几块铁皮招牌，分别写着：

理查德·P. 史蒂文森，律师（Richard P. Stevenson, Attorney-at-Law）

　　　　及

　　　　J. C. 卡彭特医生，牙医（Dr. J. C. Carpenter, Dentist）。

法官的事务所在楼梯口，肯尼思进去时，见这位老律师正坐在窗边，外套脱了，嘴里叼着一支又长又细、看上去一脸凶相的雪茄——中央城的人几乎都想不起来几时见过他没有这支雪茄，尽管谁也没见过他把这支雪茄点着过。他闲坐时，便若有所思地嚼着它。一旦争辩起来——不论是在法庭里还是法庭外，尤其是当对手惹起了他那暴躁的性子时——他便恶狠狠地嚼，仿佛恨不得以同样的方式来处置他此刻的敌人。他身材高大敦实，一头雪白的头发从前额直直往后梳去，像一头雄狮的鬃毛。日晒风吹晒得发红的皮肤，浓密的眉毛下一双火灼灼的眼睛，能在一瞬间从闪烁的诙谐好脾气一变而为闪电般的义愤或盛怒；薄薄的鼻子、阔阔的嘴——他这张脸，几乎在任何场合都能赢得敬意，至少也能引人注目。他穿着宽大松垂的衣裳，颇为从容地裹住他那魁梧的身形，反倒为他那副气派添了几分雍容。许多人乍一看都说他像那位著名的肯塔基人亨利·沃特森（Henry Watterson），他也确实与那位「亨利老爷（Marse Henry）」有不容置疑的相似之处。

===Original===
The judge’s life had been a curious combination of contradictions. He had fought valiantly in the Confederate army as a major, serving under “Stonewall” Jackson, whose memory he worshipped second only to that of his wife, who had died some ten years before. He bore a long scar, reminder of the wound that had laid him low during the battle of Atlanta. His mode of brushing his hair back was adopted to cover the mark, but when he talked, as he loved to do, of his martial experiences, he would always, at the same time in the narrative, brush, with one sweep of his hand, the hair down over his forehead and reveal the jagged scar of which he was inordinately proud.

===Chinese===
法官的一生，是一个由矛盾凑成的奇特组合。他当年在邦联军（Confederate army）里官至少校，奋勇作战，曾在「石墙」杰克逊（“Stonewall” Jackson）麾下效力——他对这位将军的追念，仅次于对亡妻的追念，妻子大约已去世十年。他身上留着一道长长的伤疤，那是亚特兰大战役中把他放倒的那处创伤留下的。他把头发往后梳的这种做法，原本就是为遮住这道疤，可每当他像他所爱做的那样，谈起自己的戎马生涯时，总会在讲到同一处时，用手一拂，把头发披到前额上，露出那道参差的伤疤——他对此自负得了不得。

===Original===
With the end of the Civil War, he had reconciled himself to the result though it had meant the loss of most of his wealth. He harboured little bitterness towards the North, unlike most of his comrades in arms who never were willing to forgo any opportunity to vent their venomous hatred of their conquerors. Judge Stevenson had counselled against such a spirit. So vigorously had he done it, he had alienated most of those who had been his closest friends. Following a speech he had delivered at one of the reunions of Confederate veterans in which he urged his comrades at least to meet halfway the overtures of friendliness from the North, he had been denounced from the floor of the convention as a “Yankee-lover,” and threatened with violence. Judge Stevenson with flashing eye and belligerent manner had jumped to his feet, offered to fight any man, or any ten men, who thought him guilty of treachery to the cause of the Confederacy, and when none accepted the challenge, denounced them as cowards and quit the convention.

===Chinese===
内战结束后，他已对这结局心平气和了，尽管这意味着他失去了大半家财。他对北方几乎不存什么怨恨，不像他那些老战友——他们从来不肯放过任何一个机会，去发泄他们对征服者的恶毒仇恨。史蒂文森法官曾劝人不要抱这种心态。他劝得那样激烈，以至于疏远了大多数原本最亲密的朋友。有一次，在邦联老兵（Confederate veterans）的一场团聚会上，他发表演说，劝老战友们至少要对北方示好的姿态接个半步，当场便被人从会场席上痛斥为「北佬党（Yankee-lover）」，还受到了动武的威胁。史蒂文森法官目光炯炯、一副好斗的模样，霍地跳起来，邀战任何一个、或任何十个认为他对邦联事业不忠的人；见无人应战，便骂他们是懦夫，拂袖而去，退出了大会。

===Original===
He had hoped that, with the passing on, one by one, of the unreconstructed veterans of the Confederacy, a newer and less embittered generation, with no personal memories of the gall of defeat, would right things. Instead had come the rise of the poor whites with none of the culture and refinement of the old Southern aristocracy, a nation of petty minds and morals, vindictive, vicious, dishonest, and stupid. Lacking in nearly all the things that made the old South, at least the upper crust of it, the most civilized section of America at that time, he saw his friends and all they stood for inundated by this flood of crudeness and viciousness, until only a few remained left high and dry like bits of wreckage from a foundered ship cast up on the shore to rot away, while all around them raged this new regime, no longer poor in purse but eternally impoverished in culture and civilizing influences. On these the judge spat his contempt and he poured upon their unconcerned heads the vials of his venom and wrath.

===Chinese===
他曾盼望，随着那些至死不肯服输的邦联老兵一个接一个离世，一个更新、积怨更浅的一代人——他们不曾亲尝过战败的苦胆——会把事情拨正过来。可取而代之的，却是穷白人（poor whites）的得势，他们身上没有一丝旧南方贵族阶层的文化与教养，整个儿是一帮心胸狭隘、品行鄙陋、报复心重、阴毒、不老实、又愚蠢的人。旧南方——至少是旧南方的上流社会——之所以成为当时美国最文明的角落，靠的几乎全是他这一辈人所具备的种种品格；如今他们在这些方面几乎一无所有，他眼睁睁看着自己的朋友和他们所代表的一切，被这股粗鄙与毒恶的洪水吞没，直到只剩下寥寥几人，像一艘沉船漂散上岸的几片残骸，被搁在高处任其朽烂，而他们周围狂飙激荡的，却是这一套新秩序——钱袋不再瘪了，文化与教化却永远贫瘠。对这些人，法官啐之以鄙夷，把他的毒涎怒火一股脑儿倾到他们那毫不在意的头上。

===Original===
The second devastating blow he suffered was the succumbing, one by one, of his children to the new order. Nancy, his eldest daughter, had run away from home and married a merchant whose wealth had been gained through the petty thievery of padding accounts and other sharp practices on poorer whites and Negroes. Mary Ann, his other daughter, whom he loved above all others of his children, had fallen victim to an unfortunate love affair with a dashing but worthless son of their next-door neighbour. She had died in giving birth to her child, which, fortunately, the judge thought, had been born dead. His son had “gone in for politics.” He had been successful, as success was measured by the present-day South, but in his father’s eyes, judged by the uncompromising standards of that member of an older and nobler generation, he had sunk to levels of infamy from which he could never recover.

===Chinese===
他所受的第二记沉重打击，是自己的孩子们也一个接一个向这新秩序屈服了。大女儿南希离家出走，嫁给了一个商人——那人的钱财是靠在更穷的白人和黑人身上虚报账目、玩弄种种欺诈伎俩这种小偷小摸勾当捞来的。另一个女儿玛丽·安，是他在所有孩子里最爱的一个，却成了与隔壁人家那个神气活现、却一无所长的儿子一桩不幸恋情的牺牲品。她死于分娩，而她生下的那个孩子——法官以为这是万幸——是个死婴。他的儿子「搞起了政治」。以今日南方衡量成功的尺度来看，他是成功了，但在父亲眼里，用老一辈、高贵一辈那毫不通融的标准来衡量，他已堕入了永远也翻不了身的下流地步。

===Original===
The crowning misfortune dealt the judge by an unkind fate was the loss of his gentle, kindly wife. She had uncomplainingly borne their misfortunes one after another, had calmed and soothed her husband’s irascible tantrums, had been a haven to which he could come and find repose when buffeted by a world which he did not and could not understand. As long as she lived, he had been able to bear up despite the bitter disappointments life had dealt him. He had gone away to try a case in a nearby county, had returned after a two days’ absence and found her with a severe cold and fever.

For three weeks he did not leave her bedside, drove away in anger the trained nurse Dr. Bennett brought to the house, ministered gently to his wife’s every need, and held her in his arms as she breathed her last breath. Frantic at this last and most crushing blow, he cursed the doctor, though Dr. Bennett had done all he could in his bungling way, cursed God, cursed everything and everybody he could think of in his grief. He never recovered from this loss. His hair rapidly became white, he neglected his profession and sat by the hour, his eyes half closed, dreaming of his dead wife.…

===Chinese===
无情的命运给法官降下的最致命的不幸，是失去了他那位温柔善良的妻子。她毫无怨言地承受了他们一桩接一桩的不幸，平息、抚慰过丈夫那一阵阵的暴躁脾气，是他被一个他既不理解、也无法理解的世界捶打得疲了时，可以归来歇息的港湾。只要她还活着，无论生活给了他多少痛心的失望，他都还撑得住。有一次他离家去邻县出庭办一桩案子，走了两天，回来便见她染了重感冒，发起烧来。

整整三个礼拜，他没有离开过她的床边，愤怒地赶走了贝内特医生（Dr. Bennett）带到家里来的那个受过训练的护士，亲力亲为、温柔地照料妻子的一切需要，并在她咽下最后一口气时把她抱在怀里。这最后一记、也是最沉重的一击让他几乎发狂，他咒骂医生——尽管贝内特医生已用他那笨拙的方式尽了一切力量——咒骂上帝，咒骂他在悲恸中想得起来的每一件事、每一个人。这一打击他始终没有恢复过来。他的头发很快变白了，他荒疏了自己的业务，一坐就是几个钟头，两眼半阖，梦着他那亡妻……

===Original===
Had he chosen to adapt himself to the new order, he could have made money. This, however, he refused to do. He boasted proudly that never had he cheated any man or been a party to any transaction from which he emerged with any stain on his honour. Friend he was to all in his gentle, kindly manner—a relic of a day that had passed.…

He started, roused from one of his usual reveries, when Kenneth knocked on the open door. The gentle breezes of late spring stirred the mane of white hair as he brought his chair to the floor with a thump.

“Come in, Ken, come right in.” He welcomed Kenneth heartily, though in accordance with the Southern custom he did not offer to shake hands with his visitor. “How’s your maw? Heard you’re doing right well since you been back. Mighty glad to hear it, because yo’ daddy set a heap by you.”

===Chinese===
他若肯迁就这新秩序，本可以发财。可他拒绝这样做。他自豪地夸口说，他从未欺骗过任何人，也从未参与过任何一桩令他的荣誉沾上半点污迹的交易。他对所有人都是朋友，用的是他那温文、和善的态度——是一个已逝时代的遗物……

肯尼思敲了敲那扇敞开的门时，他正沉在他惯常的白日梦里，吓了一跳，回过神来。他把椅子「咚」地一声放回地上，晚春和煦的微风吹动了他那一头白发，像狮鬃一般。

「进来，肯，快进来。」他热忱地招呼肯尼思，不过按南方的规矩，他并没有伸手和客人握手。「你妈可好？听说你回来之后买卖挺不错。听了真叫人高兴，因为你爹可把你看得重呢。」

===Original===
Kenneth assured him he was progressing fairly well, told him his mother was well, and answered the innumerable questions the judge asked him. He knew that these were inevitable and must be answered before the judge would talk on any matter of business. After a few minutes of the desultory and perfunctory questions and answers, Kenneth told, when asked, the purpose of his visit. Chair tilted back again, elbows resting on the arms of the chair, fingers placed end to end, and his chin resting on the natural bridge thus formed, the judge listened to Kenneth’s recital of his plan without comment other than an occasional noncommittal grunt.

“… And what I would like from you, Judge Stevenson, is, first, do you think the plan will work, and, second, will you draw up the articles of incorporation and whatever other legal papers we need?” Kenneth ended. As an afterthought he added:

“You see, we want to do the job legally and above board, so there won’t be any misunderstanding of our motives.”

===Chinese===
肯尼思让他放心，说自己进展还不错，又告诉他母亲身体安好，然后回答了法官那一连串问不完的问题。他知道，这些是免不了的，非得一一答完，法官才会谈任何正经事。几分钟东拉西扯、例行公事般的问答之后，法官问起来意，肯尼思便把此行的目的说了。法官又把椅子往后一仰，两肘搭在扶手上，十指指尖相对，下巴搁在指尖自然搭成的桥上，听肯尼思把自己的计划讲了一遍，不发一言，只偶尔含混地哼上一声。

「……所以，史蒂文森法官，我想求您两件事：一是，您看这计划行得通行不通；二是，您肯不肯替我们把公司章程以及我们所需的其他一切法律文书都拟一拟？」肯尼思这样收尾。又像想起什么似的，补了一句：

「您看，我们要把这事办得合法、办得光明正大，省得人家对我们的用意有什么误会。」

===Original===
For a long time Judge Stevenson said nothing, nor did he give any indication that he was aware Kenneth had stopped speaking. In fact he seemed oblivious even of Kenneth’s presence. Knowing better than to interrupt him, Kenneth awaited somewhat anxiously the judge’s opinion. When the silence had lasted nearly five minutes, a vague alarm began to creep over Kenneth. Suppose the judge wasn’t as friendly towards coloured people as he had supposed? A word from him could start serious trouble before they got started. He wondered if he had acted wisely in revealing so much of their plans. He felt sure he had done wrong when he saw a look of what appeared to be anger pass over the judge’s face.

At last the old lawyer cleared his throat, his usual preliminary to speech. But when he did talk he began on another subject.

“What’re the folks out your way saying about these Kluxers? Any of you getting worried about these fools parading ’round like a bunch of damn fools?”

===Chinese===
好长一阵子，史蒂文森法官一言不发，也丝毫看不出他意识到肯尼思已经说完了。事实上，他仿佛连肯尼思在场都浑然不觉。肯尼思深知不便打断他，便有些焦急地等着法官发表意见。沉默持续了将近五分钟，一丝隐隐的不安开始在肯尼思心头蔓延。万一法官并不像他所以为的那样对有色人友善呢？从他嘴里漏出一句话，他们还没动手就会惹出大麻烦。他怀疑自己透露这么多计划，是否明智。当他看见法官脸上掠过一丝像是愠怒的神情时，他几乎确信自己做错了。

这位老律师终于清了清嗓子——这是他开口前的一贯前奏。可他一开口，谈的却是另一件事。

「你们那头的人对这帮三K党徒（Kluxers）怎么说？你们有没有人替这帮蠢货戴着枕套像一群该死的傻子似的到处招摇发愁？」

===Original===
“To tell you the truth, Judge, I don’t really know yet what the coloured people are thinking.” He felt that on this subject he could speak frankly to the judge, as he was too sensible a man to take much stock in the antics of the Klan. Yet, he was not too sure—coloured people must always keep a careful watch on their tongues when talking to white people in the South.

“You ain’t getting scared out there, are you?” the judge pressed the point.

“No, I wouldn’t call it scared. Most of those with whom I’ve talked don’t want any trouble with anybody—they want to attend to their own business and be let alone. But if they are attacked, I’m afraid there will be considerable trouble and somebody will get hurt.” He paused, then went on: “And that somebody won’t be entirely composed of Negroes, either.”

===Chinese===
「法官，跟您说句实话，有色人心里究竟怎么想，我自己也还没真正摸清楚。」他觉得这件事上可以跟法官坦率地谈，因为法官是个明白人，不会把三K党（the Klan）这番做派当回事。可他又不是太有把握——在南方，有色人和白人说话，总得把舌头看得紧紧的。

「你们在外头可别是被吓着了吧，嗯？」法官紧追着问。

「不，我不会说是被吓着了。和我谈过话的人，大多数都不想跟任何人闹事——他们想管好自己的事，图个清净。可要是他们被人欺负了，我怕麻烦可就不小，会有人受伤的。」他停了停，又接着说：「而且，受伤的人里头，不会全是黑人（Negroes）。」

===Original===
“I reckon you’re right, Ken. These fools don’t know they’re playing with dynamite.” His voice took on a querulous tone. “We’ve been getting along all right here, ’cept when some of these po’ whites out of the mill or from the tu’pentine camps or some bad nigras tank up on bad liquor or moonshine.” He did not say “Negro” nor yet the opprobrious “nigger,” but struck somewhere between the two—“nigra.” “And now these fools are just stirring up trouble Lord knows where it’ll end.”

He ran his hand through his hair—a favourite trick of his when excited, and paced up and down the room.

“I’ve been telling some of the boys they’d better stay away from that fool business of gallivanting around with a pillow-slip over their heads. They talk about being against bootleggers and men runing around with loose women—humph!—every blamed bootlegger and blind tiger and whoremaster in town rushed into the Klan ’cause they know’d that was the only way they could keep from getting called up on the carpet! A fine bunch they are!”

The judge spat disgustedly.

===Chinese===
「我想你说得对，肯。这帮蠢货不知道自己是在玩炸药。」他的声音带上了几分抱怨。「我们这儿一直处得还不错，除了有些从棉纺厂出来、或者从松节油营地（turpentine camps）出来的穷白人（po’ whites），或者一些不正经的尼格拉（nigra）[1]灌了一肚子劣酒或私酿（moonshine）闹事之外。」他既不说「黑人（Negro）」，也不说那不堪入耳的「黑鬼（nigger）」，而是取了个介于两者之间的说法——「尼格拉（nigra）」。「现在这帮蠢货偏偏又要挑事，老天才知道这事会闹到什么地步收场。」

他把手插进头发里——这是他一激动就爱做的小动作——在屋里踱来踱去。

「我一直跟几个小伙子说，叫他们离头上套着枕套到处招摇那套蠢把戏远着点。他们口口声声说自己反对私酒贩子、反对跟不正经女人鬼混的男人——哼！——城里每一个该死的私酒贩子、地下酒窑（blind tiger）、老鸨，一股脑儿全挤进了三K党，因为他们明白，唯有这样他们才不会被叫去问话！这可真是一伙好东西！」

法官嫌恶地啐了一口。

===Original===
“Now about this plan you got—have you thought about the chances of your being misunderstood? Suppose some of these ornery whites get it into their heads you’re trying to start trouble between the races. What’re you going to do then?” he asked.

“That’s just why we want to do the job right,” answered Kenneth. “We want to do everything legally so there can’t be any wrong ideas about the society. I know every time coloured people start forming any kind of an organization besides a church or a burial society, there are white people who begin to get suspicious and think that Negroes are organizing to start some mischief. That’s why we want you and the other good white people to know all about our plans from the start.”

“I ain’t trying to discourage you none,” replied Judge Stevenson doubtfully, “but do you think you are wise in starting coloured folks to thinking about organizing when this Klan’s raising hell all over the South?”

===Chinese===
「再说你这个计划——你有没有想过被人家误会的可能？万一这帮下作的白人脑子里钻了牛角尖，以为你想在两个种族之间挑事，到那时候你怎么办？」他问。

「这正是我们要把事情办得正的缘故，」肯尼思答道，「我们要样样都办得合法，省得人家对这合作社有半点歪念头。我知道，有色人只要一成立什么组织——除了教会或者丧葬互助会之外——总有些白人便起疑心，觉得黑人是要结伙捣鬼。正因如此，我们才要从头到尾让您和别的好心的白人了解我们计划的一切。」

「我不是想给你泼冷水，」史蒂文森法官满腹狐疑地答道，「可这会儿三K党正在整个南方闹翻了天，你觉得在这当口鼓动有色人去想组织的事，是明智的吗？」

===Original===
“How else are we going to do anything?” asked Kenneth. “Farmers have been robbed so long they are getting tired of it. If something isn’t done, there’s going to be lots more trouble than a society like ours can possibly cause. This share-cropping business causes more trouble than any other thing that’s done to Negroes. Lynching is mighty bad, but after all only a few Negroes are lynched a year, while thousands are robbed every year of their lives.”

“That’s so. That’s so,” agreed the judge, but the doubt had not been dispelled from his voice nor removed from his face. He removed his cigar from his mouth, viewed its mangled appearance through much chewing upon it, threw it with an expression of disgust out of the window, narrowly missing a man passing in the street below. He chuckled as he placed a fresh cigar in his mouth.

“’Taint no harm in trying, though,” he said, half to himself.

===Chinese===
「不然我们还能怎么办呢？」肯尼思问。「农户被人盘剥得太久了，他们也快受够了。要是再不想点办法，将来闹出来的乱子，会比我们这样一个合作社所能惹出来的大得多。这种分成制（share-cropping）的事，比对黑人施加的任何别的事都更能生事。私刑固然坏到了极点，可一年到头到底也只有少数几个黑人遭私刑，而每年被盘剥去一生血汗的，却是成千上万。」

「是这样。是这样，」法官应道，可那疑虑既没从他声音里消去，也没从他脸上消去。他把雪茄从嘴里取下来，端详着被嚼得一塌糊涂的惨状，一脸嫌恶地把它甩出窗外——险些砸中下面街上一个过路的人。他嘿嘿一笑，又把一支新雪茄塞进嘴里。

「不过，试试总没坏处，」他半自言自语地说。

===Original===
“Besides, our plan is to enlist the support of every white man in the county who stands for something,” went on Kenneth, eager to gain the old man as a staunch ally. “We know there’ll be opposition from some of the landlords and merchants and bankers who are making money off this system, but we figure there are enough decent white people here to help us through.…

“Mebbe so. Mebbe so,” replied the judge, though there was a distinct note of doubt in his voice now. “I wouldn’t be too sure, though. I wouldn’t be too sure.”

“But, Judge—” interrupted Kenneth. The judge silenced him with a movement of his hand.

===Chinese===
「况且，我们的计划是要争取县里每一个还讲点体面的白人的支持，」肯尼思热切地想拉拢这位老人做自己的靠山，便接着说，「我们知道，那些靠这套制度发财的东家、铺子老板、银行家一定会反对，可我们盘算，本地正派的白人到底还够多，能帮我们渡过去……」

「也许吧。也许吧，」法官应道，可此刻他声音里已带着明显的疑虑。「不过我可不敢打包票。我可不敢打包票。」

「可是，法官——」肯尼思插嘴。法官一摆手，止住了他。

===Original===
“Ken, have you ever thought out what a decent white man goes through with in a town like Central City? Have you thought what he has to put up with all over the South? There ain’t a whole lot of them, but just figure what’d happen to a white man today who tried to do anything about cleaning up this rotten state of affairs we got here. Why, he’d be run out of town, if he wasn’t lynched!”

“But, Judge,” began Kenneth again, “take lynching, for example. You know, and I know, and everybody in the South knows that if a Negro is arrested charged with criminal assault on a white woman, if he’s guilty, there isn’t one chance in a million of his going free. Why don’t they bring them to trial and execute them legally instead of hanging and burning them?”

===Chinese===
「肯，你可曾想过，在中央城这样的地方，一个想正派做人的白人要受些什么罪？你可曾想过，在整个南方，他要忍些什么？这种白人本就不多，可你想想，今天要是有个白人想出来收拾收拾我们这儿的这摊烂摊子，他会落得什么下场？哎呀，他不遭私刑，也得被赶出城去！」

「可是，法官，」肯尼思又开口道，「拿私刑来说吧。您知道，我也知道，南方每一个人都知道，一个黑人要是被抓起来，说他强奸了白人女人，他要是真有罪，想脱身的可能连百万分之一都没有。那他们为什么不把他送上法庭、依法处决，反而要把他吊死、烧死呢？」

===Original===
“Why? Why?” The judge repeated the interrogative as though it were a word he had never heard before. “You know, and so do I and all the rest of us here in the South, that nine out of ten cases where these trifling women holler and claim they been raped, they ain’t been no rape. They just got caught and they yelled rape to save their reputations. And they lynch the nigra to hush the matter up.”

Kenneth was amazed at the old man. Not amazed at what he said, for that is common knowledge in the South. He was astounded that even so liberal a man as the judge should frankly admit that which is denied in public but known to be true. He hesitated to press the inquiry further, and thought it expedient to shift the conversation away from such dangerous ground.

“Why don’t men like yourself speak out against the things you know are wrong, Judge?.”

===Chinese===
「为什么？为什么？」法官把这个疑问词重复了一遍，像是一辈子头一回听见它似的。「你明白，我也明白，我们南方这里所有人都明白：那些轻浮的女人大呼小叫、说自己被强奸了的案子，十件有九件根本没有什么强奸。她们不过是被人撞见了，便喊强奸，好保全自己的名声。于是他们便把那尼格拉（nigra）私刑处死，好把这事掩盖过去。」

肯尼思对这位老人惊讶不已。惊讶的不是他说出来的话，因为这在南方是尽人皆知的事。他惊讶的是，连法官这样开通的人，竟也坦白承认了一件公开场合人人否认、私底下却人人知道是真的事。他不愿把这一追问再深下去，觉得还是把话头从这样危险的地界岔开为妥。

「法官，像您这样的人，为什么不站出来，反对那些您明知是错的事呢？」

===Original===
“What would happen to us if we did? Count me out ’cause I’m so old I couldn’t do much. But take right here in Central City the men I’ve talked with just like I’m talking to you. How many of them could say what they really want to? I don’t mean on the race question. I mean on any question—religion, politics—oh—anything at all. Suppose Roy Ewing or any other white man here said he was tired of voting the Democratic ticket and was going to vote Republican or Socialist. Suppose he decided he didn’t believe in the Virgin Birth or that all bad folks were burned eternally in a lake of fire and brimstone after they died. If they didn’t think he was crazy, they’d stop trading with him and all the womenfolks would run from Roy’s wife and daughter like they had the smallpox. That’s the hell of it, Ken. These po’ white trash stopped everybody from talking against lynching nigras, and they’ve stopped us from talking about anything. And far’s I can see, things’re getting worse every day.”

===Chinese===
「我们要是说了，会落得什么下场？我呢，把我除外罢，我老了，做不了什么事了。可就拿中央城这里说，跟我这样像跟你说话一样谈过的那些人，有几个人能说他心里真正想说的话？我指的还不是种族问题。我指的是任何一个问题——宗教、政治——啊——什么都行。假如罗伊·尤因（Roy Ewing）或者这儿任何一个白人说，他投民主党（Democratic）的票投腻了，要改投共和党（Republican）或社会党（Socialist）；假如他表示他不信童贞女生子（Virgin Birth），或者不信所有坏人在死后都会在地狱的硫磺火湖（a lake of fire and brimstone）里永世受焚烧。他们要是不觉得他疯了，就会不再跟他做生意，而且女眷们都会像躲天花一样躲着罗伊的老婆和女儿。要命就要命在这儿，肯。这帮穷白人渣（po’ white trash）叫谁都不能再开口反对对尼格拉动私刑，又顺着这一路叫我们什么都不能再开口了。而且就我所见，情况还在一天比一天糟。」

===Original===
“Couldn’t you organize those white people who think like you do?” asked Kenneth.

“No, that ain’t much use either. It all goes back to the same root—self-interest—how much is it going to cost me? I tell you, Ken, the most tragic figure I know is the white man in the South who wants to be decent. This here system of lynching and covering up their lynching with lying has grown so big that any man who tries to tackle it is beat befo’ he starts. Specially in the little towns. Now in Atlanta there’s some folks can speak out and say most anything they please, but here—” The old lawyer threw out his hands in a gesture of hopelessness.

===Chinese===
「你们不能把那些和您想法相同的白人组织起来吗？」肯尼思问。

「不成，那也没多大用处。归根结底都落在同一根子上——利己心——这事得花我多少？我跟你说，肯，我所知道的最悲剧的人物，就是南方这想要正派做人的白人。这套私刑、再用谎言把私刑掩盖起来的制度，已经膨胀得如此之大，谁想动它一根毫毛，还没动手就先输了。尤其是小城小镇里。在亚特兰大那地方，还有那么一些人能出来说话，爱说什么就说什么，可这儿——」这位老律师两手一摊，一副无可奈何的样子。

===Original===
“Why can’t the South see where their course is leading them?” asked Kenneth. “Suppose there wasn’t a white man in the South who was interested in the Negro. Suppose every white man hated every Negro who lived. Why couldn’t they see even then that they are doing more harm to themselves than they could ever do to the Negro? With all its rich natural resources, with its fertile soil and its wonderful climate, the South is farther behind in civilization than any other part of the United States—or the world, for that matter. Aren’t they ever going to see how they’re hurting themselves by trying to keep the Negro down?”

“That’s just it,” replied the judge. “A man starts out practising cheating in a petty way, and before he knows it he’s crooked all the way through. He starts being mean part of the time, and soon he’s mean all over. Or he tries being kind and decent, and he turns out to be pretty decent. It’s just like a man drinking liquor—first thing he knows, he’s liable to be drunk all the time.”

===Chinese===
「南方为什么看不见他们这条路会把自己引到哪里去呢？」肯尼思问。「就算南方没有一个白人对黑人还有一分关心。就算每一个白人都恨每一个活着的黑人。可即便那样，他们怎么也看不见，他们对自己所造成的祸害，远比他们所能加诸黑人的更深呢？南方坐拥丰饶的自然资源、肥沃的土地、宜人的气候，可在文明程度上，却比美国任何其他地方——也比全世界任何其他地方——都更落后。难道他们永远看不见，他们想压住黑人，其实是在伤他们自己吗？」

「正是这话，」法官答道，「一个人起先只是小打小闹地学着骗人，不知不觉之间，他里里外外都已歪了。他起先只是有时使坏，没多久就坏到全身。反过来，他要是试着和善正派，到头来也真就差不到哪儿去。这跟一个人喝酒一样——一不留神，他可能就成天醉醺醺的了。」

===Original===
The judge shifted his cigar to a corner of his mouth and let fly a stream of tobacco juice from the other corner, every drop landing squarely in the box of sawdust some ten feet away. He went on:

“That’s just what’s the matter with the South. She’s been brutal and tricky and deceitful so long in trying to keep the nigras down, she couldn’t be decent if she tried. If acting like this was going to get them anywhere, there might be some reason in it all, but they’ve shut their eyes, they refuse to see that nigras like you ain’t going to be handled like yo’ daddy and folks like him were.”

“What are we going to do—what can we do?” asked Kenneth. Never had he suspected that even so fine a man as Judge Stevenson had thought things through as their conversation had indicated. He felt the situation was not entirely hopeless when men like the judge felt and talked as he did. Perhaps they were the leaven that would affect the lump of ignorance and viciousness that was the South.

===Chinese===
法官把雪茄挪到嘴的一角，从另一角喷出一股烟叶汁，每一滴都端端正正落在十英尺开外那只盛锯末的箱子里。他接着说：

「南方害的正是这个病。她为了把尼格拉（nigras）压下去，残暴、狡诈、欺瞒得太久了，想正派也正派不起来了。要是这样做到底能捞着什么，倒也还有几分道理可言，可他们偏偏闭上了眼，不肯看见像你这样的尼格拉，再也不会像你爹、像你爹那一辈人那样任他们摆布了。」

「我们该怎么办——我们能做什么呢？」肯尼思问。他从未想到，连史蒂文森法官这么好的人，竟已把事情想得像他们这场谈话所表明的那样透彻。他觉得，既然像法官这样的人也这样想、这样说，局势也就并非全然绝望。也许，他们正是那一点面酵，终将去影响南方那一整块愚昧与恶毒的死面。

===Original===
“What are we going to do?” echoed the elder man. “God knows—I don’t! Mebbe the lid will blow off some day—then there would be hell to pay! One thing’s going to help, and that’s nigras pulling up stakes and going North. When some of these white folks begin to see their fields going to seed, they’ll begin to realize how much they need the nigra—just like some of ’em are seeing already.”

“But are they seeing it in the right way?” asked Kenneth. “Instead of trying to make things better so Negroes are willing to stay in the South, they’re trying more oppressive methods than ever before. They’re beating up labour agents, charging them a thousand dollars for licences, lynching more Negroes, and robbing them more than ever.”

“Oh, they’ll be fools enough until the real pinch comes. Far’s I can see, instead of stopping nigras from going North, them things are hurrying them up. Wait till it hits their pocketbooks hard. Then the white people’ll get some sense.”

===Chinese===
「我们该怎么办？」老人跟着重复，「天知道——我可不知道！说不定哪天锅盖会被顶飞——到那时候就有大乱子好瞧了！有一桩事倒是要起作用的，那就是尼格拉卷铺盖往北走。等到这些白人有几个眼看着自家地里的庄稼荒了，他们才会开始明白他们多么离不开尼格拉（nigra）——就像他们当中有些人已经看出来了那样。」

「可他们看出来的是正路吗？」肯尼思问。「他们不去想办法把事情弄好些，让黑人乐意留在南方，反倒使出比从前更狠的压迫手段。他们殴打招工的人（labour agents），向他们索一千美元的执照费，私刑处死的黑人比以前更多，对他们的劫掠也比以前更甚。」

「噢，他们要犯傻，一直犯到真正被掐住的那一天。就我所见，这些手段非但拦不住尼格拉往北走，反倒催着他们快走。等到这事真砸了他们的钱袋，白人自然会明白过来。」

===Original===
“Let’s hope so,” was Kenneth’s rejoinder as he rose to go. “It’s been mighty comforting to talk like this with you, Judge. Things don’t seem so hopeless when we’ve got friends like you.”

“’Tain’t nothing. Nothing at all,” replied the judge. “Just like to talk with somebody’s got some sense. It’s a pity you’re coloured, Ken, you got too much sense to be a nigra.”

Kenneth laughed.

“From all we’ve been saying, a coloured man’s got to have some sense or else he’s in a mighty poor fix nowadays.”

He did not resent the old man’s remark, for he knew the judge could not understand that he was much more contented as a member of a race that was struggling upward than he would have been as one of that race that expended most of its time and thought and energy in exploiting and oppressing others. The judge followed him to the door promising to draw up the necessary legal documents for the cooperative society. When Kenneth broached the subject of payment, the old man waved his hand again in protest.

“Ain’t got long to live, so’s I got to do what little I can to help. ’Tain’t much I can do, but I’ll help all I can.”

===Chinese===
「但愿如此，」肯尼思一边起身告辞，一边应道，「能跟您这样谈一谈，真是莫大的安慰，法官。有您这样的朋友在，事情好像也没那么叫人绝望了。」

「这没什么。一点也没什么，」法官答道，「不过是喜欢跟个还有点头脑的人说说话罢了。可惜你是有色人，肯，你有这么多头脑，做尼格拉（nigra）太可惜了。」

肯尼思笑了。

「照我们方才说的这些来看，一个有色人要是不长点心眼，眼下这世道可就要吃大亏了。」

他并不怪老人这句话，因为他知道，法官不可能理解：他身为一个正奋力向上攀登的种族的一员，远比做一个把大半时间、心思和精力都花在剥削压迫他人之上的种族的一员，要心安理得得多。法官送他到门口，答应替合作社把必要的法律文书拟好。肯尼思提起付报酬的事，老人又是一摆手，连连推辞。

「我也活不了多久了，能做点小小的事帮衬帮衬，总得做。我能做的不多，可我一定尽力而为。」

===Original===
Thanking him, Kenneth started to leave, but the judge recalled him after he had reached the hallway. “Ken, just consider all I said as between us. Can’t tell what folks’d say if they knew I been running on like this.”

There was almost a note of pleading in his voice. Kenneth assured the judge their conversation would be treated as confidential. As he walked home, he reflected on the anomalous position the judge and men like him occupied, hemmed in, oppressed, afraid to call their souls their own, creatures of the Frankenstein monster their own people had created which seemed about to rise up and destroy its creators. No, he said to himself, he would much rather be a Negro with all his problems than be made a moral coward as the race problem had made the white people of the South.

===Chinese===
肯尼思道了谢，正要走，可他已走到过道里时，法官又把他叫住了。「肯，我方才说的这些，就当只是你我二人之间的话。要是让人知道我这样喋喋不休，谁知道人家会怎么说。」

他声音里几乎带上了一丝央求的味道。肯尼思向法官保证，这场谈话一定当作私话守密。他一路走回家，心里琢磨着法官以及像他那样的人所处的那个尴尬境地：被四面围住、被压迫、连自己的灵魂都不敢认作自己的——他们都是自己本族一手造出来的那个弗兰肯斯坦（Frankenstein）怪物的造物，而那怪物如今似乎就要抬起头来，反过来毁灭它的造物主了。不，他对自己说，他宁可做一个黑人，承受做黑人的所有难题，也不愿像种族问题那样，被弄得成了道德上的懦夫——它已把南方的白人弄成了这个样子。

===Original===
The judge stood at the window, dim with the dust of many months, and gazed at Kenneth’s broad back as he swung down Lee Street. Long after he had disappeared, the old man stood there, chewing on the cigar which by now was a mangled mass of wet tobacco. At last he turned away and resumed his seat in the comfortable old chair where Kenneth had found him. He shook his head slowly, doubtfully, and murmured, half to himself, half to the dusty, empty room:

“Hope this thing turns out all right. Hope he don’t get in no trouble. But even if he does, there’ll be more like him coming on—and they got too much sense to stand for what nigras been made to suffer. Lord, if we only had a few white folks who had some sense…”

It was almost a prayer.

===Chinese===
法官站在窗前——窗玻璃蒙了几个月的灰，已经昏暗不清——目送肯尼思宽阔的背影沿着李街大步流星地走远。在肯尼思消失之后许久，老人仍站在那里，嚼着那支雪茄——此时它已被嚼成一团湿漉漉、烂糟糟的烟叶。他终于转过身，回到肯尼思初来时他所坐的那张舒适旧椅上坐下。他缓缓地、满腹狐疑地摇着头，半自言自语、半对那落满灰尘的空屋子嘟囔：

「这事有个好结果就好了。盼他别惹上什么麻烦。可就算他真惹上了，后头还有更多像他这样的人要来——他们头脑够用，再也不会忍着尼格拉（nigras）受过的那些罪了。主啊，我们要是也有几个有头脑的白人就好了……」

这几乎是一句祷告。

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **nigra（尼格拉）**：美国南方白人对黑人的轻蔑读法，介于标准称谓「Negro（黑人）」与种族主义极端贬语「nigger（黑鬼）」之间。原文在此处特地点明——「他既不说 Negro，也不说那不堪入耳的 nigger，而是取了个介于两者之间的 nigra」。这一语音上的细微差别，是南方白人中那种「自以为不算太失礼、实则仍含轻蔑」的典型态度的写照。译文依项目策略以「尼格拉」音译保留其介于两者之间的语气，不软化其贬义，以存其历史与批判语境。法官作为本章中相对开明的白人角色，其使用此词，正折射出即便是较开通的南方白人，也无法摆脱那一套根深蒂固的种族轻蔑——这亦是怀特批判笔锋所指之一。

## XII / 十二

===Original===
From Judge Stevenson’s office Kenneth went directly to tell Jane of the interview. So absorbed was he in contemplation of the wider vision of the problem he was attacking which the judge’s words had given him, he forgot to telephone her to ask if it was agreeable for him to call at so unconventional an hour. He found her clad in a bungalow apron busily cleaning house and singing as she worked.

They sat on the steps of the back porch while he told her all that had been said. Taken out of his preoccupation with his own affairs, Kenneth had shaken off his negative air and now he talked convincingly of their plans. Jane said nothing until he had finished.

“That’s fine!” she exclaimed when he had ended. “Even if Judge Stevenson is doubtful of how much we can accomplish, we can do something. Now all that remains is for you to present your plan﻿—”

“Not mine, yours,” he corrected.

“No, it will have to be yours,” she answered. “You know how folks are in the South﻿—they think all that women can do is cook and keep house and bear children. If you want the thing to go, it’ll be best to make them think it’s your scheme.”

===Chinese===
从史蒂文森法官的办公室出来，肯尼思径直去找简，把这次会面告诉她。法官的一番话，使他对正在着手解决的这个问题有了一个更开阔的视野，他沉浸在这一思索里，竟忘了先打个电话给她，问问这么个不合常礼的钟点登门她方不方便。他到时，见她穿着一件家常的围裙罩衣，正忙着打扫屋子，一边干活一边唱歌。

他俩在后廊的台阶上坐下，他把人家说的那一切原原本本告诉了她。肯尼思从对自己那一摊事的牵挂里抽身出来，已甩掉了原先那股消极的气色，如今讲起他们的计划来，说得煞有说服力。简一言不发，直等他说完。

「好极了！」他话音一落她便叫起来。「哪怕史蒂文森法官对我们能办成多少心存疑虑，我们总能做点什么。眼下剩下的事，就是你把你的计划端出来——」

「不是我的，是你的，」他纠正她。

「不，非得是你的不可，」她答道。「你是知道南方人是什么做派的——他们觉着女人能做的，无非是做饭、持家、生孩子。你要这事办得成，顶好让他们以为这是你的主意。」

===Original===
Kenneth demurred, but in vain. She would have it no other way. She felt no jealousy. She knew of the peculiar Southern prejudice which relegated women to a position of eternal inferiority. Though she felt the injustice of such arbitrary assumptions, she did not resent it. Like all women, coloured women, she realized that most of the spirit of revolt against the wrongs inflicted on her race had been born in the breasts of coloured women. She knew, and in that knowledge was content, that most of the work of the churches and societies and other organizations which had done so much towards welding the Negro into a racial unit had been done by women. It was amusing to see men, vain creatures that they are, preen themselves on what they had done. It was not so amusing when they, in their pride, sought to belittle what the women had done and take all the credit to themselves. Oh, well, what did it matter? The end was the all-important thing﻿—not the means. Jane appreciated Kenneth’s thoughtfulness and felt no tinge of jealousy if her idea﻿—their idea﻿—should be a success in forming societies to help poor, helpless Negroes out of the morass in which they were bogged. Of such material has the coloured woman been made by adversity.

===Chinese===
肯尼思推让，却属徒劳。她不肯有第二种安排。她心里没有半点嫉妒。她知道南方那种特有的偏见，把女人打发到一个永远低人一等的位置上。她虽感到这种武断成见的不公，却并不因此怀恨。和所有女人一样，和所有有色人（coloured）女人一样，她明白：反抗本族所受种种冤屈的那股反抗精神，大多都诞生在有色人女人的胸膛里。她知道——也因这一知道而心安——那些教会、社团以及其他种种组织所做的事，那些在把黑人（Negro）锻造成一个种族共同体上起了莫大作用的事，大半都是女人做出来的。看着那些男人——他们原是些爱虚荣的造物——在自己所做的事上自鸣得意，倒也好笑。可等到他们因着那份骄傲，竟要贬低女人们所做的，把一切功劳都揽到自己身上，那便不那么好笑了。唉，这又有什么要紧？要紧的是结局——不是手段。简感念肯尼思的体贴，纵然她的主意——他们的主意——果然办成了，办成了这些社团、把那些穷苦无告的黑人从他们深陷的泥沼里搭救出来，她也不会有半点嫉妒之心。磨难，便是由这样的材料，把有色人女人造就出来的。

===Original===
She watched Kenneth as he told her the developments of which he had thought, the details he had worked out. Each day, it seemed to her, Kenneth became more keenly alive each day saw a brighter sparkle in his eyes, a springiness in his step that had not been there before. There are many men who could willingly have followed﻿—and do follow﻿—without revolt or much inward conflict a course of self-abnegation such as he had mapped out for himself. Not so, however, with Kenneth. He was almost puritanical in his devotion to the fixed moral code he had worked out for his own guidance. It was not a superimposed one, but an integral part of his very being. Nothing could have induced him to surrender to deliberate malice or guile or what he considered dishonesty or cowardice. His was a simple nature, free from the barnacles of pettiness which encumber the average man. He was not essentially religious in the accepted meaning of the word. He believed, though he had not thought much on the subject of religion, so immersed had he been in his beloved profession, in some sort of a God. Of what form or shape this being was, he did not know. He had more or less accepted the beliefs his environment had forced upon him. He doubted the malignity of the God described by most of the ministers he had heard. As a matter of fact, he was rather repelled and nauseated by the religion of the modern Church. Narrow, intolerant of contrary opinion, prying into the lives and affairs of its communicants with which it had no concern, its energies concentrated on raising money and not on saving souls, of little real help to intelligent people to enable them to live more useful lives here on earth, and centering instead on a mysterious and problematical life after death, he felt the Church of Jesus Christ had so little of the spirit of the Christ that he had little patience with it. He went to services more as a perfunctory duty than through any deep-rooted belief that he could get any real help from them in meeting the problems of life he faced. He bore the Church no grudge or ill will﻿—it simply was not a factor in the life of today as he saw it.

===Chinese===
她望着肯尼思，听他讲他所想到的进展，他所理出的细节。在她看来，肯尼思一天比一天活得更为热切，每一天都见他的眼里有一抹更亮的火花，脚步里有一股从前没有的轻快。世上有许多男人，本可以心甘情愿地追随——也确在追随——像他为自己规划的那样一条自我克制的路，既不反抗，内心也无多大挣扎。肯尼思却不是这样。他对自己所立、用以自率的那套固定道德准则，近乎清教徒般地恪守。那准则不是外加于他的，而是他整个存在里不可分的一部分。任凭什么也不能诱使他向蓄意的恶意与诡诈屈服，不能向他视为不诚实或怯懦之事屈服。他的天性简朴，没有那种附庸在寻常人身上的琐碎的藤壶。就「宗教」一词通行的意义而言，他本质上并不算虔诚。他沉浸于心爱的本行之中，对宗教这题目想得不多，但他相信冥冥中有那么一个上帝。这存在是何形何状，他并不知道。他多多少少接受了环境强加于他的那些信仰。他怀疑他所听过的大多数牧师所描绘的那个上帝，是否真怀着那种恶意。事实上，现代教会的宗教倒叫他相当反感、乃至作呕。狭隘，容不得半点异见，爱窥探那些与它毫不相干的信徒的私生活与私事，心思全用在募款上而不是救灵魂上，对于有心人在这世上过更有用的人生给不了多少实在的帮助，反倒把重心放在死后那缥缈而可疑的来生——他觉得耶稣基督的教会里，基督的精神实在所剩无几，他对它也就没什么耐心。他去做礼拜，更多的是尽一桩敷衍的本分，而非出于任何深信能从中得到真正的助益、去对付他所面对的人生难题。他对教会并不记恨、也不怀恶意——只是在他看来，它压根儿算不上今日生活里的一个因素。

===Original===
Nevertheless he had a deep religious or, better, an ethical sense. When he was about to return to Central City, that ethical code had been adapted to conditions he expected to find there. It was galling to him to accept a position of subserviency to things he knew were unjust and wrong, tacitly to admit his inferiority to men to whom he knew he was superior in morals and training and in all the decencies of life, solely because of the mere accident that they had been born with skins which were white and he with one which was not white. When doubts had assailed him, he had quieted or salved his conscience by the constant reminder that he was following such a course for greater eventual good. On his return, when he had found a course such as he had charted for himself was becoming increasingly difficult, he had refused to face the facts his mind told him were true and had plunged more deeply into his work, seeking in it an opiate. Only when Jane had confronted him with the utter futility of his course and had, in effect, accused him of being a moral quitter in considering only himself and blinding himself to the far greater problems of those so closely bound to him by race, did his eyes begin to be opened. Wearied of illusory hopes of peace through compromise, he had grasped the tangible reality of work towards a definite end, through means which he had created and which he would guide and develop as far as he could. With the buoyant hopes and ambitions of the young, especially of the very young, he felt that he had already created that which he was hoping to create.

===Chinese===
然而他有一种深沉的宗教感——或者更确切地说，一种伦理感。在他即将返回中央城之际，他把这套伦理准则调整得合乎他预料将在那儿遇到的境况。要他去屈居于他明知不公不当之事之下，要他心照不宣地承认自己低人一等——低那些他在道德、教养以及为人处世的种种体面上都明知胜过一筹的人一等——仅仅因为他们碰巧生了一身白皮肤、而他生了一身非白的皮肤，这件事让他深感屈辱。当疑虑袭来时，他便时时提醒自己：走这条路，是为了日后更大的善——以此安抚、宽慰自己的良心。回乡之后，当他发现自己原先所拟的那条路越来越难走时，他不愿正视他心里明知为真的事实，反倒更深地一头扎进工作里，要在其中寻一剂麻醉。直到简拿他这条路的全然无用来当面诘问他，实际上等于指责他是个道德上的逃兵——只顾自己、对那些因种族而与他紧密相连的人那远为沉重的难题视而不见——他的眼睛才开始睁开。他已厌倦了靠妥协换取和平的虚妄希望，转而抓住了一个切实可触的现实：朝着一个明确的目标做事，所用的法子是他自己创造、要由他自己尽可能去引导、去发展的。带着年轻人、尤其是年纪尚轻之人那股蓬勃的希望与抱负，他觉得自己已经把那想要创造的东西创造出来了。

===Original===
Like a traveller who has lost his way in a dense forest, an indefinable restlessness had pervaded his being and made him sorely discontented. Now that he had found what seemed the path which would lead him into the clear, open air, the clouds of doubt and perplexity were cleared away just as the bright sun, as it bursts forth after a shower in spring, drives away the moisture in the air.

They sat there in the warm sunlight of early summer, dreaming and planning all the great things they were going to accomplish. It had rained earlier in the morning and from the ground rose a misty vapour. The odour of warm wet earth mingled with the aroma of the flowers. Hens scratched industriously for food to feed the cluster of tiny chicks around them. A cat sneaking along the fence slyly crept near. With a great fluttering of wings and raucous cackling, the hens drove him away. From afar off came the voices of two women, resting for a minute from their morning toil, gossiping with much loud laughter. It was a peaceful, restful scene. To Kenneth as he sat there, problems seemed remote and out of place in that place where all was so calm.

===Chinese===
像一个在密林中迷了路的旅人，一种说不清的不安曾弥漫他全身，叫他满心苦闷。如今他似乎找到了那条能引他走到开阔明亮处的路，疑虑与困惑的阴云便一扫而空——正如春日一阵雨后，明媚的阳光骤然迸射，把空气里的潮气驱散殆尽。

他俩就那样坐在初夏温暖的阳光里，梦想着、筹划着他们将要成就的那一切大事。早上早些时下过一场雨，地里升起一层薄薄的雾气。温热湿润的泥土气息和着花香混在一处。母鸡们勤快地刨着食，喂身边那一窝小鸡雏。一只猫顺着篱笆鬼鬼祟祟地摸近前来。母鸡们一阵猛烈的扑翅、粗嘎的咯咯大叫，把它赶跑了。远远传来两个女人的说话声，她们正歇一口气、暂缓早晨的劳作，大着声说笑闲聊。这是一幅安宁、恬静的画面。肯尼思坐在那里，觉得一切难题都显得遥远，在这般沉静的所在显得格格不入。

===Original===
He looked at the girl by his side. It seemed Jane had never looked more charming clad in her bungalow apron, dustcloth in hand. He was glad she had made no silly, conventional excuses because of her dress. The usual girl would have tried to rush indoors and change her dress. Most women, he reflected, looked like angels at night, but in the harsh glare of morning looked terrible. Jane seemed to him to be even prettier without powder or the soft light of evening. He felt a thrill of pleasure as he saw her dusting furniture in their home.

They rose as Kenneth started to leave. Jane was telling him of some trivial incident, but Kenneth heard nothing of what she said. He turned towards her suddenly.

She divined his intentions﻿—she could almost feel the words that were on his lips. Quickly wishing him success in the meeting to be held that next evening, she bade him goodbye.

===Chinese===
他望着身旁的姑娘。穿着家常围裙罩衣、手里拿着抹布的简，似乎从没有这一刻这般迷人。他庆幸她没有因着自己这身打扮，作出什么俗套的傻里傻气的推托。寻常的姑娘总恨不得一头冲进屋去换身衣裳。他寻思，大多数女人夜里看着像天使，可一到早晨刺目的光里就显得可怕了。在他看来，简不施脂粉、不在傍晚柔光里，反倒更漂亮些。他看着她在他们的家里抹着家具，心头掠过一阵欢愉。

肯尼思起身要走，两人便都站了起来。简正跟他说着一桩什么琐碎的小事，可她说什么肯尼思一句也没听进去。他忽地朝她转过身去。

她猜到了他的心思——她几乎能感觉到他嘴边就要出口的那几个字。她赶忙祝他明晚的会开得顺利，便与他道了别。

===Original===
After Kenneth had gone, Jane sat for some time struggling with the problem she was facing. What was she to do? As a little girl she had loved Kenneth with a simple, childlike love though he, with the infinite difference of eight years of age, had paid no attention to her. She was not at all sure now of the nature of her feelings towards him. She liked him, it is true, but when it came to anything deeper than that, she was not so certain. She had been told, and had always believed, that love came as a blinding, searing, devastating passion which swept everything before it. She felt none of this passion and experienced no bit of that complete surrender which she had believed was a part of the thing called love. Jane was much in the position of the sinner on the mourner’s bench who had been told that when he became a Christian, angels and all sorts of heavenly apparitions would miraculously appear before him, and, seeing none, feels that he is being cheated.

Jane had seen in Kenneth’s eyes that soon he would make some sort of declaration of his love. What was she going to say? She did not know.﻿ ﻿…

So pleasant had it been sitting there in the warm sunlight talking with Jane, Kenneth had forgotten the time. Entering his office, he found half a dozen patients waiting somewhat impatiently for him. As he entered his private office, he heard old Mrs. Amos, in her chronic quarrelsomeness, mutter:

“Dat’s just what I allus say. Soon’s a nigger begin to get up in the world, he thinks hisself better’n us po’ folks. Thinks he can treat us any way he please.”

Kenneth laughed and, with a few bantering words, mollified the irascible old woman. The coloured doctor has to be a diplomat as well as a physician﻿—he must never allow the humblest of his patients to gain the impression that he thinks himself better than they. Of all races that make up the heterogenous populace of America, none is more self-critical than the Negro﻿—its often unjust and carping criticism of those who stand out from the mass serves as an excellent antidote for undue pride and conceit.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
肯尼思走后，简独自坐了许久，为她正面对的那个难题苦苦挣扎。她该怎么办？还是个小姑娘时，她曾以一种单纯的孩子气的爱恋爱过肯尼思，可那时的他，隔着八岁年纪的那道鸿沟，根本没把她放在心上。如今她对自己对他的那份感情究竟为何，全无把握。她喜欢他，这倒是真的，可要说比这更深的什么，她就拿不准了。她一向听人说、也一向信以为真：爱情来时，是一道灼人的、毁灭性的、令人目眩的狂热，要把眼前一切都席卷而尽。这种种狂热她一丝也没有体会到，那种彻底的缴械投降——她原以为是那叫作爱情的东西里应有的一部分——她也未曾经验过分毫。简这处境，倒很像是那个坐在慕道席上的罪人：人家告诉他，等他成了基督徒，天使和种种天上的异象便会奇迹般显现在他眼前，可他什么也看不见，便觉得自己是让人给骗了。

简从肯尼思眼里看出来，他不久就要对她作一番爱情的表白。她要怎么说呢？她不知道。……

坐在那温暖的阳光里和简说话，实在太惬意了，肯尼思把时间都忘了。一进诊所，他见已有半打病人在那里等候，等得颇有些不耐烦。他走进里间诊室时，听见好拌嘴成性的阿莫斯老太太嘟囔道：

「我就是这么说来着。一个黑鬼（nigger）[1]才刚要在世上发迹，就觉得自己比我们这些穷人强了。觉着可以随便打发我们了。」

肯尼思笑起来，几句打趣的话，便把这位火暴脾气的老太太哄住了。有色人的医生，既要当医生，又得当外交家——他绝不能让哪怕最卑微的病人得到一种印象，觉得他自视比他们高。构成美国这个杂色人群的诸多种族中，没有哪一个比黑人更爱自我批判的了——它对那些从众人里冒尖的人，那种往往有失公允、吹毛求疵的批评，倒正是一剂上好的解药，专治过分的骄傲与自负。……

===Original===
The next evening the seven men met again at Mr. Wilson’s. Kenneth stopped by for Mr. Phillips, but he did not see Jane. The Reverend Stewart, Tucker, Tracy, Swann, and Mr. Wilson sat awaiting them. Tom Tracy was exhibiting, somewhat proudly it seemed, a note he had found tacked to his door that morning. It was crudely lettered in red ink on a cheap-quality paper. It read:

**Nigger! You’ve ben talking too dam much! If you don’t shut your mouth we will shut it for you! And for good! Let this be a warning to you. Next time we will act!**

**K.K.K.**

Beneath the three initials was a crude skull and crossbones. Though all seven of the men knew that the warning was not to be disregarded, that it might possibly portend a serious attempt on the life of Tracy, that any or all of them present might receive a similar grim reminder of the ill will of the hooded band, there was a complete absence of fear as they sat around the table and conjectured as to the possible result of the warning. The calmness with which they accepted the omen of trouble would probably have amazed the senders of the warning. Perhaps the clearest indication of how little the South realizes the changes that have taken place in the Negro is this recrudescence of the Klan. Where stark terror followed in the wake of the Klan rides of the seventies, the net result of similar rides today is a more determined union of Negroes against all that the Klan stands for, tinctured with a mild amusement at the Klan’s grotesque antics. It was fortunate for Kenneth, in a measure, that Tracy had received the threat on the day it came. With such a reminder before them, the seven felt there was greater need than ever before for organization for mutual protection.

===Chinese===
第二天晚上，七个人又在威尔逊先生家碰头。肯尼思顺路去接菲利普斯先生，却没见到简。斯图尔特牧师、塔克、特雷西、斯旺，还有威尔逊先生，已坐着等他们。汤姆·特雷西正带着几分得意——看着像是得意——给大家看一张那天早上他发现钉在自家门上的字条。那是用红墨水在劣质纸上歪歪扭扭写的。上面写着：

**黑鬼（nigger）！你说得他妈太多了！你再不把嘴闭上，我们就替你闭上！叫它永远闭上！这一回算给你一个警告。下一回我们就要动手！**

**K.K.K.**

三个字母下面，画着一个粗陋的骷髅和交叉的骨头。七个人心里都清楚，这道警告不可等闲视之，它很可能预示着要对特雷西的性命下狠手，在座的任何一个、乃至全部，都可能收到一道类似的、来自那头戴兜帽的一伙人的阴森提醒。然而他们围着桌子坐下，揣测这道警告可能引来什么后果时，脸上却全无惧色。他们面对这凶兆时所显出的镇定，多半会让发出警告的人大吃一惊。也许，南方对于黑人所发生的变化是多么一无所知，最清楚的说明正是这三K党（Ku Klux Klan）的死灰复燃。七十年代三K党夜骑之后，紧跟着的是赤裸裸的恐惧；而如今类似夜骑的净结果，却是黑人们结成了更坚定的同盟、来反对三K党所代表的一切，其间只掺着一点对三K党滑稽行径的淡淡好笑。对肯尼思来说，在某种程度上也算是侥幸——特雷西正是在收到恐吓的当天把它带来的。有这样一个警醒摆在眼前，七个人都觉得，比起以往任何时候，如今更有必要组织起来，互为卫护。

===Original===
They discussed means of protecting Tracy, but he assured them he was amply able to take care of himself. He had sent his parents that day to stay with friends until the trouble had blown over, telling them nothing of the warning, as he did not want them to be worried by it. Two of his friends had agreed to stay with him at night. He was well supplied with ammunition and was sure the three of them could successfully repel any attack that might be made upon him. Such trying periods have happened to Negroes so frequently in the South that they have become inured to them. The subject was soon dropped.

Then Kenneth presented his plan. He outlined in detail how the society should be organized. He proposed that the first lodge be formed at Ashland, then gradually spread until there was a branch in every section of the county. They left until later the problem of extending the society’s activities to other parts of Georgia and the neighbouring States. Each member would be required to pay an initiation fee of one dollar. Men would pay monthly dues of fifty cents each, women twenty-five. The sums thus secured were to be pooled. Half of the amount was to purchase supplies like sugar, flour, shoes, clothing, fertilizer, seeds, farm implements, and the other things needed to satisfy the simple wants of the members. To make up any deficit, Kenneth and Mr. Phillips agreed to lend money that the supplies might be purchased for cash, effecting thereby a considerable saving. The other half was to be used as the nucleus of a defence fund with which a test case might be made in the courts when any member was unable to secure a fair settlement with his landlord.

===Chinese===
他们商量着怎样保护特雷西，他却向大家保证，自己完全有本事照料自己。他当天已把父母送去朋友家住，等这场风波过去再回来；那道警告他一点也没告诉他们，因为他不愿他们为此担惊受怕。他的两个朋友已答应夜里来陪他。他的弹药充足，深信他们三个人足以击退任何可能向他发动的袭击。这样的关口，对南方的黑人来说发生得实在太频繁了，他们早已习以为常。这话题很快便搁下了。

接着，肯尼思端出了他的计划。他详详细细地勾画出这个团体该如何组织。他提议，第一个分部先在阿什兰成立起来，再渐渐铺开，直到县里每一片地方都有一个支会。至于把这个团体的活动扩展到佐治亚其他地区及邻近各州的事，他们留待日后解决。每个成员须缴一元入会费。男会员每人每月缴月捐五角，女会员二角五分。这样收上来的款项要合在一处。其中一半用来采购物资——糖、面粉、鞋、衣物、肥料、种子、农具，以及会员们简朴所需的其他种种。若有亏空，由肯尼思和菲利普斯先生答应借钱填补，好让这些物资能用现金买进，借此省下一笔可观的花销。另一半则用作一笔辩护基金的底子，日后哪位会员与东家结算时不能得到公道，便可拿它到法庭上去打一个试验性的官司。

===Original===
Similarly were other details presented and discussed and adopted or modified. A name had to be chosen. Kenneth would have preferred a short, simple one, but here he was overruled. That it might appeal to the simple, illiterate class to which most of the prospective members belonged, a sonorous, impressive name was necessary. They decided on “The National Negro Farmers’ Cooperative and Protective League.”

At first the plan was considered a bit too ambitious, but as Kenneth warmed up to it in presenting it as simply and forcefully as he could, the objections, one by one, were overcome. One change, however, had to be made. It came from Hiram Tucker.

“Ain’t you figgerin’ on havin’ no signs and passwords and a grip like dey have with de Odd Fellers and de Masons and de Knights of Pythias?” he asked.

“I didn’t think that was necessary,” replied Kenneth.

“Well, lemme tell you somethin’, son.
