He tried to shake off the fear that seized him—fear of what would happen if it became known among the whites that Mary Ewing had died while a Negro was operating on her. No mortal could have done more. Even were that known and admitted, it would not save him, Kenneth knew.

The tense situation became too much for him. When he should have been steadiest, the double strain on his nerves caused his hand to slip. Blood spurted forth. Kenneth feverishly caught the bleeding artery with a haemostatic and sought to repair the damage he had done.

“Tough luck,” muttered Dr. Bennett. Kenneth looked up at him. The older man grunted and smiled encouragingly. A burden seemed lifted from Kenneth’s shoulders. Mrs. Johnson wiped the perspiration that streamed from Kenneth’s face. She seemed endowed with a sixth sense that told her his needs almost before he was aware of them himself.

It was a strange sight. Anywhere in America. In Georgia it was amazing beyond belief. A white woman patient. A white anaesthetizer. A black nurse. A black surgeon.…

===Chinese===
尽管竭尽全力，肯尼思知道玛丽在飞速地下沉。那失血与失力，那剧烈的休克，那阵阵地耗竭她元气的痉挛剧痛，已把她榨得一丝抵抗力都不剩。肯尼思知道贝内特医生也知道这一桩——纵在这绝望的搏斗里，他仍忍不住想，万一姑娘死了，那一位会说什么、做什么。他努力抖落那攫住他的恐惧——怕的是，万一白人之间传开，说玛丽·尤因是死在一个黑人给她动手术的当口，那要怎样。凡人能做的他都已做了。纵使这点被知道、被承认，也救不了他，肯尼思明白。

这绷紧的情势终于超出了他的承受。正该他最稳的时候，双重紧张压在神经上，使他的手一滑。血喷了出来。肯尼思拼命用止血钳夹住出血的动脉，设法补救自己所闯的祸。

「真不巧。」贝内特医生低声说。肯尼思抬头看他。老人哼了一声，鼓励地笑了笑。仿佛一副担子从肯尼思肩头卸下。约翰逊太太抹去从他脸上直淌的汗。她像天生有第六感，几乎在他自己察觉之前便知道他缺什么。

这是一幕奇景。放在美国任何一地都是。放在佐治亚则简直匪夷所思到不可信。一个白人女病人。一个白人麻醉师。一个黑人护士。一个黑人主刀。……

===Original===
All things must come to an end. Kenneth rapidly sewed up the incision. He bandaged the wound tightly. She yet breathed.

Kenneth opened the door and admitted Ewing, who had paced the hall since the operation began. Every minute of the hour he had been there, he had had to fight hard to keep himself from bursting into the room and stopping the operation. He had been restrained by the positiveness with which he had been ejected from the room by Kenneth—there was something in the physician’s air that had warned him without words that he must not interfere. Something within him told him Kenneth was right—knew what he was doing. The colour and race of the surgeon had been almost forgotten in the strange circumstances. “Will she live?” he asked, his words whispered in so hoarse a tone they could hardly be heard.

“I don’t know—it’ll be forty-eight hours before we can tell—if she lives that long,” answered Kenneth. The strain had been greater than he had known. Kenneth felt a strange weakening—lassitude gripped his body—he felt a nausea that came with the reaction after the mental ordeal. Ewing stood by the table on which lay his child. Tears which he forgot to wipe away stood in his eyes as he watched her laboured breathing. Dr. Bennett put his hand on Ewing’s shoulder.

“He did all he could!” he declared, nodding at Kenneth. There was admiration in the old doctor’s voice.

Ewing rushed off to give the news to his wife.…

The three men carried the unconscious form to her room. With a short “Good night” to Dr. Bennett, Kenneth left the house with Mrs. Johnson and drove away.…

===Chinese===
凡事总有终了之时。肯尼思飞快地把刀口缝合，紧紧缠上绷带。她还有呼吸。

肯尼思打开门，让尤因（Ewing）进来——自手术开始，他便一直在走廊里来回踱步。他在那儿守了整整一个钟头，每一分钟都得拼命克制自己，才没冲进屋里叫停手术。拦住他的，是肯尼思把他赶出门时那股不容置疑的气势——医生神色间有种无需言语便已昭然的东西，警告他绝不可插手。他心里有某种声音告诉他，肯尼思是对的，清楚自己在做什么。在这样奇异的情境下，主刀医生的肤色与种族，几乎已被抛到了脑后。「她能活下来吗？」他问，声音嘶哑得几乎听不见。

「我不知道——要等四十八小时才能见分晓——如果她能撑那么久的话。」肯尼思答道。这场紧张的程度超出了他自己的察觉。肯尼思感到一阵异样的虚脱——倦怠攫住了他的身子——精神熬战之后的反应化作了阵阵恶心。尤因站在孩子躺着的那张桌旁，望着她艰难的呼吸，眼泪夺眶而出，却忘了去擦。贝内特医生把手搭在尤因肩上。

「他已经竭尽全力了！」他说着，朝肯尼思点了点头。老医生的嗓音里透着赞许。

尤因奔出去给妻子报信去了……

三人把那个仍不省人事的小身子抬回她的房间。肯尼思向贝内特医生简短道了声「晚安」，便带着约翰逊太太离开这户人家，驱车而去……

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **Ed Stewart 埃德·斯图尔特**：本章登场人物，白人种植园主、汤姆·特雷西的东家，三K党徒、揭发「黑人阴谋」的发言者。须与第12章出现的黑人牧师斯图尔特（Reverend Stewart）严格区分——二人同名不同人、不同种族，本书有意以这一巧合凸显南方种族鸿沟。译文中以「埃德·斯图尔特」标注，黑人牧师作「斯图尔特牧师」。
>
> [2] **Exalted Cyclops 崇高独眼巨人**：三K党地方分部（Klavern）的首领职衔，名取自其种族主义神秘仪式。本章此职由鲍勃·帕克警长兼任，揭示三K党与南方白人执法权力结构的勾连。
>
> [3] **Invisible Empire 无形帝国**：三K党自封的国中之国称号，呼应其罩袍蒙面、秘密夜行的组织形式。
>
> [4] **Kligrapp 纪录官**：三K党地方分部的秘书职衔，本章此职由卫生委员亨利·莱恩兼任——卫生委员同时是三K党纪录官，照应本书所揭南方白人权力网络与三K党的渗透。

## XVI / 十六

===Original===
The following day Kenneth was kept busy arranging his affairs in order to leave the following morning for Atlanta for the operation on Mrs. Tucker. It had been a most difficult task for him to persuade her to have it done. He had been at last successful when he made her realize that it would mean either the operation or death. She dreaded the trip to Atlanta but Kenneth refused to perform the operation except at a hospital and there was none nearer than Atlanta at which a Negro could operate.

During the day he had been kept so busy that he had not had time to go out of the coloured section except once, and that when in the late afternoon he drove through Lee Street to see how Mary Ewing was faring. He had been so busy with his own thoughts that he had paid little attention to the whites who were standing around on the streets. He did not see the threatening and hostile looks they gave nor did he notice the excited whispering and muttering when he came into their sight.

===Chinese===
第二天，肯尼思忙着一桩桩安排妥当，好第二天一早动身去亚特兰大给塔克太太动手术。劝她答应做这手术，可是费了他九牛二虎之力。直到他让她明白——要么做手术，要么就没命了，他才终于说服了她。她怕去亚特兰大，可肯尼思不肯在医院以外的地方做这手术，而能让黑人主刀的医院，最近的也在亚特兰大。

这一整天他忙得脚不沾地，除了一趟之外，连有色人区都没出过——那是傍晚时分他驱车穿过李街，去看看玛丽·尤因怎么样了。他一头扎在自己的心事里，街上那些三五成群站着的白人，他几乎没怎么留意。他没有看见他们投来的那些恶狠狠、满是敌意的目光，也没有察觉他一露面，他们之间那压着嗓子的激动私语和嘟囔。

===Original===
Ed Stewart had partly told the truth at the meeting of the Klan when he said that Dr. Williams had informed him of the organization Kenneth and the others were forming. Kenneth had seen little of the pompous and intensely jealous physician since the time when he had forced Dr. Williams to assist him in the appendicitis operation on Mrs. Emma Bradley. Kenneth had felt nothing but an amused contempt for his fellow-practitioner, for he knew that Dr. Williams covered his deficiencies in medical knowledge and skill with the bombastic and self-important air he affected.

Dr. Williams, on the other hand, had never forgiven Kenneth for the incident in which Kenneth had shown him up in a manner that injured the former’s pride far more than Kenneth had suspected. Dr. Williams felt that the younger man had deliberately and with malice aforethought offered a gratuitous insult to him as dean of the coloured medical profession of Central City, though that profession numbered but two members. Kenneth’s success as a physician in Central City, having taken as he had some of the best of Dr. Williams’ own patients whom he had considered peculiarly his own, the insult plus Kenneth’s success had rankled in his breast until, being of a petty and mean disposition, he hated the younger man with a deep and vindictive hatred.

===Chinese===
埃德·斯图尔特在三K党的聚会上倒也算说了一半实话——他说威廉斯医生把肯尼思他们正在筹办的组织告诉了他。自从肯尼思硬逼着威廉斯医生给埃玛·布拉德利太太那场阑尾炎手术当助手之后，肯尼思就很少见到这位浮夸、嫉妒心极重的同行了。肯尼思对这位同行只有一种觉得好笑的蔑视，因为他知道威廉斯医生是用他那套装腔作势、自命不凡的神气，来遮掩自己在医术和本事上的欠缺。

而威廉斯医生那边，对那一回的事却始终耿耿于怀——肯尼思让他当众下不来台，伤他自尊之深，远过肯尼思自己所料。威廉斯医生觉得，这个后生是存心算计好了、当众来羞辱他这位中央城有色人医学界的「院长」——虽说这行当在全城统共也只有两个人。肯尼思在中央城行医又如此成功，把威廉斯医生原视为自家禁脔的几个最好的病人都拉了过来，这羞辱再加上这成功，在他胸口结成了无法化解的疙瘩，终于，他本就心胸狭隘、刻薄成性，对这后生恨之入骨，恨得咬牙切齿、不共戴天。

===Original===
He had not, however, intended that his conversation with Ed Stewart should assume the proportions that it eventually did. On the day before the meeting of the Klan at which Kenneth had been named as the one responsible for the organization of the Negroes, Dr. Williams had met Ed Stewart driving out along a country road near Ashland. Williams was returning from making a professional call in that neighbourhood. Stewart, a big, rawboned, and lanky “Cracker” or “Peck,” as they are called by Negroes in the South, was going to inspect the cotton crops of his tenant-farmers, that he might estimate how big the crops would be and might know accordingly how large the tenants’ bills should be for supplies furnished.

They had stopped to pass the time of day and for Stewart to find which of the Negroes on his place was sick. He wanted to know if that sick one was too sick to work the crop, as the loss of even one worker during cotton-picking time was serious, what with the number of Negroes who had gone North. Having gained the information, he started to question Dr. Williams in a way that he thought was exceedingly adroit and clever, but through which ruse the coloured doctor saw instantly and clearly.

===Chinese===
不过，他当初和埃德·斯图尔特那一番话，本无意闹到后来那么大的动静。在三K党那场把肯尼思指作「煽动黑人结社的首犯」的聚会前一天，威廉斯医生在阿什兰附近一条乡间路上遇见了埃德·斯图尔特驾车迎面而来。威廉斯刚在那附近出诊回来。斯图尔特是个大块头、骨节粗大、瘦长条子的「穷白佬（Cracker）」——或者照南方黑人的叫法，「佩克佬（Peck）」[1]——正要去查看他那些佃农地里的棉花，好估算今年的收成能有多大，也好照着这个，定下佃户们该为赊欠的农用物资背多大的账。

两人停下车寒暄几句，斯图尔特顺便打听他地头上哪个黑人病了。他要知道那病的人是不是病得太重、下不了地，因为摘棉花的时节，哪怕少一个劳力都是了不得的事——何况已有那么多黑人往北方跑了。问清楚这些之后，他便开始盘问威廉斯医生，自以为这一手极其巧妙机灵，可这点点障眼法，有色人医生一眼就看穿了。

===Original===
“Say, Doc, you know anything ’bout these niggers ’round here holdin’ these meetin’s nearly ev’ry night? Seems t’ me it’s mighty late for them to be holdin’ revival services and indo’ camp-meetin’s?” he queried in as casual a tone as he could manage.

An idea sprang full-grown to Williams’ mind. Kenneth Harper was getting far too popular through the organization of his cooperative societies. Williams was shrewd enough to see that if they were as successful as they gave promise of being, Kenneth would be the leading Negro of the town, if not of that entire section of Georgia. And correspondingly he, Williams, would become less and less the prominent figure he had been before Kenneth had come back from France to Central City. That was it! Stewart was one of the biggest planters in Smith County. It was also rumoured he was prominent in the Ku Klux Klan. Stewart’s fortunes would be the hardest hit in the county if Kenneth’s societies achieved their purpose, for he, Stewart, had as many sharecroppers and tenant-farmers as any other man in the county if not more. Stewart also had the reputation, a long-standing one, of being the hardest taskmaster on his Negro tenants in the county﻿—the one who profited most through juggled accounts and fraudulent dealings. He could have cut, had he chosen, five notches in the handle of his gun, each one signifying a Negro who had dared to dispute the justness of settlements for crops raised.

===Chinese===
「我说，大夫，这一带这些黑鬼（nigger）几乎天天晚上开会，你知不知道是怎么回事？依我看，这会儿搞什么奋兴布道、室内营帐聚会，节令未免太晚了吧？」他尽量装出随口的口气问。

一个念头在威廉斯脑子里一下子成形了。肯尼思·哈珀靠着他那些合作社，人气正旺得过头。威廉斯精明得很，看得出这些组织一旦真像势头所预示的那样成了事，肯尼思就会是全城头一号的黑人，弄不好还是整个佐治亚这一片头一号的黑人。而相应地，他威廉斯，在肯尼思从法国回到中央城之前那种举足轻重的地位，便会一日不如一日。正是这样！斯图尔特是史密斯县数一数二的大种植园主，还有传言说他在三K党里也是个要人。肯尼思的合作社若真能成事，全县受打击最重的便是斯图尔特——因为他手下的分成制佃农和租地农户，不比县里任何人少，弄不好还最多。斯图尔特还有一桩由来已久的名声：全县数他对黑人佃户逼得最凶、最狠——也数他靠做假账、耍骗术从佃户身上榨得最多。他若是愿意，本可以在自己那把枪的枪柄上刻下五道缺口，每一道都记着一个胆敢跟他争庄稼账公道、结果丢了命的黑人。

===Original===
All these thoughts raced through Williams’ brain while Stewart waited for a reply to his questions. Williams had no intention of the exaggeration of his statements which Stewart later made. He merely intended that by telling Stewart of the societies, Kenneth’s rapidly increasing prominence in the community should receive a check through obstacles which Stewart and his fellow-landlords might put﻿—in fact, were sure to put﻿—in the way of success of the farmers’ organizations.

“No, sir, they ain’t holdin’ revivals, Mr. Stewart. I reckon if you white folks knew what was goin’ on, you wouldn’t feel so comfortable.”

Williams was playing with Stewart as is done so often by Negroes in the South with the whites, though the latter, in their supreme confidence that they belong to an eternally ordained “superior race,” seldom realize how often and how easily they are taken in by Negroes. Williams enjoyed the look of concern that had come to Stewart’s face at his words.

===Chinese===
这些念头在斯图尔特等他答话的当口，一连串地从威廉斯脑中掠过。威廉斯并无心把话说成斯图尔特后来所夸大的那个样子。他不过是想：把合作社的事告诉斯图尔特，让斯图尔特和他那些地主同伙——必然会——给农户组织使绊子，好叫肯尼思这日见高涨的声势受一受挫罢了。

「不是，先生，他们不是搞奋兴布道，斯图尔特先生。我瞧呀，要是你们白人知道他们在搞什么名堂，你们可就睡不安稳了。」

威廉斯是在耍着斯图尔特玩——南方黑人这样耍白人，本是家常便饭，只是白人既笃信自己天生属于一个永恒钦定的「优越种族（superior race）」，便很少意识到自己被黑人糊弄得多频繁、多轻易。威廉斯瞧着斯图尔特听了这话脸上浮起的那份关切，心里很是受用。

===Original===
“What’s goin’ on, Doc?” he asked in an eager tone, from which he tried with but little success to keep the anxiety that he felt.

“Heh, heh, heh!” laughed Williams in a throaty chuckle. “These Negroes are figurin’ on takin’ some of these landlords to court that’s been cheatin’ them on their crops. Of course,” he added hastily, “that don’t need to worry you none, Mr. Stewart, but from what I hear, there are some ’round here that the news will worry.”

Stewart flushed, for he was conscious of a vague feeling that Williams might have been indirectly hitting at him when he had said that the court proceedings wouldn’t affect him. He fell back on the old custom of flattering and praising fulsomely the Negro from whom a white man wants information regarding the activities of other Negroes. Williams, like every other Negro in the South, knew what value to put on it, but he was playing a far deeper game than Stewart suspected.

===Chinese===
「他们搞什么名堂，大夫？」他急切地问，竭力想压住心里的那份焦灼，却压得并不成功。

「嘿嘿嘿！」威廉斯用喉头的笑声咯咯地笑。「这些黑人正盘算着，要把那几个在庄稼账上做手脚坑他们的东家告上法庭呢。当然，」他赶紧补了一句，「这犯不着您操心，斯图尔特先生，可听人说，这一带有些人听了这消息，是要操心的。」

斯图尔特脸一红，因为他隐隐觉得，威廉斯说那官司牵连不到他，话里说不定是拐着弯在点他。他便退回到那套老办法上——一个白人想从某个黑人口里套出旁的黑人的动静，便对这黑人百般奉承、肉麻吹捧。威廉斯和南方任何一个黑人一样，清楚这一套值几个钱，可他这盘棋下得比斯图尔特所猜疑的要深得多。

===Original===
“Doc, why ain’t all these niggers good, sensible ones like you? If all the niggers in the South were like you, there never would be any trouble.”

“That’s right, Mr. Stewart, that’s right. As I was sayin’ to some of the folks out your way this mornin’, they’d better stop followin’ after the fool ideas of these coloured men who’ve been up No’th.”

He looked at Stewart shrewdly and appraisingly to see if he had penetrated the subtlety of his remark. Stewart, slow of thought, had not fully done so, it seemed. Williams continued:

“You see, it’s like this, Mr. Stewart. Folks like you and me could live here for a hundred years and there’d never be no trouble. There’d never be no race problem if they was only like us. But”—and his voice took on a doubtful and sorrowful sound—“the most of this trouble we’re havin’ is caused by fool Negroes who go up No’th to school and run around with those coloured folks in New York and Chicago who tell ’em how bad we po’ coloured folks are bein’ treated in the South. They get all filled up with ‘social equality’ ideas, and then they come back down here and talk that stuff to these ignorant Negroes and get them all stirred up—”

===Chinese===
「大夫，怎么这帮黑鬼就不能都像你这样，又乖觉、又懂事呢？要南方的黑人都像你这样，就一辈子也不会闹出什么事来。」

「这话不假，斯图尔特先生，不假。今儿早上我还跟你那边几个人说来着，叫他们别再跟那些上过北边（No’th）[2]的有色人屁股后头，学那些个糊涂主意。」

他狡黠地、品量似地打量斯图尔特，看他有没有听出自己这话里的弯弯绕。斯图尔特脑筋慢，似乎没全咂摸出味儿来。威廉斯接着说：

「您瞧，事情是这样的，斯图尔特先生。像您跟我这样的人，在这儿住上一百年，也绝不会闹出什么事来。要都像咱们这样，就根本不会有什么种族问题。可是」——他的声音带上了几分犹疑、几分痛心——「咱们眼下这些乱子，大半都是那些上北方去念书的糊涂黑人闹出来的。他们跟纽约、芝加哥那些有色人厮混，那些人就告诉他们，说咱们南方这些穷苦有色人受的待遇有多坏。他们一脑门子灌满了『社会平等』的念头，回到下边来，又把这些话去对这些无知的黑人讲，把他们全煽动起来——」

===Original===
Stewart was seeing more clearly what Williams was driving at.

“I see,” he said reflectively. “I alw’ys said too much education sp’iled niggers—that is, some niggers,” he added hastily for fear Williams might take offence before he had done with him. “Co’se it don’t bother sensible ones like you, Doc.” The last was said conciliatingly. “Let’s see, mos’ this trouble’s stahted since that other doctor’s been back, ain’t it?” he asked as casually as he could.

“I ain’t sayin’ who’s doin’ it,” replied Williams as he started the engine of his car. “But you’re a good guesser, Mr. Stewart,” he threw back over his shoulder as he drove away.…

===Chinese===
斯图尔特渐渐看清了威廉斯在暗示什么。

「我明白了，」他若有所思地说。「我常说，书读多了就把黑人宠坏（sp’iled）了——呃，是说有些黑人，」他生怕还没把威廉斯套够、对方就恼了，赶忙补了一句。「当然啦，像你这样懂事的人是不受影响的，大夫。」最后这句是安抚的口气。「我想想，这些乱子多半是打那位另一个医生回来之后才起的，是吧？」他尽量装作漫不经心地问。

「我可没说是谁干的，」威廉斯一边答，一边发动了汽车。「不过您可真会猜，斯图尔特先生，」他一边开车离去，一边回头丢下这一句。……

===Original===
Stewart clucked to his horse and rode in deep thought down the road. His mind was busy devising schemes to circumvent the action of the societies to take into court men like himself who had been robbing Negroes. They’d lose in the local courts, he knew, but suppose they raised enough money to take a case to the United States Supreme Court. No, that would never do! He’d see Parker and talk it over with him right away! He put the whip to his horse and drove rapidly into town. Mustn’t let the damn niggers organize, that would be hell!…

===Chinese===
斯图尔特嘬嘴驱马，一路沉思着沿大路走去。他满脑子盘算着法子，要怎样挫败那些合作社把像他这样劫掠黑人的东家告上法庭的图谋。他知道，这些案子在本地法庭是打不赢的，可万一他们凑足了钱，把官司一路打到美国最高法院（United States Supreme Court）呢？不行，那万万使不得！他得立马去找帕克，把这事跟他商量商量！他扬起马鞭，飞车般赶进城去。绝不能让那帮该死的黑鬼结起社来，那可就要了命了！……

===Original===
Kenneth was going about his business on the day following the meeting of the Klan that had been caused by Dr. Williams’ talk with Stewart, in blissful ignorance of the storm rapidly gathering about his head. His mind was intent on a number of things—but trouble on account of the cooperative societies was furthest from his mind. Had he been told there was any trouble, such news would probably have been greeted with a laugh of unconcern. All the white people of the South weren’t scoundrels and thieves like Stewart and Taylor and their kind! They were but a few. Besides the poor whites, the majority of whites would undoubtedly heartily approve his plan when it had been developed to the point where it could be made public.

===Chinese===
三K党那场聚会，原是威廉斯跟斯图尔特那一席话惹出来的；聚会过后的第二天，肯尼思照旧忙着自己的营生，浑然不觉一场风暴正飞速地朝自己头顶上聚拢。他心思扑在好些事情上——可因合作社而起的麻烦，是他最不会去想的一桩。纵使有人告诉他出了麻烦，他大概也只会一笑置之。南方的白人，并不都是斯图尔特、泰勒那一伙的无赖和窃贼！那不过是少数。除了那些穷白人，大多数白人，等他的计划一旦铺开、可以公之于众，无疑会打心底里赞成。

===Original===
But Kenneth thought of none of those things. His mind was too full of other events that loomed on the horizon. First, of course, he thought of Jane. He thought of his great good fortune in knowing a girl like her. There was a girl for you! He thought of the home he would build for her—he was mighty glad his father had been in fairly comfortable circumstances and that he had been successful in his practice. He would be able to build a mighty nice home for Jane. They wouldn’t bother with the cheap and flashy furniture, fumed oak or mission, to be obtained in Central City. Oh, no! Soon’s Mrs. Phillips was better, the three of them would go to Atlanta and buy everything they needed there. They’d have the best-looking home in Central City, white or coloured! His mother and Mamie wanted him to bring Jane into the house. He might do that… but the house which had seemed so comfortable before, now seemed too ordinary to bring a girl like Jane to.… He’d talk that over with her tonight.… And then after a time there might be a little Jane… and a Kenneth, Jr.… Kenneth laughed softly to himself as he saw Jane and himself sitting by the fire of an evening with two little rascals playing on the floor.… And later they’d go off to school. He’d see that they got the best there was in life.… So his thoughts ran.

===Chinese===
可这些事肯尼思一件也没去想。他心头装满了别的事，一桩桩在天边浮现。头一桩，自然是想简。他想到自己能结识简这样的姑娘，是何等的大福气。这才叫姑娘呢！他想自己要给她置一个家——他很庆幸父亲当年家境还算殷实，而他自己开业以来也算顺遂。他能给简置一个挺像样的家。他们才不稀罕中央城能买到的那种俗气花哨的便宜货家具，什么烟熏橡木的、什么「传教会」式的。哦，才不！等菲利普斯太太身子好些，他们三人就上亚特兰大，把要用的东西一股脑在那儿置齐。他们要拥有中央城最漂亮的一个家，不论白人还是有色人！他母亲和玛米想让他把简接到家里来住。这倒也未尝不可……只是这房子原先觉得挺舒坦，如今却显得太平凡，配不上把简这样的姑娘娶进来。……今晚得跟她商量商量这事。……再过些时候，说不定会有一个小简……还会有一个小肯尼思……肯尼思想着自己和简夜里坐在炉边，两个小淘气在地板上玩耍，不由得自己轻声笑了。……再后来，他们要离家去念书。他决意要让他们得到人生最好的东西。……他的念头就这样一程一程地跑下去。

===Original===
And then he thought of Roy Ewing and the operation of the night before. Must have been a mighty terrible ordeal for them to have to call a Negro in to operate on their daughter. Race prejudice is a funny thing! A white man will eat food prepared by black hands, have it served by black hands, have his children nursed by a black nurse who most of the time was more a mother to them than their own mother, let his clothes be taken into a black home to be washed, allow all the most intimate details of his life to be handled by black folks.… Even lots of them would consort with black women at night to whom they wouldn’t raise their hats in the daytime.… But when it came to recognizing a Negro outside of menial service, then there came the rub.… Yet in a matter of life and death like Ewing’s case, they forgot prejudice.… Maybe in time the race problem would be solved just like that… when some great event would wipe away the artificial lines… as in France.… He thought of the terrible days and nights in the Argonne.… He remembered the night he had seen a wounded black soldier and a wounded white Southern one, drink from the same canteen.… They didn’t think about colour in those times.… Wouldn’t the South be a happy place if this vile prejudice didn’t exist?… He wondered why folks didn’t see it as clearly as he did.…

===Chinese===
接着他又想起罗伊·尤因，想起昨夜那场手术。要他们不得不请一个黑人给自己的女儿主刀，那滋味想必是难堪到了极点。种族偏见这东西真是怪！一个白人肯吃一双手做的饭、肯让这双手端上来伺候他，肯让自己的孩子由一个黑人奶妈带——这奶妈多半比孩子亲妈还像个母亲，肯让自己的衣裳被抱进一个黑人家里去洗，肯让自己生活里最隐微的种种都由黑人来经手。……他们里有好多人，夜里跟黑人女人厮混的那些，白天见了是连帽子都不屑一抬的。……可一旦要在佣仆伺候之外承认一个黑人的地位，别扭就来了。……然而在尤因家这样生死攸关的关头，他们就把偏见忘了。……也许种族问题迟早也会这样自然而然地了结……等某桩大事把这道人为的界线一抹而去……就像在法国那样。……他想起阿尔贡那些可怕的白昼和黑夜。……他记得那一夜，他亲眼看见一个受了伤的黑人士兵和一个受了伤的南方白人士兵，同饮一壶里的水。……那种时候，他们是不计较肤色的。……要是没有这恶浊的偏见，南方该是个多么快活的地方啊？……他不明白，为什么别人不能像他一样看得这么清楚。……

===Original===
At last the long, busy day ended. He went over to have supper with Jane. That dress she had on the night they had told each other of their love, that reddish-coloured one, that had been a beauty. But tonight—ah, the other one wasn’t nearly so pretty! It was of white, simply made. Satin slippers, silk stockings, also of white. Her hair piled high and pierced with a large tortoiseshell comb. Always she brought pictures to Kenneth’s mind. Tonight it was again of the dark-eyed, seductive Spanish señorita on a balcony. After supper, they sat in the canvas porch swing. They talked of their plans—impetuously, enthusiastically—with all the glorious dreams of youthful love. All the little things—little, but so great when one is young and in love they said to each other. The things they said when the Pyramids were being built. The things they will say a thousand years from now.

===Chinese===
这漫长而忙碌的一天，终于过去了。他过去陪简吃晚饭。那天夜里他们互诉衷肠时她穿的那件衣裳，那件带点红褐色的，真是美极了。可今夜——啊，今夜这一件却远没有那件那么艳丽！这是一袭白裙，剪裁素净。缎子便鞋，丝袜，也都是白的。她头发高高盘起，插着一把大玳瑁梳。她总是这样，给肯尼思唤来一幅幅画面。今夜，他又想起那个阳台上的、黑眼睛、风姿撩人的西班牙姑娘（señorita）。吃过晚饭，他们坐在门廊那架帆布吊椅上。他们说着一桩桩计划——又急切，又热烈——带着少年人恋爱的全部光辉梦想。全是些小事——小，可人年轻、又在恋爱的时候，又是何等的大——他们这样对彼此说。这些话，是造金字塔的年代就已经说过的。这些话，是一千年以后还要说的。

===Original===
Tonight there were no warning signals from Mr. Phillips when ten o’clock came. He had been glad, and had said so, when Kenneth asked him for Jane. “We don’t feel we’re losing a daughter—we’re gaining a son instead!” he had said.

They talked on until there was no other sign of life discernible in the neighbourhood, save for the passage of a prowling cat, or the sound of the crickets in the grass. At last he had to go. Early the next morning he was to leave for Atlanta with Mrs. Tucker. Three days he was to be gone. He would return on Friday.

===Chinese===
今夜十点钟一到，菲利普斯先生那边没有发出任何逐客的信号。肯尼思向他求娶简的时候，他是高兴的，并且把这份高兴说出了口。「我们不觉得是少了个女儿——倒是多了个儿子呢！」他是这么说的。

他们一直说着话，直到这一带再看不见别的人间烟火，只有一只四处游荡的猫从旁经过，或是草丛里蟋蟀的叫声。到底他还是得走了。第二天一早，他就要陪塔克太太动身去亚特兰大。这一去要三天。他星期五回来。

===Original===
In October they were to be married. Mrs. Phillips’ health was not improving as they had hoped. She was cheerful but she wanted Jane to be happily married before she died. They had decided to live at his house with his mother and Mamie. They’d refurnish it and do over all the rooms. Later on, when he had made lots of money, they’d build.

Mamie and Jane and Kenneth were to go to Atlanta the latter part of September, there to buy the furniture and all the other things, they would need. Mrs. Phillips was too ill to stand the strain of the long journey and the excitement of the shopping.

===Chinese===
他们定在十月成婚。菲利普斯太太的病并没有照他们所盼望的那样好转。她精神还好，却想在自己死前看到简幸福地嫁出去。他们已经商定，婚后住在肯尼思家里，跟他母亲和玛米一起。他们要把那房子重新布置过，所有房间都翻新一遍。将来他挣了大钱，再盖新的。

玛米、简和肯尼思打算九月下旬一同去亚特兰大，在那儿把家具和所有用得着的物事都买齐。菲利普斯太太病得太重，经不起长途跋涉的劳顿和采买的兴奋。

===Original===
Jane tiptoed into the house so as not to wake her mother. She returned in a few minutes with a fluffy white mass in her arms. It was her wedding-gown which she was to make herself. They sat silent for a minute at the token of what it meant.

Tears stood in Jane’s eyes when he went down the stairs. He saw them when he looked back to say the last soft goodbye.

“Three days is an awful long time,” she said plaintively.

Of course, there was nothing else for him to do but go back up the steps and kiss her goodbye all over again.…

===Chinese===
简踮着脚走进屋里，免得吵醒母亲。过了几分钟她出来，怀里抱着一团毛茸茸的白色物事。那是她的婚纱，要由她自己亲手缝制。面对着这象征，两人默默坐了一会儿。

他下楼时，简眼里含着泪。他回头说最后一句温柔的道别时，看见了那泪。

「三天，太久了。」她幽幽地说。

自然，他再没有别的办法，只能折回台阶上去，把她重新吻别一遍。……

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **Peck（佩克佬）**：南方黑人对贫苦白人的又一蔑称，与「Cracker（穷白佬）」义近而更轻贱；本书中由叙述者点出斯图尔特这类南方种植园主兼三K党徒的阶级与种族底色。译本首现附原文，存其历史语境。
>
> [2] **No’th（北边）**：南方白人方言对「North（北方）」的读法，带轻蔑口吻；威廉斯此处刻意模仿白人话头，把自己伪装成「跟白人一条心、也瞧不起上北方念书的黑人」的「乖觉黑人」，正是南方黑人在白人面前惯用的自保伪装。

## XVII / 十七

===Original===
Bob was packing for his journey to Cambridge, whistling cheerfully the while. It was certainly great to be going away up to Boston to school. All his life he had wanted to live there for a while where he could learn the things which he knew of only at second hand now. He pictured in his mind how he would arrange his life at school. There’d be none of the kiddish pranks he had read about that college boys did. He was too old for that. He had seen too much of the seamy and sordid side of life to waste his time playing. He’d study every minute he could. He’d make a record in scholarship that would make his mother and Mamie and Kenneth proud of him. He’d go to summer school so as to finish the rest of his college course in two years instead of three. And then, law school. By jiminy, he’d be the best lawyer there was! Not the best coloured lawyer. The best lawyer! Never did youth have more brilliant dreams of life than Bob. He paused at the sound which came from downstairs through the half-opened door. It couldn’t be in Ken’s office, for he had gone to Atlanta with Mrs. Tucker that morning. It sounded like crying—as one would cry who had suffered some great bereavement or terrible misfortune. He went out in the hall and leaned over the balustrade, the better to find out what was the matter.

===Chinese===
鲍勃正在为去坎布里奇（Cambridge）的旅程收拾行李，一边快活地吹着口哨。能离开这儿到波士顿去念书，实在再好不过。他这一辈子都想在那儿住上一阵子，在那里能学到那些他如今只能间接听说的事。他在脑子里描绘自己上学后的生活要怎样安排。他读过的那些关于大学生淘气恶作剧的事，他一概不会沾。他年纪太大了，不来那一套。他见过的那生活的阴暗肮脏的一面太多了，哪肯把光阴浪费在玩耍上。他要一有空就念书。他要在学业上创出个成绩来，让母亲、玛米和肯尼思都为他骄傲。他要去上暑期学校，好把剩下的大学课程用两年而不是三年读完。然后，是法学院。老天作证，他要当最好的律师！不是最好的有色人（coloured）律师。是最好的律师！少年人再也没有比鲍勃更辉煌的人生梦想了。他停下手，听着从楼下透过半掩的房门传上来的声响。不会是在肯的诊室里，因为肯今早陪塔克太太去亚特兰大了。那声响像是在哭——像一个人遭了什么大丧或可怕的不幸时那样的哭。他走到门厅，探身伏在栏杆上，好弄清楚到底出了什么事。

===Original===
It was Mamie and his mother. He looked puzzled, for he could think of nothing to make Mamie cry that way. His mother was trying to soothe and calm her as Mamie told her the cause of her weeping. Bob crept down the stairs as softly as he could to hear.

Mamie between sobs was telling her mother of some accident that had befallen her.

“I had been—to Ewing’s Store and that Jim Archer—and Charley Allen—and two or three other white boys—that hang around Ewing’s Store—said nasty things to me when I came out—I hurried home they must have followed me.”

Here she broke down again while her mother crooned softly to her, pleading with her not to cry so hard. Mamie choked back her sobs and went on. Bob’s face became terrible to see. He hung there on the steps almost breathless, waiting, and dreading what he felt was coming.

“At that old field-near the railroad—they jumped out—and grabbed me oh, my God! My God! Why didn’t they kill me? Why didn’t they kill me?” Mamie’s screams were horrible to hear. “Then—oh, God! God help me!”

For a minute Bob stood there as one frozen to the spot. Then a blind, unreasoning fury filled him. He ran up the stairs to Kenneth’s room and got the revolver he knew Kenneth kept there. Without hat or coat he ran down the stairs. Out the door and down the street. Mamie and her mother were roused by his action. Mamie, lying on the floor with her head in her mother’s lap, her clothes torn and bloody, her face and body bruised, struggled to her feet. She ran to the open door through which Bob had disappeared. An even greater terror, if such was possible, was on her face.

===Chinese===
是玛米和母亲。他一脸困惑，因为他想不出有什么事能让玛米哭成那样。母亲正一边安慰她、劝她镇定，一边听她说出哭泣的缘由。鲍勃蹑手蹑脚地尽量不出声地下楼，要听个究竟。

玛米抽抽搭搭地，告诉母亲自己遭了什么变故。

「我刚才——去了尤因百货商行，那个吉姆·阿切尔——还有查利·艾伦（Charley Allen）——还有两三个别的白人小子——总在尤因百货商行门口晃荡的——我出来的时候他们冲我说下流话——我赶紧往家跑，他们一定是跟了上来。」

说到这里她又说不下去了，母亲柔声哄着她，求她别哭得那么凶。玛米强忍住抽泣，接着往下说。鲍勃的脸变得惨不忍睹。他僵立在楼梯上，几乎屏住了呼吸，等着，又害怕着他预感到即将到来的那句话。

「在那片老荒地——靠近铁路的地方——他们窜出来——抓住我，噢，我的天！我的天！他们为什么不杀了我？他们为什么不杀了我？」玛米的尖叫刺耳得叫人不忍听。「然后——噢，天哪！天主救我！」

有一分钟，鲍勃像被钉在了原地。随后一种盲目的、丧失理智的狂怒填满了他的胸膛。他奔上楼冲进肯尼思的房间，取出他知道肯尼思放在那里的左轮手枪。他没戴帽子，也没穿外套，就冲下楼梯。出了门，顺着街飞奔。玛米和母亲被他这一举惊起。玛米原本瘫在地上，头枕在母亲膝上，衣裳撕破，沾着血，脸上和身上满是青紫伤痕，这时挣扎着爬了起来。她跑到那扇鲍勃已消失的门边。她脸上，如果还能更恐惧的话，是一层更大的恐惧。

===Original===
“Bob! Bob! Come back! Come back!” she cried in ever louder cries.

“Bob! Bob!”

But Bob was too far away to hear her.

===Chinese===
「鲍勃！鲍勃！你回来！回来！」她一声比一声更响地喊。

「鲍勃！鲍勃！」

可鲍勃已经跑得太远，听不见她了。

---

===Original===
In front of Ewing’s Store there sat a group of nine or ten men and boys. They were gathered around one who seemed to be relating a highly interesting and humorous story. Every few minutes there’d be a loud laugh and a slapping of each other on the back. Suddenly, silence. A hatless and coatless figure was running down the street toward them. The group opened as its members started to scatter. In the middle of it there stood Jim Archer and Charley Allen. The former had been telling the story.

Bob walked straight up to Jim Archer, whose face had turned even paler than its usual pasty colour. He turned to run but it was too late. Without saying a word, his eyes burning with a deadly hatred, Bob raised the revolver he had in his hand and fired once—twice—into Archer’s breast. Charley Allen rushed upon Bob to overpower him. He met head-on the two bullets that came to meet him, and fell gasping and coughing on the ground at Bob’s feet.

The rest of the crowd had fled.

Without hurrying, Bob stepped into a Ford delivery truck that had been left at the curb, its engine running. Before the crowd which with miraculous suddenness filled the street could stop him, he drove straight down Lee Street, turned into Oglethorpe Avenue, and headed for the country beyond the town.…

===Chinese===
尤因百货商行门前坐着八九个十个男人和小子。他们围着一个人，那人似乎在讲一个极有趣、极好笑的故事。每隔几分钟就爆出一阵大笑，互相拍着后背。忽然，静了。一个没戴帽子、没穿外套的人影正顺着街朝他们飞奔过来。那一伙人散开，各人四下里躲。当中站着吉姆·阿切尔和查利·艾伦。先前讲故事的正是前者。

鲍勃径直走到吉姆·阿切尔跟前，阿切尔的脸比平时那副灰白相还要白上几分。他转身要逃，可已经来不及了。鲍勃一言不发，眼里烧着一种要置人于死地的恨，举起手里的左轮手枪，朝阿切尔的胸口开了一枪——两枪。查利·艾伦朝鲍勃扑上来，要夺他的枪。迎面两颗子弹正撞上他，他倒气喘着、呛咳着，摔在鲍勃脚下。

其余的人早已作鸟兽散。

鲍勃不慌不忙地钻进一辆停在路边的福特送货卡车，引擎还点着。街上奇迹般一下子挤满了人，还没等他们来得及拦住他，他便顺着李街直驶下去，转上奥格尔索普大道，朝城外乡间的方向去了……

===Original===
Three miles out of town the Ford spluttered, coughed, shook mightily, and stopped. Its gasolene tank was empty. Shoving it into the underbrush on the side of the road, far enough to be out of sight, Bob ran on. If he could only get across country as far as the railroad going North, he might be able to get to Macon, where he could hide. When the excitement died down, he could go on farther North. Perhaps he could eventually reach Canada. He fought his way through brushes, across vast fields of cotton that seemed to have no end. Near midnight he could go no farther. He had eaten nothing since breakfast—he had been too excited over his packing to eat any dinner. Bitterly he thought of the change a few hours had brought forth. Twelve hours before, he had been eagerly planning to leave for school. Now, his sister ruined, he a murderer twice over—fleeing for his life! He hoped that he had killed both of them! It would be too ironical a fate for them to live.… He thought for a moment of what would happen if they caught him. He put the thought away from him. God, that was too terrible! Mustn’t think of that! I’ll lose my nerve.…

===Chinese===
出城三英里，福特车噼啪了两声、咳嗽着、剧烈地抖了一阵，停了下来。油箱空了。鲍勃把它推进路边的灌木丛里，推到从路上看不见的地方，又接着跑。只要他能穿过乡野，跑到那条北上的铁路，他也许就能到梅肯，在那里躲起来。等风头过了，他再往更北的地方走。说不定，他最终能到加拿大。他披荆斩棘，穿过一片片仿佛没有尽头的棉田。将近午夜，他再也走不动了。他从早饭起就没吃过一口东西——收拾行李时太兴奋，午饭一口没吃。他痛苦地想着几个钟头里所带来的一切变化。十二个钟头前，他还在热切地计划着动身去上学。如今，妹妹毁了，他成了杀过两个人的凶手——正在逃命！他但愿那两个人都被他杀了！叫他们活着，那命运也太讽刺了。……他有一瞬间想起，要是他们逮住他，会怎样。他把这念头推开。老天，那太可怕了！不能想这个！我会泄了那股劲的……

===Original===
What was that? Lord, he must have fallen asleep! What is that? Dogs? Bloodhounds! Great God!

I must get away! How did they get away from bloodhounds in books? That was it! Water!

He’d find a stream and wade in it. Then the damned dogs would lose the scent.

The thought of water reminded him suddenly that he was thirsty—terribly thirsty. God, but his throat was dry! Felt like ten thousand hot needles were sticking in it!

His legs and thighs ached. He dragged them along like a paralysed man. He thought petulantly of a paralysed man he had seen once in Atlanta. What was his name? Bill? No, that wasn’t it. Jim? No, not that either. Some sort of a name like that.

Wonder how Mamie was? Mamie? Who’s Mamie? What had happened to her? He racked his brain to remember. At last he gave it up. No use trying. Old—old—brain don’t work right.

Wonder what’s the matter with it?

===Chinese===
那是什么？天哪，他刚才一定睡着了！那是什么？狗？寻血猎犬！我的老天！

我得逃！书里头人家是怎么甩掉寻血猎犬的来着？对了！水！

他要找一条小溪，蹚着水走。那样那该死的狗就闻不着味儿了。

一想到水，他猛地觉得自己渴了——渴得要命。老天，他嗓子干得要冒烟！像是有一万根烧红的针扎在里头！

他的双腿和大腿酸痛得要命。他像一个瘫痪的人那样拖着两条腿往前挪。他烦躁地想起从前在亚特兰大见过的一个瘫痪的人。那人叫什么来着？比尔？不对，不是。吉姆？也不是。一个差不多的名字。

玛米怎么样了？玛米？谁是玛米？她出了什么事？他绞尽脑汁去想。末了他放弃了。想也没用。老了——老了——脑子不管用了。

不知道这是怎么回事？

===Original===
His delirious brain was suddenly cleared by an ominous baying close at hand. Those damned dogs again. They’d never take him alive! He felt in his pockets to see if the gun was still there. It was. He felt in the other pocket to count the cartridges there while he ran. One—two—three—four—five—six—seven—eight! All there! Seven for the mob! One—for—Bob!

An old barn suddenly loomed up before him in the rapidly approaching light of dawn. He dragged himself into it and barred the door. Not much protection! But—a little! Just a little! Better’n none! He sat down on an old box by the door, There was a knothole farther over. He dragged the box in front of it. Reloaded the revolver. One—two—three—four cartridges! Two that hadn’t been used! That left six in the gun! And four more! Listen! The dogs sound like they’re near!

===Chinese===
他昏乱的脑子忽然被近处一声不祥的犬吠声震得清醒过来。又是那帮该死的狗。他们休想活捉他！他伸手摸摸口袋，看枪还在不在。在。他一边跑，一边伸手到另一个口袋里数子弹。一——二——三——四——五——六——七——八！都在！七颗给那帮暴民！一颗——给——鲍勃！

一座旧谷仓在迅速逼近的晨曦里忽然耸立在他面前。他拖着身子钻进去，把门闩上。挡不了多少！可是——能挡一点！就挡一点点！总比没有强！他在门边一只旧木箱上坐下，那边再过去一点有个节疤孔。他把木箱拖到孔前。给左轮手枪重新装弹。一——二——三——四颗子弹！两颗没用过的！枪里还剩六颗！再加上这四颗！听！狗好像近了！

===Original===
There they are! He wouldn’t waste his precious bullets on dogs! Oh, no! He’d save them for the human dogs! God damn ’em! He’d show ’em a “damned nigger” knew how to die! Like a man! Here they come! God, but it was tough to have to die! Just when life seemed so sweet! Wonder who’d sit in his seat at Harvard! Hope a coloured boy’d get it! Harvard seemed so far away from where he was! Looked like it was as far’s the moon! Might as well be for him!

Look at ’em spreading out! Whyn’t they come up like men and get him? There’s Jim Archer’s brother! Bang! Got him! Look at ’im squirm!

That’s two Archers won’t run after coloured girls any more! Bang! Damn it, I missed ’im! Can’t waste ’em like that! Got to be more careful! Must take better aim next time! Bang! Bang! Hell, I missed again! Nope! Got one of ’em!

One—two—three—four gone! Six left! Five for the “Crackers”! One for me! Bang! Bang!

===Chinese===
他们来了！他不会把宝贵的子弹浪费在狗身上！哦，才不呢！他要留着给那两条腿的狗！该死的！他要给他们看看，一个「该死的黑鬼（nigger）」也懂得怎样去死！像条汉子那样去死！他们过来了！老天，可要送命真不是滋味啊！偏偏在这日子正甜的时候！不知道谁会去坐他在哈佛的那个位子！但愿是个有色人小子坐上！哈佛离他这儿好像远极了！看着就跟月亮一样远！对他来说，也就跟月亮一样远了吧！

瞧他们散开了！他们干嘛不像汉子一样上来抓他？那是吉姆·阿切尔的兄弟！砰！打中了！瞧他扭的！

这是第二个阿切尔，再也不会追有色人姑娘了！砰！妈的，没打中！不能再这样浪费了！得当心点！下次得瞄准了再打！砰！砰！见鬼，又没打中！不对！打中一个！

一——二——三——四个倒了！还剩六颗！五颗给那些「穷白佬（Crackers）」！一颗给我自己！砰！砰！

===Original===
Got another! Must reload! One—two—three four! Nearly all gone! Five—ten—fifteen minutes to live! Why did they pick on Mamie?

Whyn’t they take one of those girls that live in those houses on Butler Street? That’s always running around after men? Why’d they bother a nice girl like Mamie?

Bang! Listen at ’im howl? That’s music for you! Listen to the damn “Peck” squalling!

What’s th’ matter? Looks like they’ve gone! Wonder if I can make a run for it? Th’ damn cowards! Fifty—one hundred—a thousand—five thousand—to one! That’s the way “Crackers” always fight coloured folks! Never heard yet of one “Cracker” fighting one Negro! Have to have thousan’ to kill one little fellow like Bob Harper!

===Chinese===
又打中一个！得再装弹！一——二——三——四！差不多全用光了！还能活五——十——十五分钟！他们干嘛偏要挑上玛米？

他们干嘛不去挑巴特勒街那些房子里住的姑娘？那些成天围着男人转的姑娘？干嘛要来糟蹋玛米这样一个本分姑娘？

砰！听他嚎的？这才是好音乐呢！听那该死的「穷白佬（Peck）」[1]嚎得多响！

怎么回事？好像他们撤了！不知道我能不能冲出去跑一阵？这帮该死的孬种！五十个——一百个——一千个——五千个——对付一个！「穷白佬」打起有色人来向来都是这个打法！从没听说过哪一个「穷白佬」单挑一个黑人（Negro）的！总得凑上几千个，才能弄死鲍勃·哈珀这么一个小个子！

===Original===
Smoke? Can’t be smoke! Yes, it is! Goin’ t’ burn me up! Bang! Bang! Got one of ’em!

My God! Only one bullet left! Never take him alive! Lynch him! Might burn him! Burned coloured boy last month ’n Texas! Better not let ’em get him! Goodbye, everybody! Goodbye!

Goodbye! Good—Bang……

===Chinese===
烟？不会是烟吧！是，真是烟！要把我烧死了！砰！砰！打中一个！

我的天！只剩一颗子弹了！绝不能让他们活捉！要私刑（lynch）他！说不定要烧他！上个月在得克萨斯就烧死过一个有色人小伙子！不能让他们逮住！永别了，大伙儿！永别了！

永别了！再——砰……

===Original===
It was some time after Bob had died before the posse dared enter the barn which by this time was burning rapidly. They feared the cessation of firing was only a ruse to draw them into the open. At last, after riddling the burning structure with bullets, a few of the more daring cautiously approached the barn, entered, and found Bob’s body. After the bullet from his own gun had entered his head, killing him instantly, his body had fallen backwards from the box on which he had been sitting. His legs were resting on the box, his thighs vertical, his body on the floor and his head slightly tilted forward as it rested against a cow-stall. His arms were widespread. The empty revolver lay some ten feet away, where he had flung it as he fell backwards. His face was peaceful. On it was a sardonic smile as though he laughed in death at cheating the howling pack of the satisfaction of killing him.

The mob dragged the body hastily into the open. The roof of the old barn was about to fall in. Before dragging it forth, they had taken no chances. A hundred shots were fired into the dead body. Partly in anger at being cheated of the joy of killing him themselves. They tied it to the rear axle of a Ford. Howling, shouting gleefully, the voice of the pack after the kill, they drove rapidly back to town, the dead body, riddled and torn, bumping grotesquely over the holes in the road.…

===Chinese===
鲍勃死后又过了好一阵，追捕队（posse）[2]才敢进那座这时已烧得正旺的谷仓。他们怕那枪声的停歇不过是个诱他们露面的圈套。末了，他们先朝那燃烧的屋架扫了一通子弹，几个胆大的才小心翼翼地走近谷仓，进去，找到了鲍勃的尸体。他自己那把枪的子弹射入他的头颅，使他当场毙命之后，他的身子从他坐着的木箱上向后仰倒下去。他的两条腿搭在箱子上，大腿竖直，身子躺在地上，头微微前倾，靠在一个牛栏的隔板上。他的双臂大大地张开着。那把空了的手枪落在大约十英尺开外，是他向后倒下时甩出去的。他脸上很安详。嘴角挂着一丝讥诮的笑，仿佛他在死去的一刻，嘲笑着那一群嚎叫的人——笑他们到底没能亲手弄死他、没能尝到那份快意。

暴民们急急忙忙把尸体拖到露天里。旧谷仓的屋顶眼看就要塌下来。在把尸体拖出来之前，他们可没敢冒任何险。他们朝那具尸体又打了一百枪。其中一部分，是出气——出他们没能亲手杀掉他的那口恶气。他们把尸体拴在一辆福特车的后轴上。他们嚎着，兴高采烈地叫着，那正是狼群噬杀猎物之后的声音，他们飞速开车回城，那具千疮百孔、残破不堪的尸体，在路上的坑洼间古怪荒诞地一路颠簸着……

===Original===
Back to the public square. In the open space before the Confederate Monument, wood and excelsior had been piled. Near by stood cans of kerosene. On the crude pyre they threw the body. Saturated it and the wood with oil. A match applied. In the early morning sunlight the fire leaped higher and higher. Mingled with the flames and smoke the exulting cries of those who had done their duty—they had avenged and upheld white civilization.…

The flames died down. Women, tiny boys and girls, old men and young stood by, a strange light on their faces. They sniffed eagerly the odour of burning human flesh which was becoming more and more faint.

… Into the dying flames darted a boy of twelve. Out he came, laughing hoarsely, triumphantly exhibiting a charred bone he had secured, blackened and crisp.… Another rushed in.… Another.… Another.… Here a rib.… There an armbone.… A louder cry.… The skull.… Good boy! Johnny!… We’ll put that on the mantelpiece at home.… Five dollars for it, Johnny!… Nothin’ doin’!… Goin’ to keep it myself!…

The show ended. The crowd dispersed. Home to breakfast.

===Chinese===
回到了公共广场。邦联纪念碑前的空地上，早已堆起了木柴和木刨花。旁边立着一桶桶煤油。他们把尸体抛上那座简陋的火葬堆。把尸身和木柴都浇透了油。一根火柴点上去。在清晨的阳光里，火苗蹿得越来越高。和火焰与浓烟交织在一起的，是那些尽了自己本分的人得意的呼喊——他们报了仇，他们捍卫了白人的文明……

火势熄了下去。女人、小小的男孩女孩、老人和年轻人，都站在一旁，脸上映着一种奇异的光。他们贪婪地嗅着那人肉烧焦的气味，那气味正越来越淡。

……一个十二岁的男孩冲进将熄的火堆。他跑了出来，沙哑地笑着，得意地举起他抢到的一根烧焦的骨头，黑乎乎、脆生生的。……又一个冲了进去。……又一个。……又一个。……这儿是一根肋骨。……那儿是一截臂骨。……一声更响的欢呼。……头骨。……好小子！约翰尼！……我们要把它供在家里壁炉台上。……五块钱卖不卖，约翰尼！……不卖！……我要自己留着！……

表演结束。人群散去。各自回家吃早饭去了。

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **Peck（穷白佬）**：本章为鲍勃在谷仓负隅、向围攻的白人暴民开枪时口中的蔑称，是 peckerwood 的简称。peckerwood 本是啄木鸟的方言读法，为南方白人对自己的「雅化」自称，后被黑人反转借用，作为对穷白人的轻蔑称呼（与 Cracker 同义）。此处鲍勃用以痛斥围攻的白人，译作「穷白佬」并保留原文，呼应本书种族用语分级处理策略。
>
> [2] **posse（追捕队）**：美国南方由警长临时召集、追捕逃犯的平民武装队。本章中这支追捕队实为白人暴民借合法之名行私刑之实，是 1920 年代南方种族恐怖的典型装置。

## XVIII / 十八

===Original===
Three men sat around a table that evening in the office of Sheriff Parker in the courthouse. The sheriff was one. Another was Commissioner Henry Lane. The third was Ed Stewart.

The latter was talking.

“Yep, after I talked to that nigger Williams, I rustled ’round among the niggers on my place. At fust, they wouldn’t talk much. But I found a way to make ’em! By God, a taste of a horsewhip’ll make any of ’em open up! Found they’s only two niggers we got to worry ’bout. One’s this nigger doctor. The other’s my nigger Tom Tracy. She’ff, if you hear’n tell of an accident out to my place in the nex’ few days, you needn’t bother to come out to investigate. It’ll be se’f-defence. Tom Tracy’s goin’t come up on me with an open knife. I’m goin’ t’ shoot t’ save my life.”

The three laughed at the good joke. The sheriff agreed not to bother. “Good riddance!” he commented.

===Chinese===
那天傍晚，法院里帕克警长（Sheriff Parker）的办公室里，三个人围着一张桌子坐着。警长是一个。另一个是委员亨利·莱恩（Henry Lane）。第三个是埃德·斯图尔特（Ed Stewart）。

正在说话的是后一位。

「没错，我跟那个黑鬼威廉斯谈过之后，又在我那块地上的黑鬼里头四下摸排了一番。起先他们话不多。可我找着了法子让他们开口！老天爷，抽上几马鞭，他们谁都开得了口！查出来了，咱们犯愁的黑鬼统共只有两个。一个是这个黑鬼医生。另一个是我的黑鬼汤姆·特雷西（Tom Tracy）。警长，往后这几天里，要是你听见说我那地方出了桩意外，你不用费事跑出来查。那会是正当防卫。汤姆·特雷西拿着拉开刀子的折刀扑上我，我开枪是为了保命。」

三个人为这妙主意笑了起来。警长答应不去管。「死得好！」他点评道。

===Original===
Stewart went on:

“Now ’bout this other nigger. He’s the brains of the whole thing. But we’ve got to be mighty careful, ’cause these other niggers thinks he some sort of a tin god. Ef they think he’s bumped off ’cause of these lodges he’s been organizing, they might raise hell. Ev’ry nigger out my way would go through hell ’n’ high water for him. Never seen ’em think so much of another nigger befo’. Mos’ the time they’ll come and tell me ev’rythin’ that any them other niggers doin’. This nigger Harper’s got ’em hoodooed or somethin’.”

The sheriff broke into Stewart’s monologue in a complaining, reminiscent fashion:

“Don’t know what’s gettin’ into the niggers nowadays. They ain’t like they useter be. Take this nigger’s daddy, f’r example. Old man Harper was as good a nigger’s I ever seen. If he met you on the street twenty times a day, he’d take off his hat ’n’ bow almos’ to the groun’ ev’ry time. But these new niggers, I can’t make heads nor tails of ’em. Take that uppity nigger they burned this mornin’. Always goin’ ’round with a face on ’im like he’s swallowed a mess of crabapples. What if that Jim Archer did have a little fun with the nigger’s sister? ’Twon’t hurt a nigger wench none. Oughter be proud a white man wants her.”

===Chinese===
斯图尔特接着说：

「再说另一个黑鬼。整个这事都是他在出主意。可咱们得格外小心，因为别的那些黑鬼把他当一尊锡菩萨供着似的。要是他们觉得他是为了组织这帮互助会的事给干掉的，他们会闹翻天。我那一片的每一个黑鬼都肯为他赴汤蹈火。从没见他们这么看得起过哪一个黑鬼。多半时候，他们一有风吹草动就会跑来，把别的黑鬼干的什么都告诉我。这个黑鬼哈珀像是给他们施了什么迷魂药。」

警长带着几分埋怨、几分追忆的口吻，打断了斯图尔特这番独白：

「不晓得如今这些黑鬼中了什么邪。他们不像从前了。拿这个黑鬼的爹来说。老哈珀是我见过的顶听话的黑鬼（Negro）[1]了。一天里在街上碰见你二十回，他每回都脱下帽子，腰几乎要弯到地。可这些新派黑鬼，我实在摸不着头脑。就说今天早上他们烧掉的那个不知天高地厚的黑鬼。成天沉着一张脸，像吞了一肚子野山楂。那个吉姆·阿切尔（Jim Archer）不过是跟他妹妹开了点小玩笑，又怎么了？糟蹋不到一个黑鬼娘们（nigger wench）[2]身上。白人男人要她，她该觉得光彩才是。」

===Original===
His voice took on at the next remark a tone of pained and outraged surprise.

“Nigger gals gettin’ so nowadays they think they’re’s good as white women! And what ’chu think that old fool Judge Stev’nson said t’ me today? Had the nerve t’ sayt’ me that he don’t blame that nigger Bob for killin’ Jim Archer!”

He demanded of his companions in an almost ludicrous surprise: “What’s goin’t come of the South when *white men* like the judge say such things? Guess he’s gettin’ so old he’s kind of weak in the head! I tol’ him he’d better not say that to nobody else. Somethin’ might happen to *him*!”

“Damn Judge Stevenson!” broke in Stewart, anxious to get a chance to tell his story. “He alw’ys was a sort of ‘nigger-lover’ anyway!”

Henry Lane spoke for the first time.

“Reck’n the Gov’nor’d say anythin’ ’bout this burnin’?” he asked in a tone that anticipated the answer.

===Chinese===
说到下一句时，他声音里带上一种又痛心又震怒的诧异。

「如今的黑鬼丫头也了不得了，居然觉得自己和白人女人一样金贵！你猜那老糊涂史蒂文森法官（Judge Stevenson）今天跟我说什么？他竟敢跟我说，他不怪那个黑鬼鲍勃（Bob）杀了吉姆·阿切尔！」

他几乎是滑稽地一脸惊讶，质问两位同伴：「连法官这样的*白人*都说出这种话，南方还有什么指望？怕是老糊涂了、脑子不中用了！我跟他说，这话最好别再跟旁人讲。不定哪天会出事，落到*他自己*头上！」

「去他妈的史蒂文森法官！」斯图尔特急着想接着讲他的事，插嘴道。「他一向就是个『黑鬼佬』那一号的！」

亨利·莱恩头一次开口。

「你觉着州长会对这次烧人的事说什么不？」他用一种早料定答案的口吻问。

===Original===
Parker laughed ironically.

“What kin he do?” he demanded. He answered his own question. “Nothin’! Under the laws of Georgy, he can’t even sen’ a man down here to investigate unless he’s officially asked by citizens of th’ county! And who’s goin’ t’ ask him?” He laughed again. “If anybody’s fool enough to ask him, they’ll be havin’ a visit paid ’em one of these nights! Reck’n we don’t need to bother none ’bout the Gov’non meddlin’ in our affairs,” he ended assuredly.

“Le’s get back to this Harper nigger ’n’ quit all this foolin’ ’round,” Stewart demanded, irritably. “How’re we goin’ t’ settle him?” He added, after a pause: “Without stirrin’ up the niggers all over the county?”

===Chinese===
帕克冷笑。

「他能做什么？」他反问。他自答自问。「什么也做不了！按佐治亚的法律，除非本县公民正式请他，他连一个人都派不下来查！可谁会去请他？」他又笑。「谁要是蠢到去请他，这几天哪天夜里就有人上门去拜访他了！我看，州长想插手咱们的事，咱们不必担心，」他笃定地收了尾。

「咱们还是回到这个黑鬼哈珀身上，别扯这些淡了，」斯图尔特不耐烦地说。「咱们怎么收拾他？」他顿了一顿，又补一句：「还不能搅得全县的黑鬼都闹起来？」

===Original===
“An’ they ain’t all we got to look out for,” added Sheriff Parker. “They’s some white folks ’round here who’ll kick up a stink if we ain’t careful.” “Who’ll do that?” asked Stewart contemptuously. “Judge Stev’nson can’t do it all by hisse’f.” “Well, there’s him an old Baird an’ Fred Griswold. An’ then the one’s mos’ likely to raise the mos’ fuss is Roy Ewing. He thinks a lot of that nigger lately for some reas’n. Ain’t been able t’ figger it out as yet, but he sets a heap by him.” He scratched his head in an abstracted manner. “Tol me over t’ the sto’ yestiddy that this Harper’s a fine type of nigger t’ have ’round Central City ’n’ that we oughter encourage other niggers to be like him.”

“Another one gettin’ ol and weak-minded befo’ his time!” was Stewart’s comment. “But I want t’ know if we’re goin’ to sit here all night talkin’ ’bout things that’s goin’ t’ keep us from punishin’ this nigger or if we’re goin’ to get down to business. Fust thing we know, we’ll be ’lectin’ this nigger mayer the town!” His sarcasm was thinly veiled, if veiled at all. Parker and Lane showed by the sudden flush on their faces that the shot had reached its mark.

===Chinese===
「而且不光是他们，咱们还得提防着点，」帕克警长补了一句。「这一带还有些白人，咱们一个不小心，他们就要吵翻天。」「谁会那么干？」斯图尔特轻蔑地问。「史蒂文森法官一个人可成不了事。」「呃，有他，还有老贝尔德（Baird）和弗雷德·格里斯沃德（Fred Griswold）。还有一个最有可能闹得最凶的，是罗伊·尤因（Roy Ewing）。不知为什么，他近来挺看得起这个黑鬼。我还没琢磨透是为什么，可他把他看得很重。」他心不在焉地挠了挠头。「昨天在铺子里跟我说，哈珀是中央城（Central City）难得一见的好黑鬼，咱们该鼓励别的黑鬼学他。」

「又一个没到岁数就老糊涂了！」斯图尔特如此评价。「可我想知道，咱们是要在这儿坐一整夜，谈这些个妨碍咱们惩办这黑鬼的事，还是要干正经事。一不留神，咱们就要把这个黑鬼选成本镇的镇长啦！」他那点讽刺几乎毫无遮掩。帕克和莱恩脸上骤然涨红，可见这一箭射中了靶心。

===Original===
“You don’t have to be so cantankerous ’bout it, Ed.” Parker showed in his voice, as well as on his face, that he didn’t particularly care for Stewart’s brand of irony. “You know we’re jus’ as anxious as you to get rid of him. But we got to be careful. You live out in the country ’n’ you don’t know the situation here in town like me ’n’ Henry.”

He sat meditatively for a time. Stewart fidgeted in his chair, and Henry Lane sat lost in thought. Parker suddenly sat up eagerly.

“I got it!” he exclaimed. The others looked at him inquiringly.

“We’ll fix it so’s we can say that Harper insulted a white woman!”

His companions looked slightly disappointed and doubtful.

“How’re you goin’ t’ do that?” asked Lane. “This nigger, as fur’s I can see, since he been back’s been stayin’ out where he b’longs in the nigger section. Only time he comes over this way’s when he comes to the bank or the sto’ or here to th’ courthouse. That’s one thing I can say in his fav’r! Bein’in France ain’t sp’iled him none so fur’s white women’s concerned. If he ran around with them Frog women, he never tried any of it ’round here.”

===Chinese===
「你犯不着这么横，埃德。」帕克声音里、脸上都明摆着：他不大领教斯图尔特这路讽刺。「你清楚，咱们和你一样急着要除掉他。可咱们得小心。你住在乡下，城里这边的局面，你摸得没我和亨利这么透。」

他若有所思地坐了一阵。斯图尔特在椅里坐立不安，亨利·莱恩陷在沉思里。帕克猛地一下挺直了身子，一脸兴奋。

「有了！」他叫道。另外两人探询地望着他。

「咱们设个局，就说哈珀侮辱了一个白人女人！」

两个同伴看起来有点失望，又有点将信将疑。

「你打算怎么设？」莱恩问。「据我所见，这个黑鬼打回来以后，一直老老实实地待在他该待的黑鬼区里。他上这边来的次数，除了去银行、去铺子、或者上这法院，再没有旁的。这一点我得替他说句公道话！在法国待过，倒没把他惯坏到对白人女人动心思的地步。他就是跟那些法国娘们胡混过，在这儿也没见他试过半下。」

===Original===
“It ain’t necessary for him to bother with white women in Central City for us to put that on ’im,” Parker declared defensively. “Nearly all white folks ev’n up No’th b’lieves that ev’ry time a nigger’s lynched down this a way, its ’cause he’s raped a white woman.” His manner became triumphant. “Here’s how we’ll fix it.”

The three men, although they were alone in the dark courthouse and there was none to hear, drew their chairs together. Their heads were close for more than ten minutes, while they talked excitedly together. Occasionally there would be a low burst of laughter—again an oath. At last Stewart rose, took a paper-bound book from the desk, copied for some time from it, and left the courthouse.

===Chinese===
「用不着他在中央城真的对白人女人怎么样，咱们就能把这条扣他头上，」帕克一脸护短地断言。「北方那些白人，几乎个个都信，南边这边每吊死一个黑鬼，都是因为他强奸了白人女人。」他的神情得意起来。「咱们这么着。」

三个人虽说身处昏暗的法院、四下无人能听见，还是把椅子拖拢到了一处。十来分钟里，他们的头凑在一处，兴奋地低声谋划。间或低低地爆出一阵笑——又或是一句咒骂。末了斯图尔特起身，从桌上拿起一本纸面簿子，抄录了好一阵，随后离开了法院。

===Original===
The next morning each of fifteen “white, Protestant, Gentile” citizens of Central City received a letter. There was no writing of any sort on the envelope save their names and addresses. They were of ordinary quality such as can be purchased at five cents a package in any cheap stationery store. In it was a letter typed on plain paper, of a quality to match the cheapness of the envelope. There was no printing of any sort on the letter, nor was it addressed other than: “Dear Sir.” It read:

“Dear Sir:

“You have been chosen, as one known to be loyal, brave, and discreet, to meet a situation affecting the welfare of the Nation, the State, and the Community. You are hereby commanded to be present at the time and place and date given on the enclosed card.

“Be wise! Be discreet! Discuss this with no one! Fail not!

**“The Committee”**

There was a plain card enclosed, also of cheap and easily obtained quality, on which was typed a date, time, and place.…

Mirabile dictu, each of the fifteen recipients of this cryptic missive was a Ku Klux Klansman.…

===Chinese===
第二天早上，中央城十五位「白人、新教徒、纯种」[3]公民，每人都收到了一封信。信封上除了姓名地址之外，没有任何字迹。信封是最普通的那种，随便一家廉价文具店里五分钱一打就能买到。里头是一封打字机打在素净白纸上的信，纸张的廉价与信封相称。信上没有任何印刷字样，抬头也只有一句：「敬启者。」信上写道：

「敬启者：

「阁下已被选定为忠诚、勇敢、慎言之士，以应一桩关乎国家、州、本社区福祉之局势。兹命令阁下于内附卡片所载之时间、地点、日期准时出席。

「务必明智！务必慎言！此事不得与任何人谈论！切勿有误！

**「委员会」**

信里附了一张素净的卡片，同样是那种廉价易得的货色，上面用打字机打着日期、时间和地点。……

说来也奇（Mirabile dictu）[4]，这封鬼头鬼脑的信所送达的十五位收信人，个个都是三K党徒（Ku Klux Klan）[5]。……

---

> 译注：
>
> [1] **nigger（黑鬼）**：白人种族主义贬语。本章为南方白人密谋者（帕克警长、斯图尔特、莱恩）赤裸裸的筹谋之语，nigger 一词贯穿全章是其种族压迫逻辑的底色。作者怀特系 NAACP 领袖、黑人平权活动家，有意以白人三K党徒的原始口吻揭露南方种族恐怖体制的狰狞，故译文不软化其贬义冲击力，保留白人方言的粗鄙口吻。原文为南方白人各方言拼写（nigger / she’ff / ’bout / ev’rythin’ / Gov’nor 等），译文以中文粗鄙口语对等传达，不用怪异拼写制造「口音感」。本注为本书该贬语首现（第1章）译注的延续。
>
> [2] **nigger wench（黑鬼娘们）**：白人种族主义对黑人女子的极度贬称，在 nigger 之外再加以 wench（低贱女仆/淫妇）的双重贬抑。本章出自帕克之口，为吉姆·阿切尔调戏玛米开脱，直白呈现南方白人将黑人女性视作可随意凌辱之猎物的禽兽逻辑，译文保留冲击力。
>
> [3] **「白人、新教徒、纯种」（white, Protestant, Gentile）**：三K党入会资格限定（见第9章术语表），排斥黑人、犹太人、天主教徒及外来移民。本章以三K党召集信的收件人身份为隐语，揭示中央城地方权力网络与三K党的高度重合。Gentile 此处指「非犹太裔白人」。
>
> [4] **Mirabile dictu（说来也奇）**：拉丁语，字面意为「说来令人惊奇」，常带反讽口吻。作者以此拉丁短语反讽这十五位「忠诚、勇敢、慎言」的公民碰巧全是三K党徒——并非巧合，而是南方白人权力结构的写照。译文保留原文以存反讽意味。
>
> [5] **Ku Klux Klansman（三K党徒）**：白人至上主义秘密恐怖组织成员（见第9章术语表）。本章为三K党徒以召集信密谋迫害肯尼思·哈珀的开端，照应全书种族压迫主题。

## XIX / 十九

===Original===
Mrs. Tucker was operated on at Atlanta on Thursday morning at the Auburn Infirmary, owned and conducted by a group of coloured physicians of that city, as none of them could operate in the white hospitals. Kenneth keenly enjoyed being in a hospital again, with all its conveniences. The operation finished and Mrs. Tucker resting easily, he purchased, after much picking and choosing, Jane’s engagement ring﻿—a beautiful, blue-white diamond solitaire.

That important task performed, he telephoned Dr. Scott, to whom Judge Stevenson had given him a letter of introduction. So engrossed had he been in the operation and the purchasing of the ring, he had almost forgotten the promise made to the judge to see and talk with Dr. Scott, known to be a liberal leader of Southern public opinion and one deeply concerned with the problem of race relations.

“That’s a mighty intelligent plan you’ve worked out,” Dr. Scott boomed over the wire. “I’d like to have you talk that over with me and one or two others here. Can you do it before going home?”

Kenneth told him he had to leave early next morning for Central City. As Dr. Scott had a meeting that would keep him engaged all afternoon, it was decided that they should meet that evening at an office in a building downtown in the business section.

===Chinese===
星期四早上，塔克太太在亚特兰大的奥本医院动了手术。这家医院由该城一群有色人医生自家集资开办、自家经营——因为他们没有一个人能在白人的医院里主刀。肯尼思再一次置身医院之中，享用着这里种种便利，心里十分受用。手术做完，塔克太太安安稳稳地歇下了，他便挑挑拣拣了好一番，买下了简的订婚戒指——一枚美丽的、蓝白色的单颗钻石戒指。

这桩要紧事办妥之后，他给斯科特医生打了个电话，史蒂文森法官曾给他写过一封引荐信。他一门心思扑在手术和买戒指上，几乎把答应过法官的话——去见见斯科特医生、和他谈一谈——给忘了。这位医生素有南方舆论界开明领袖之名，对种族关系这一桩问题尤为关切。

「你拿出来的这套计划相当高明，」电话那头斯科特医生的声音洪亮地传来。「我想请你来跟我、还有这儿的另外一两位当面谈一谈。你回家之前能来一趟吗？」

肯尼思告诉他，自己第二天一早就得动身回中央城。斯科特医生那天下午整整一个下午都有会，于是他们定下当晚在商业区一栋写字楼的一间办公室里碰头。

===Original===
It was with a deal of eagerness﻿—and with some degree of anxiety, for he did not know how he would be received by Dr. Scott and the others﻿—that Kenneth set forth that evening for the meeting. He found three men awaiting him in the office of John Anthony, who was one of the three. His footsteps echoed in ghostlike fashion as he walked down the hallway of the deserted building. From the open window there floated from the street below the subdued clatter of automobiles, the cries of newsboys, the restless shuffling of the leisurely crowd as it moved up and down Peachtree Street. Kenneth sought to weigh the three, who were, he felt, representatives of that “new South” of which so much was heard, but signs of whose activities he had so seldom seen. He was seeking to find out their motives, their plans of accomplishing that spirit of fair play toward the Negro, to determine how far they would go towards challenging the established order that was damning the South intellectually, morally, economically. Kenneth, with too-high ideals for his environment, was almost naive in his eager search for the great champion he had dreamed of who would brave danger and contumely and even death itself for a newer and brighter day for his people in the South. That hope had been dulled somewhat by the things he had seen since his return to Central City, for he was not of an unreflective mind. Yet he had not seen far enough beneath the surface of that volcano of passion and hate and greed which is the South to realize that the South had never produced a martyr to any great moral cause one who had possessed sufficient courage to oppose, regardless of consequences, any one of the set, dogmatic beliefs of the South. True it was that there were some who had fought in the Civil War with firm belief that the South was right﻿—even though it had been shown that their idealism was a perverted one. But even then these had moved with the tide of sectional sentiment and not against it.

Educated in Southern schools where the textbooks of history always exalted the leaders of the Confederacy, raising Lee and Jackson and Johnston and Gordon to heights but little lower than the heroes of Grecian mythology, and ever tending to disparage and revile the Union cause and its leaders, Kenneth, like many coloured youths, had accepted the readymade and fallacious estimates set before him. It was, therefore, but natural that he set his hopes for stalwart, unafraid leadership too high and, at the same time, failed to realize that the South had never begotten an Abraham Lincoln, a Garrison, a Sumner, or even a meteor-like John Brown, bursting into brilliance born of indignation against stupidity or ignorance or wrong and dying gloriously for that cause. Kenneth’s eyes had partially been opened by his memorable talk with Judge Stevenson. Etched upon his mind by the acid of bitter truths were the judge’s words that the boasted Anglo-Saxonism of the South had curdled into moral cowardice on all subjects by the repression incident to the race problem. Nevertheless Kenneth was too inexperienced as yet in the ways of life to comprehend the full import of the older man’s cynicism. He yet sought him who would fulfil his ideal of a great leader who, like a latter-day Crusader, would guide white and black together out of the impasse in which the South seemed to be. Kenneth thus anxiously examined the three before him to see if by chance any one of them bore the accolade which would stamp him the Moses that he sought.

===Chinese===
那天傍晚肯尼思动身赴会，心里既怀着几分热切的盼望，也揣着几分忐忑——因为他不知道斯科特医生和其他几位会怎样待他。他在约翰·安东尼的办公室里见到有三个人在等着他，安东尼便是这三人之一。他走过那栋空无一人的大楼的走廊，脚步声像幽灵似的来回回荡。敞开的窗外，从下面街上飘进来压低了的汽车嘈杂声、报童的叫卖声，还有那悠闲的人群在桃树街（Peachtree Street）[1]上来来往往时不安分的窸窣脚步声。肯尼思打量着这三位，他感到他们是那种人们听得耳熟、却难得见到行动迹象的「新南方」的代表。他想要揣摩他们的动机，揣摩他们打算怎样去为黑人争取那份公道，想弄清他们究竟敢走多远，去挑战那正从智识上、道德上、经济上扼杀着南方的既定秩序。肯尼思对他所处的环境怀着太高的理想，急切地寻觅着他梦中那位伟大的斗士——一个肯为南方黑人一个更光明的新时代，敢冒危险、敢受辱骂、甚至敢舍生赴死的人——这寻觅近乎天真。自他回到中央城以来所见的种种，已叫这希望暗淡了几分，因为他不是个不肯反思的人。然而，他还没看透那座由激情、仇恨与贪婪堆成的、便是所谓南方的火山底下究竟埋着什么；他还没意识到：南方从未为任何一桩伟大的道德事业造就过一个殉道者——一个有足够的勇气、不计后果地站出来反对南方任何一条既定、教条信念的人。诚然，内战之中也有些人怀着南方是对的这等坚定的信念而战——纵然后来证明他们的理想主义是扭歪了的。可即便在那时，这些人也只是随地域偏见的潮流而动，并非逆流而上。

肯尼思是在南方的学校里受的教，那里的历史课本一向把邦联的领袖们捧得高高的，把李、杰克逊、约翰斯顿、戈登抬到只比希腊神话里的英雄低那么一点点的高度，又一味地贬损、咒骂北方的事业和北方的领袖。像许许多多有色人青年一样，肯尼思把摆在他面前的这一套现成的、谬误百出的评判照单全收了。因此，他把对坚强、无畏的领袖的指望定得过高，同时又没能看清南方从未孕育出一个亚伯拉罕·林肯、一个加里森（Garrison）、一个萨姆纳（Sumner），甚至连一个像约翰·布朗（John Brown）那样、因对愚蠢、无知与罪恶的愤慨而迸发出夺目光华、又为这一桩事业壮烈赴死的流星式人物都不曾有过——这其实再自然不过。那次与史蒂文森法官令人难忘的长谈，已使肯尼思的眼睛睁开了一半。法官的话——说南方自夸的盎格鲁-撒克逊优越论（Anglo-Saxonism），因种族问题所带来的种种压制，已在一切事情上凝结成了道德上的懦弱——像酸液一样蚀刻在他的心头。然而肯尼思于人情世故毕竟还太生，还无法完全领会这位长者那番愤世之言的全部分量。他仍在寻觅那个能符合他心目中伟大领袖理想的人——那个人会像一个新时代的十字军，引领白人和黑人一同走出南方似乎正陷在其中的这潭死水。于是肯尼思满怀焦灼地审视着面前的这三位，想看看他们当中是不是碰巧有人佩着那枚足以证明他就是他所寻觅的摩西（Moses）的勋章。

===Original===
Naturally enough, his eyes first went to Dr. Scott, as it was of him that Judge Stevenson had spoken most favourably. Minister to one of the larger Atlanta churches, he had spoken frequently and with considerable vigour for Georgia in behalf of greater kindness and fairness toward the Negro. He was very tall. His more than ordinary height with his attenuated and lanky slenderness gave him an almost cadaverous appearance which the loose suit of black mohair he wore accentuated. From beneath the folds of a low collar there sprang a white starched-linen bow tie, the four ends standing stiffly, each in a separate direction, like the arms of a windmill. His rather large head was bald on top but around the edges ran a fringe of yellowish-white hair with curling ends that made his face appear rounder than it was. Bushy eyebrows shaded pale blue eyes that twinkled in unison with the ready smile which revealed large yellow teeth. Into his conversation Dr. Scott injected at frequent intervals ministerial phrases﻿—“the spirit of Jesus”﻿—“being Christians”﻿—“our Lord and Saviour.” He always addressed his white companions as “Brother Anthony” and “Brother Gordon.” Kenneth he always called “Doctor.”

Kenneth felt a certain doubt of Dr. Scott’s sincerity. He tried to penetrate what seemed to be a mask over the minister’s face that effectively hid all that revolved in the mind behind it. Something intangible but nevertheless real blocked his path﻿—an unctuous affability that seemed too oily to be sincere. No, Kenneth reflected, Dr. Scott is not the man. All of this examination had taken but a few seconds, yet Kenneth’s mind was made up. In prejudging him so hastily, Kenneth did an injustice to Dr. Scott that was unconscious but real. In his heart of hearts Dr. Scott had realized that to accomplish anything at all in the South towards enlightenment he must necessarily become, at least as discretion seemed to dictate, a mental chameleon. He had suffered because of that decision, for had circumstances placed him in a more liberal and intelligent environment, he would have been far more advanced in his religious and other beliefs. The traces of gold in the ore that was his mind had been revealed in the suffering which had come to him through his speaking out against a system that seemed to him wrong.

He had been reviled, misunderstood both deliberately and by those who were not so advanced as he. He had borne in silence whatever had come to him, even threats of tarring and of death from the Ku Klux Klan, seeking a course directed by wisdom if not by valour.

===Chinese===
自然而然地，他的目光头一个便落在斯科特医生身上，因为史蒂文森法官说起他时最为赞许。他是亚特兰大一所较大教堂的牧师，常常替佐治亚仗义执言，呼吁对黑人要多一分仁慈、多一分公道，言辞颇见气力。他个子极高。那超出常人的身量，配上又干又细又长的身板，使他看上去几乎有些形容枯槁，他那一身宽松的黑毛呢西服又把这副模样衬得更甚。低领的褶子底下探出一只上了浆的白麻布蝴蝶结领结，四个尖端僵僵地翘着，各朝一方，活像风车的四片翅翼。他那颇大的脑袋头顶已秃，可边上绕着一圈黄白色的头发，发梢打着卷，叫他的脸显得比实际更圆。浓密的眉毛遮着一双淡蓝色的眼睛，那双眼睛与随时挂在脸上的笑一起眨动着，笑时露出一口大大的黄牙。斯科特医生说话，时不时夹进些牧师的口吻——「耶稣的精神」——「作为基督徒」——「我们的主和救主」。他称呼他的白人同伴总是「安东尼弟兄」「戈登弟兄」。对肯尼思，他始终只称「医生」。

肯尼思对斯科特医生的诚意隐隐生出几分疑窦。他想要看透那张仿佛罩在牧师脸上的面具——那面具把他脑中翻腾的一切都遮得严严实实。某种说不清道不明、却又实实在在的东西挡住了他的去路——一种过分殷勤的和气，油腻得让人难以相信是真心。不，肯尼思思忖，斯科特医生不是那个人。这番打量前后不过几秒钟，肯尼思心里却已拿定了主意。他这样仓促地给斯科特医生下了断语，对斯科特医生实在是一种虽出于无心、却确确实实的不公。在斯科特医生的心底深处，他其实早就明白：要在南方为开明做点什么事，他就势必——至少在审慎所指引的范围之内——做一个心智上的变色龙（chameleon）[2]。他为这一抉择吃过苦，因为倘若命运把他放在一个更开明、更明理的环境里，他的宗教信仰以及种种别的信念，本会比现在先进得多。他那块心智之矿里本含的金子，已在他因直言反对一套在他看来不义的制度而招来的苦楚中显露了出来。

他挨过咒骂，被人误解——有的是人家存心的，有的则出于那些不如他开明的人。无论什么落到他头上，他都默默忍下，哪怕是三K党扬言要给他涂柏油、要他的命之类的威胁，他也一味忍着，所寻的不过是一条由审慎——若说不上由勇气——所指点的路。

===Original===
While he was being introduced by Dr. Scott, Kenneth examined critically the other two men. Mr. Anthony, who had volunteered the use of his office for the conference, as no comment would be likely if the four of them were seen in the office building, was first presented.

John Anthony might well have posed as model for a typical American business man or lawyer. Of rotund figure, well-fed appearance, hair close-cut, his face clean-shaven, clad in neatly tailored but undistinguished clothing, he sat leaning slightly forward, his fingers interlocked, his thumbs and forefingers holding his cravat while his elbows rested on the arms of his chair. He acknowledged the introduction to Kenneth with a brief “Pleased t’ meetcha.” He did not rise, nor extend his hand in greeting, but he at once shrewdly appraised and catalogued Kenneth. John Anthony’s interest in interracial affairs had been first aroused by the wartime migration of Negroes to the North. His personal fortunes had been touched directly by this loss of labour, and the resultant decrease in profits had caused him to inquire into the problem of the labourers who had been always so plentiful. Like most Americans, and particularly those in Southern States, he had had no idea of, or interest in, what Negroes were forced to endure. Though near to this problem, he had been a living example of those in the proverb who “live so close to the trees, they cannot see the woods.” His inquiry, conducted with the clear-sightedness and energy he had acquired from long business training, had revealed brutalities and vicious exploitation that had amazed and sickened him. He was too shrewd to believe that Negroes would be restrained from leaving the South by attempts to picture Negroes freezing to death in the North, or to try to beguile them by transparent falsehoods to the effect that the Southern white man is the Negro’s best friend. Though he did not voice it save to his more intimate friends, he felt naught but contempt for the hypocrisy of those who too late were attempting to flatter the Negro to keep him in the South. His motives were therefore curiously mixed in his support of efforts toward interracial goodwill. Economic in part were they, because retention of Negro labour meant the continuation of his own successful business career. Equally, almost, did they proceed from a hitherto latent sense of moral indignation against the treatment which the South had accorded to Negroes in the past. Direct of speech, analytical of mind, he went straight to the heart of the problem with that same perspicacity that had won for him more than usual success in his business of conducting one of the South’s largest department stores.

===Chinese===
趁斯科特医生替他引见的当儿，肯尼思细细打量着另外两位。安东尼先生主动把自家办公室借出来作碰头之用，因为万一四个人在那栋写字楼里被人撞见，也多半引不起什么风言风语——他头一个被介绍过来。

约翰·安东尼大可充当典型美国商人或律师的模特。身材圆胖，一副养尊处优的模样，头发剪得短短的，脸刮得干干净净，一身剪裁合身却并不出挑的衣裳。他微微前倾地坐着，十指交扣，拇指和食指捏着领带，两肘搭在椅子扶手上。与肯尼思见礼，他只短短一句「幸会幸会」。他并不起身，也不伸手相握，却随即精明地把肯尼思上下打量了一番，在心里归了档。约翰·安东尼之关切种族间的事，最初是被大战期间黑人北迁之事勾起来的。他自家的营生直接受了这流失劳工的牵累，由此而来的利润下降，叫他开始去查问那些一向多得是、如今却走了的劳工所处境地的问题。和大多数美国人、尤其是南方各州的美国人一样，他一向既不知道、也不关心黑人被迫忍受着什么。他虽离这问题这样近，却正活生生是那句谚语里所说的人——「离树太近，反倒看不见林子」。他凭着多年经商练出来的那副犀利眼光和那股子劲头去做这番查究，查出的种种暴行和狠毒的盘剥，既叫他惊骇，又叫他不忍。他精明得很，绝不会相信靠渲染黑人在北方冻死就能把黑人留在南方，也不信凭那种「南方白人才是黑人最好的朋友」之类的破绽百出的谎话，就能把黑人哄住。其实他心里对那些人满怀鄙夷——那些人直到为时已晚，才想起要奉承黑人，好把他们留在南方——只是这话除了最亲近的朋友之外，他从不轻易出口。正因如此，他支持种族间亲善的种种努力，其动机颇为错综。一部分是出于经济上的算计，因为留住黑人劳工，就意味着他自己这桩顺顺当当的生意可以继续做下去。而几乎同样强烈的，是一种此前一直蛰伏着的、对南方过去苛待黑人的道德义愤。他说话直截了当，心思缜密，看问题一针见血，正是凭着这同一份精明，他在经营南方数一数二的大百货公司这桩生意上，赢得了非同寻常的成功。

===Original===
Here again did Kenneth figuratively shake his head and decide that John Anthony was not destined to be the Moses of the new South. He could not for the life of him dissociate Anthony’s interest in behalf of justice from his direct financial interest in keeping Negroes in the South, where, with the inevitable working of the law of demand and supply, a surplusage of Negro labour would mean continued high profits for men like Anthony. Kenneth was too young to know that the more largely a man profits from a liberal cause, the more loyal will be his support of that cause and the lesser likelihood of his defection when difficulties arise.

Of the three men, Kenneth felt greatest hope in the third﻿—David Gordon﻿—younger than Kenneth, alert, capable, and with an engaging frankness of face and of manner to which Kenneth warmed instinctively. Gordon was a graduate of Harvard, where he for the first time in his life had learned to know coloured fellow-students as men and human beings instead of as “niggers.” At first he had rebelled strenuously, his every instinct had revolted against dining in the same room, however large, with a “nigger.” So indignant had he been that he had taken it up with the president. Benign, kindly, clearheaded, and patriarchal, the older man calmly and dispassionately and without rancour had shown Gordon the injustice of his position﻿—how unfair it was to deny an education to a man for the sole offence of having been born with a black skin. Before he quite knew how it had happened, Gordon found himself ashamed of what now was seen to be petty nastiness on his part. So interested had he become after his eyes were thus opened that he had made a special study of the Negro problem. After finishing both his college and law courses, he had returned to Atlanta to practise law with his father. His interest in the race question had increased since his return. He was now one of that liberal and intelligent few who are most free from prejudice an emancipated Southerner. Some inner voice told Kenneth instantly that greatest hope of the three lay in David Gordon﻿—and men like him.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
对这个人，肯尼思也在心里摇了摇头，断定约翰·安东尼注定做不了新南方的摩西。他怎么也没法把安东尼替正义张罗的那份热心，跟安东尼要把黑人留在南方的直接经济利益分开来看——在南方，供求之律必然地起着作用，黑人劳力一过剩，就意味着像安东尼这样的人能源源不断地获取高额利润。肯尼思还年轻，还不懂得：一个人从一桩开明的事业中获利越大，便越会忠心拥护这事业，一旦遭遇风浪，倒戈的可能性也就越小。

三人之中，肯尼思把最大的希望寄托在第三个——大卫·戈登——身上。戈登比肯尼思年轻，机敏，能干，脸上和举止间有一种招人亲近的坦率，肯尼思一见便本能地生出好感。戈登是哈佛的毕业生，在那儿，他平生头一回学着把有色人同学当作人、当作同胞来认识，而不是当作「黑鬼（nigger）」。起初他竭力抗拒，他每一根神经都抗议着不肯和一个「黑鬼」在同一个食堂里吃饭，无论那食堂有多大。他愤愤不平，为这事一直告到了校长那里。那位长者慈祥、和蔼、头脑清明、颇具长者风度，不温不火、不带半点怨气地把戈登所处立场的种种不公摆给他看——仅凭一个人呱呱坠地时生就了一副黑皮肤这一桩罪过，便剥夺他受教育的权利，这是何等的不公。戈登还没怎么回过味来，就发现自己竟为如今看来纯属自己这一方小心眼儿的恶毒而羞愧。眼睛这样一睁开之后，他便对黑人问题起了莫大的兴致，着手作了一番专门的研究。修完大学和法学院的课程之后，他回到亚特兰大，跟父亲一道执律务。自回乡之后，他对种族问题的兴趣只增不减。如今他是那为数不多的、开明而明理、又最不受偏见束缚的少数人中的一个——一个获得了解放的南方人。肯尼思心里有个声音顿时告诉他：三人之中最大的希望，就落在大卫·戈登——以及像他那样的人——身上。……

===Original===
The introductions completed, Dr. Scott opened the conversation.

“Doctor, we’ve heard of the society you’ve started in Central City. Tell us how you’re getting along.”

“You have heard of it?” asked Kenneth in surprise. He did not know his fame had preceded him.

“Oh, yes,” answered Dr. Scott. “You see, I know a man in the Klan headquarters here. They’ve got, so I understand, a pretty full account of your movements.”

“They honour me,” laughed Kenneth, a note of irony in his voice. He was not a physical coward﻿—threats bothered him little. He had paid little attention to the report of the Klan meeting at Central City, though it had worried his mother and Bob considerably. No more would he be perturbed by any reports of his activities the Klan might have in their files.

===Chinese===
引见已毕，斯科特医生开了场。

「医生，我们在亚特兰大已经听说你在中央城办起了那个会。跟我们说说，事情进行得怎么样了。」

「你们听说了？」肯尼思惊讶地问。他不知道自己的名声竟赶在自己前头传了过来。

「哦，是的，」斯科特医生答道，「是这样的，我这儿认识一个在三K党本地总部里的人。据我所知，他们手里有一份相当详尽的、关于你行踪的记录。」

「他们真是抬举我，」肯尼思笑道，话音里带着一丝讥讽。他不是个身子骨上的懦夫——恐吓对他起不了多大作用。中央城那场三K党集会的报告他没怎么放在心上，尽管这事把他母亲和鲍勃愁得够呛。同样，三K党档案里再有什么关于他活动的记录，他也断不会为之所动。

===Original===
“Then, too, Judge Stevenson’s been writing me about you,” continued Dr. Scott. “We are all interested in what you’re doing, Doctor, and we want you to talk frankly. You can to us,” he added.

The three men were genuinely interested in the plan on which Kenneth was working. They were too intelligent to fail to see that something would have to be done towards adjustment of race relations in the South to avert an inevitable clash. What that something was they did not know. They felt the time was not ripe for a challenge to the existing order, and they would not, in all probability, have been willing to issue such a fiat had the time been propitious. Yet they were anxious to examine the plans of this coloured man, hoping against hope that therein might lie an easy solution of the problem.

Frankly and clearly Kenneth told of the simple scheme. Occasionally one of his hearers would interrupt him with a question, but for the most part they heard him through in silence. The story ended, the three men sat in silence as each revolved in his mind the possibilities of the plan. John Anthony was the first to speak, and then he approached the whole race problem instead of Kenneth’s plan for attacking one phase of it.

===Chinese===
「还有哇，史蒂文森法官也一直写信跟我谈起你，」斯科特医生接着说。「我们这几位对你做的事都很感兴趣，医生，我们盼你开诚布公地谈。对我们，你尽可以敞开来说，」他添了一句。

这三位对肯尼思正在筹划的那套计划，确实抱着真切的兴趣。他们都够聪明，看得出：为着避免一场不可避免的冲突，南方的种族关系非得作某种调整不可。至于那「某种」调整究竟是什么，他们并不知道。他们觉得挑战现有秩序的时机尚未成熟，而且哪怕时机成熟，他们多半也不愿下这样一道的命令。然而他们又急切地想审视这位有色人提出的计划，心存一线几乎无望的希望——其中或许藏着一条轻易解开这道难题的捷径。

肯尼思坦率而清楚地讲了一遍这套简单的方案。听的人偶尔插进来问一句，但大多数时候都静静地把他的话听完。事情讲完，三人都默然坐着，各自在心里盘算这套计划的种种可能。约翰·安东尼头一个开口，可他一上来谈的却是整个种族问题，而不是肯尼思针对其中某一方面所提的方案。

===Original===
“Doctor,” asked Mr. Anthony, “do you believe there is any solution to the race problem? Just what is the immediate way out, as you see it?”

“It would take a wiser man than I to answer that,” laughed Kenneth. “You see, we’re in the habit of thinking that we can find a simple A-B-C solution for any given problem, and the trouble is there are mighty few that are simple enough for that.”

“Yes﻿—yes﻿—I know all that,” interjected Mr. Anthony, rather testily. “What I want to hear is what you, as an intelligent Negro, think. I want you to tell us exactly what men like you are saying among yourselves.”

“Well, we’re talking about lynching﻿—poor schools﻿—the way Negroes are denied the ballot in the South” began Kenneth.

“Er﻿—that’s a thing we can’t discuss,” hastily interrupted Dr. Scott. “Conditions in the South are too unsettled to talk about giving the Negro the vote as yet.”

===Chinese===
「医生，」安东尼先生问，「你认为种族问题有解吗？在你看来，眼下究竟出路何在？」

「要回答这个问题，可得一个比我聪明的人才行，」肯尼思笑道。「你瞧，我们总习惯以为，随便什么难题都能给它找出个简简单单的、A-B-C 式的答案来，可麻烦就在于，真正简单到那个份上的难题，实在少之又少。」

「是——是——这些我都知道，」安东尼先生有些不耐烦地插嘴。「我想听的，是你作为一个有见识的黑人，是怎么想的。我要你原原本本告诉我们，像你这样的人，彼此之间私下都谈些什么。」

「这个嘛，我们谈私刑——谈糟糕的学校——谈南方的黑人怎么被剥夺了选举权——」肯尼思开了个头。

「呃——这一桩我们谈不得，」斯科特医生连忙打断，「南方眼下的情形太不稳，谈给黑人选举权，为时还太早。」

===Original===
“As yet,” echoed Kenneth. “If we can’t discuss it now, when can we talk about it?”

“It’ll be a long time,” answered Dr. Scott frankly. “There are a lot of white people in the South who know disfranchisement is wrong. We know that we can’t keep the ballot from the Negro always. But,” he ended with a shrug of his shoulders and a thrusting-out of his hands, palms upward, in a gesture of perplexity and despair Kenneth was learning to know so well that he was associating it instinctively with the Southern white man, “we’d stir up more trouble than we could cope with.”

“And while you’re waiting for the opportune time, conditions are getting steadily worse, the problem is getting more complicated, and it’ll be harder to solve the longer you put off trying to solve it,” urged Kenneth. It was with an effort that he kept out of his voice the impatience he felt. “Why don’t men like you three band together with those who think as you do, so you can speak out?” he asked.

“They think about it all the time,” replied Kenneth. “We know the mere casting of a ballot isn’t going to solve all our problems, but we also know we’ll never be able to do much until we do vote.”

===Chinese===
「为时还太早，」肯尼思应道，「要是我们现在都谈不得，那什么时候才能谈？」

「还得有很长一段时间，」斯科特医生坦率地答道。「南方有许许多多白人，都知道剥夺选举权是不对的。我们也知道，我们不可能永远不让黑人投票。可是，」他耸了耸肩，两手掌心向上往外一摊，做出一派既困惑又绝望的姿势——这个姿势肯尼思已经见得太多，简直已本能地把它和南方白人联系在一起了——「我们一开口，惹出的乱子会比我们能应付的多得多。」

「你们一边等着那个合适的时机，情形却一天比一天糟，问题一天比一天复杂，越往后拖，越难下手解决，」肯尼思力劝。他费了好大的劲，才把心头那股按捺不住的不耐烦从话音里压下去。「像你们三位这样的人，为什么不彼此联合起来，把和你们想法相同的人拢到一处，好把话讲明白呢？」他问。

「他们时时刻刻都在想，」肯尼思答道，「我们心里明白，光是投下一张选票，并不能把咱们所有的难题都解决掉；可我们也清楚，在真正能投票之前，我们永远也办不成什么大事。」

===Original===
“That’s just what we are trying to do, but we have to go very cautiously,” answered Dr. Scott. “We must use discretion. How much are Negroes thinking about voting?”

“You must be patient﻿—wait until the time is ripe﻿—” cautioned Dr. Scott.

“Patience can be a vice as well as a virtue.” It was David Gordon who spoke.

Kenneth looked at him gratefully.

“Your race’s greatest asset,” continued Dr. Scott, addressing his remark to Kenneth, yet seeking to impart a gentle rebuke to Gordon, “has been its wonderful gentleness under oppression. You must continue to be sweet-tempered and patient﻿—”

===Chinese===
「这正是我们在努力去做的，可我们行事得格外小心，」斯科特医生答道，「我们非得审慎不可。黑人这边，对投票这件事，心里是怎么想的？」

「你们得忍着点儿——等到时机成熟——」斯科特医生告诫。

「忍耐既可以是美德，也可以是恶习。」说这话的是大卫·戈登。

肯尼思感激地望了他一眼。

「你们这个民族最大的长处，」斯科特医生接着说，这话是对着肯尼思讲的，却想借这话对戈登作一句委婉的申斥，「一向是在受压迫之下那份惊人的温顺。你们得继续和颜悦色、继续忍着——」

===Original===
“That’s all very well to advise, but how would you or any other white man act if you had to suffer the things the Negro has had to suffer?” demanded Kenneth. “Suppose you saw your women made the breeding-ground of every white man who desires them, saw your men lynched and burned at the stake, saw your race robbed and cheated, lied to and lied about, despised, persecuted, oppressed﻿—how would you feel, Dr. Scott, if somebody came to you and said: ‘Be patient’?”

Kenneth poured forth his words like a burning flood of lava﻿—indicative of the raging fires of resentment smouldering beneath. He paused, completely out of breath. Dr. Scott flushed until his face became a dull brick-red in colour. He restrained with an effort the anger caused by the coloured man’s impetuous words.

“I know﻿—I know,” he said soothingly. “It’s hard, I know, but you must remember the words of Jesus to his disciples: ‘When men shall persecute and revile you﻿—’ The spirit of Jesus is growing in the hearts of the South﻿—it will come to your rescue in due season.”

“We’re always hearing about this liberal white opinion,” rejoined Kenneth, nettled by the unctuous suavity of the words, “but we so seldom see any signs of it﻿—almost never in places like Central City. Sometimes I think it’s like trying to put your finger on mercury﻿—when your finger is about to touch it, it rolls away﻿—it’s somewhere else. Meanwhile lynching goes on.”

“You’re right, Doctor,” broke in John Anthony, who had been following the conversation with deep interest though he had taken little part in it. “We’ve got to do something, and that soon﻿—the only problem is how to do it. Now about your society in Smith County﻿—tell us how we can help you make it a success. Do you need any money to get it working properly?”

===Chinese===
「这话劝起来容易，可换作是你、或者随便哪一个白人，不得不忍受黑人不得不忍受的那些，你又会怎样？」肯尼思逼问，「假设你眼睁睁看着你本族的女人沦为每一个起了淫心的白人的繁殖场，看着你本族的男人遭私刑、被绑在柱子上烧死，看着你本族的同胞遭劫掠、受蒙骗、被人造谣中伤、又被人轻贱、迫害、压迫——斯科特医生，这种时候要是有人走上前来跟你说一句：『忍着点儿』——你会作何感想？」

肯尼思这一番话像滚烫的熔岩倾泻而出——正昭示着他心底郁结的那股愤懑之火在熊熊燃烧。他停了下来，气都喘不过来。斯科特医生涨红了脸，直红得成了暗暗的砖红色。他强自按捺住被这有色人一番激愤之言所激起的怒气。

「我明白——我明白，」他抚慰地说，「是难受，我明白，可你总得记着耶稣对门徒说的那句话：『人若因我辱骂你们、逼迫你们——』南方人心底里耶稣之灵正在增长——到了时候，自会来搭救你们。」

「我们老是听说这种自由派的白人舆论，」肯尼思反唇相讥，被那番油嘴滑舌的话刺得心头火起，「可难得看见它半点影子——在中央城这种地方，几乎就见不着。我有时候想，这玩意儿就像你拿手指去按水银——手指快要够着它了，它一滚，就溜到别处去了。可私刑照旧在闹。」

「你说得对，医生，」约翰·安东尼插话——这一席话他一直极有兴致地听着，虽然多半时候没怎么插嘴。「我们是非做点什么不可了，而且得快——问题只在于怎么做。就说你们史密斯县那个会吧——你说说，我们怎么帮你们把它办成？要把这事顺顺当当地办起来，你们要不要钱用？」

===Original===
Kenneth turned to the quiet man who had proposed the first tangible offer to help.

“Thanks a lot for the offer,” replied Kenneth. “There are two things I can think of that’ll be immediately helpful. One is that you and Dr. Scott and Mr. Gordon do what you can to help mould public sentiment so this liberal white opinion will become a force in the South against the Ku Klux Klan and lynching and all the other forms of prejudice. That’s what seems to me to be most needed.”

“Yes﻿—yes﻿—I agree with you, but tell us just exactly how we can help you.” Anthony, in his direct way, was impatient of theorizing. “Do you need any money﻿—credit﻿—legal advice﻿—that is, any we can give quietly without it getting out that we gave it?”

“Yes, there is something,” answered Kenneth. “Most of the men in our societies have been working on shares for so many years that instead of having any money, they owe their landlords large sums. The big problem is credit for the things they need until they sell their crops next fall.”
